--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication of the work, addressed to Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria, and his wife Elizabeth of Lorraine. The text establishes the central theme of the book—Eternity—and frames it as the most worthy subject for the contemplation of rulers.</meta>

# TO MAXIMILIAN
->AND<-
# ELIZABETH
->Most Serene Princes, Counts<-
->Palatine of the Rhine,<-
->Dukes of the Bavarians,<-
->Most Clement L O R D S.<-

<insert>Library stamp: TILBURG UNIVERSITY LIBRARY</insert>

Most Serene Princes, that "Eternity" which you have heard described with your own ears, I now entrust to your most serene hands with a reverent and humble hand. I would not do so, unless I believed that Eternity was so present in your daily thoughts that heaven is in your eyes and Eternity is in your heart; for no other thing can better fill the minds of Princes than the thought of Eternity <note>The author, likely Jeremias Drexel, suggests that a ruler's focus on the afterlife ensures just and pious governance on earth.</note>. Indeed, I could not have undertaken a shorter little work, nor could I have undertaken a longer one. For theologians <term>Theologians: scholars who study the nature of the divine</term>, Eternity is a single "now" and an instant, than which nothing can be shorter or more con- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "tra-", likely continuing as "contracted" or "continual" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page is a formal dedication to Maximilian I of Bavaria and his wife Elizabeth, presenting them with a treatise on Eternity as a subject uniquely suited for the meditation of noble rulers.</summary>
<keywords>Maximilian I, Elizabeth of Lorraine, Bavaria, dedication, Eternity, theology, Princes, Palatine</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Maximilian I and Elizabeth of Lorraine. The author, Jeremias Drexel, transitions from describing the theological "instant" of Eternity to its infinite nature, using the metaphor of the book as a "shadow" seeking the "light" of his patrons' favor.</meta>

# DEDICATION.

...more *contracted*? <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...than which nothing can be shorter or more contracted."</note> But in truth, even this *Now of Eternity* is so long that it is never to be ended by any span of ages; what can be longer than this? Whatever long duration of time, when compared to *Eternity*, is something brief, and very like a fleeting moment. Thus, *Eternity* exceeds every series of years—even the most extended—not only by the longest distances, but by infinite reaches.

And so, the shadow of this *Eternity*, though reduced to the narrow confines of a single little book <note>The OCR reads "si belli" (if of war), but the context clearly indicates "libelli" (little book), referring to the volume itself.</note>, submits itself of its own accord and out of duty at the knees of your Serene Highnesses, and it promises itself perpetual light from these twin most serene stars <note>A common Baroque compliment, comparing the Elector and his wife to celestial bodies that provide guidance and light.</note>.

Permit, Most Serene Princes, this shadow its own momentum; for a shadow follows its sun. This one runs toward your Serene Highnesses all the more boldly because it believes it has surely found two suns truly capable of *Eternity*. I do not touch upon the countless merits and favors of your Most Serene lineage toward our Order <term>Order: The Society of Jesus (Jesuits), the religious organization to which Drexel belonged.</term> at this time for this reason: I ask that they be increased now by this one request—that you [receive] this painted shadow of *Eternity*, Most Serene—

### TO
# THE YOUTH
## DEVOTED TO STUDY
### THE PRINTER.

> *Come now, I pray, O Youth*
> *Thirsting for holy things.*
> *Desiring to drink the waves of salvation*
> *From the eternal water,*
> *Which the sacred flesh of Christ,*
> *Who dwells among the stars, poured forth.*
> *Time, with too rapacious a flight,*
> *Flees and hastens to cut down*
> *Everything along with it*
> *With the blow of its scythe.*
> *Why do you foolishly turn*
> *Your eyes toward these fleeting things?*
> *Direct your thoughts*
> *To the celestial places of heaven:*
> *Places that escape the fleetingness*
> *Of the flying age:*
> *Places that surpass the rapacity*
> *Of devouring Orcus <note>original: "Orci." In classical mythology, Orcus was a god of the underworld; here it serves as a poetic term for death or the grave.</note>.*
> *Where there is nothing to be feared,*
> *Where everything is to be loved.*
> *There, O youth, remaining*
> *Longing for the heavenly good,*
> *You will intoxicate your soul*
> *With rivers of flavorful wine*
> *From the ever-present Bliss <note>original: "Beatitatis." This refers to "Beatitude," the state of supreme, eternal happiness in the presence of God.</note>.*
> *There, treading upon all Time,*
> *You will remain within the abyss <note>In 17th-century spiritual writing, the "abyss" (abyssus) often describes the infinite, bottomless depth of God's eternity or divine love.</note>.*

**Key Vocabulary:**
Youth, Time, Orcus (Death), Christ, Printer, Salvation, Bliss

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the work, establishing the author's intent and the spiritual gravity of the subject matter. It follows the title page and dedication.</meta>

# TO THE KIND

## R E A D E R.

There is not yet, *kind Reader, an end to the writing of books: some follow others, and they grow into the most extensive families of libraries through daily arguments: yet I know of no one who has, until now, taken this specific labor upon himself. Eternity is presented here as if in a small painting <note>original: "in tabella"</note>, not only to be read, but much more to be meditated upon, weighed, and turned over day and night with a most vigilant mind. I warn you beforehand not to get stuck on the words: these outlines of Eternity were drawn with a swift and rough brush, and in several places more had to be given to feeling and piety than to the purity of the Latin language <note>The author is prioritizing spiritual impact over polished literary style</note>. Indeed, it is not fitting to chase after fleeting little words when we are seeking heaven—when we are contemplating the home of Eternity, which we shall soon enter. Therefore, I ask you, my Reader, not to look upon these things merely for the sake of amusement or to relieve boredom. These truly serious matters look toward the soul, salvation, heaven, and immortality. In other things, one may be permitted to jest or to feel disdain; here, no one can be watchful enough! Farewell, and live for Eternity.*

<vocab>Eternity <term>Æternitas: the state of being timeless or the afterlife</term>, Reader, library, Latinity <term>Latinitas: the quality of one's Latin style</term>, immortality</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces his work on Eternity, urging the reader to focus on the spiritual substance and salvation rather than the literary style or the quality of the Latin. He frames the book as a serious tool for meditation on the afterlife rather than mere entertainment.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, salvation, piety vs. rhetoric, meditation, immortality, preface</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page begins the first consideration of the work, following the introductory material. The catchword 'dinem' from the previous page likely completed the word 'beatitudinem' (blessedness) or a similar term in the preceding section.</meta>

# ON ETERNITY
## CONSIDERATION
### THE FIRST.

->*What Eternity is.*<-

When Simonides was asked by Hiero, the King of Sicily—as Cicero relates in the first book of *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original: "De Natura Deorum"</note>—what God might be, he requested one day to consider the matter. When that day had passed, he said it was not yet clear to him, and he asked for two days for the same deliberation. When the two days had passed, he demanded three; finally, he responded only this: the more he thought, the more there remained to be thought upon; he found himself less and less able to explain the matter the longer he wrestled with the idea. For us who are about to contemplate Eternity, the very first principle of inquiry presents itself: what is Eternity? Boethius, in the fifth book of *The Consolation of Philosophy* <note>original: "De Consolatione Philosophiae"</note>, prose 6, says that it is "the complete, simultaneous, and perfect possession of everlasting life." No one should take it ill if we say that this cannot be fully known, and the more it is tracked down, the more remains to be tracked. For how can that be defined which has no limits? If anyone should press the point and desire even a shadowy sketch of it, we believe it is easier to do so by saying what it is not, rather than what it is. Thus Plato said of God in the *Timaeus*: "What God is, I do not know; what He is not, I do know." Likewise, Augustine, the Bishop of Hippo <note>original: "Hipponensis Præsul"</note>, in his 64th sermon on the words of the Lord, says regarding that true blessedness among those in heaven...

<summary>This page introduces the first meditation on the nature of eternity, arguing that it is a concept beyond human definition, much like the nature of God himself. The author cites classical and patristic authorities including Cicero, Boethius, Plato, and St. Augustine to illustrate the difficulty of the subject.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Simonides, Hiero of Sicily, Cicero, Boethius, Plato, St. Augustine, definition of God, everlasting life</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The previous page likely introduced the concept of eternity; this page continues the definition by contrast and philosophical authority.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>What eternity is not</margin>
...depicts its greatness by removing every thought of any evil from it: "We find it easier," he says, "to discover what is not there than what is." In heaven there is no pain, no sadness, no poverty, no lack, no disease, no death, no evil. We can speak of Eternity in the same way, for whatever we see in this life and admit through our external senses is not Eternal. "For the things which are seen," (says Saint Paul in 2 Corinthians 4) "are temporary, but the things which are not seen are Eternal." <note>original: "Quæ enim videntur... temporalia sunt, quæ autem non videntur, Æterna sunt"</note> Thus one may say: this joy of mine, this pleasure and delight; this treasure, that honor; this proud building, this life of mine—they are fleeting, they are perishable, they are not Eternal. Thus, wherever we point our finger, we point to things that will die. Common people often say: "this structure is eternal," or "this monument is immortal." In the same way, a patient man complains that his pains are eternal.

But those "eternities" are far too brief and are easily captured in words; whatever we say of the *true* eternity, we shall say too little. Thus Augustine says in his commentary on Psalm 63: "Whatever you wish, you say about eternity: but you say whatever you wish because whatever you have said, you have said too little." <note>original: "Quidquid vis... dicis de æternitate"</note> But it is necessary that you say something, so that there may be a starting point for you to think about that which cannot be spoken. Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and alchemy</note> says in his *Asclepius*: "The soul is the horizon of eternity and time; for in that it is immortal, it participates in eternity, but in that it is infused into the body by God, it participates in time." Before we proceed further, for the sake of maintaining order, let us see—

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature of eternity, moving from abstract definitions to historical perceptions and symbols used by ancient civilizations.</meta>

# FIRST PART.

What the men of ancient times—the Romans, Greeks, Egyptians, and others—thought concerning Eternity. Certainly, they acknowledged it and depicted it in various ways.

---

->I. *Various views and depictions of eternity*<-
->*among the ancients.*<-

First, they represented eternity by a **Ring** or a **Circle**, which lacks a beginning and an end; it starts in no part of itself and finishes in none, which is the unique characteristic of divine eternity. Thus, since God is eternal and His duration is properly called eternity, a circle signified God to the Egyptians. The Persians once believed they were offering the greatest possible honors to God if, having climbed the highest tower, they addressed that circle of the sky. Furthermore, it is a custom among the Turks (as Pierius <note>Valeriano Bolzani, known as Pierius, a 16th-century Italian humanist and author of a famous work on hieroglyphics.</note> explains more extensively) to cry out from the highest watchtower in the morning: "God always was, and always will be," while simultaneously greeting their Muhammad. The Saracens also used to call God a "circle." That famous Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius Trismegistus." A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretism of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with ancient wisdom.</note>, whom I mentioned, the most renowned of philosophers (who wrote as many books as no other mortal, if we believe Seleucus and Menaecus), said that God is an **intellectual sphere**, whose center is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere, because the majesty and immensity of God are bounded nowhere. For that reason, the ancients built round temples for their gods; thus Numa Pompilius is said to have consecrated a round shrine to Vesta at Rome; likewise Augustus Caesar, in the name of Agrippa, built a temple to all the gods <note>The Pantheon.</note>

<summary>This page explores how ancient cultures—including Egyptians, Persians, and Romans—symbolized eternity through the circle and the ring, emphasizing God's lack of beginning or end. It highlights the philosophical definition of God as an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere.</summary>

<keywords>eternity, circle, symbolism, Hermes Trismegistus, ancient religions, Numa Pompilius, divine immensity</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the initial definitions of the divine; this page continues the exploration of ancient symbols for God and eternity, specifically the circle, the sphere, and the seated posture.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

dedicated in a round circuit, and inscribed it the
->Pantheon.<-
For this reason, Pythagoras, in order to show that God is everlasting, commanded his disciples to adore Him by a turning of the body; and, according to Numa—as witnessed by Brisson <note>Barnabé Brisson (1531–1591), a French jurist and scholar of Roman law and ritual.</note>—it was decreed that those about to adore God should turn themselves in a circle. Therefore, to the ancients, God is a circle, but a circle without a periphery or circumference, and whose center is everywhere; because God is at once the beginning and the end of all things. In *Job, chapter 36*, he most deservedly exclaims:

> *Behold, God is great, surpassing our knowledge: the number of his years is inestimable.* <note>original: "Ecce Deus magnus vincens scientiam nostram: numerus annorum ejus inaestimabilis."</note>

Next, the ancients represented eternity through the sphere and the globe. Thus, for the Empress Faustina, a coin was struck with this form and image: there was a globe upon which the Empress sat, stretching out one hand and holding a scepter in the other, with the inscription:
->*Eternity*<-
From this, many of the ancients judged that the world was eternal because it is round; to whom Saint Basil aptly responds:

> Though the world may be circular, the beginning of the circle is the center.

Thirdly, *Eternity* was signified among the ancients by a chair <note>original: "sella," referring to a ceremonial seat or throne.</note>, by which they designated eternal rest. The Nasamones, a people of Africa, desired for the most part not only to die while sitting, but even to be buried in this position, as if they had already entered eternity and that long cessation from labors. Just as even today, Kings and Emperors are occasionally laid to rest in subterranean mausoleums, sitting in silent and mournful majesty. It was also customary for the Romans to place cast statues of deceased Emperors upon similar chairs

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological discourse on the nature of eternity and the futility of seeking rest in the mortal world. It follows a discussion on the endurance of the soul.</meta>

# FIRST PART

->to support them, as those who already enjoy eternity.<-

There are people who often reason with themselves in this manner: "Look at me, so poorly treated and oppressed by cares and labors until now; why do I not take a breath? Why do I not pause a little? Why do I not cease these troublesome and long labors? I have worked long enough; let others work as much as I have. Therefore, I shall rest now and stop." Thus, they set aside their weary limbs and declare a holiday for themselves—but oh, one that will not last long! They establish a weary rest and embrace quiet, but neither in its proper place nor at its proper time. How truly and holily that golden little book *On the Imitation of Christ* <note>A famous 15th-century devotional work by Thomas à Kempis.</note> (Book 2, Chapter 12) pulls our ear with these words: *Arrange and order all things according to your will and sight, and you will find nothing except that you must always suffer something, either willingly or unwillingly, and so you will always find the cross.* <note>original: "Dispone & ordina omnia secundum tuum velle & videre..."</note> And: *The whole life of Christ was a cross and a martyrdom, and do you seek rest and joy?* <note>original: "Tota vita Christi crux fuit & martyrium..."</note>

Therefore, weariness is to be laid down in heaven, not here; among so many disturbances, quiet will not stand still. Even if everything else were to spare this weary state, death will eventually overturn it. No true rest is to be hoped for, except that which is eternal. If, however, there is any rest in this life, it will be this: to commit oneself and all one's affairs entirely to God and God's will, to trust Him completely, and to consider all other things as vain. Thus instructing us, the author of Ecclesiasticus <note>A book of the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon, also known as Sirach.</note> says in Chapter 12: *Trust in God, and remain in your place.* <note>original: "Confide in Deo, & mane in loco tuo"</note> Outside of this peace of mind, there is nothing but mere disturbances, a mere sea with great waves, and a present hell. But I return to the ancients.

Fourthly, *Eternity* was shadowed forth by the ancients through the Sun and the Moon. The Sun always

# CONSIDERATION

It lives again, even though it seems to die daily and be buried by its own hand; it always rises again, although daily it falls headlong and sets. In the same way, the Moon never fails to grow again and be enriched after her monthly poverty. Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus, a Roman poet of the 1st century BCE</note> wisely warns in his hendecasyllabic verses:

> *Suns are able to set and return,*
> *But for us, when once our brief light sets,*
> *There is one perpetual night to be slept.*
> <note>original: "Soles occidere & redire possunt, / Nobis cum semel occidit brevis lux, / Nox est perpetuò una dorminda."</note>

In the underworld, certainly, that night will be perpetual, but without sleep: no one sleeps there, because they slept here when they ought to have been awake. Therefore, they are awake there after a sleep in sins that was not long—though they themselves, if they could have, would have made it the longest possible, indeed eternal. The logic is far different in heaven. The Roman Church sings concerning the Martyrs of Christ:

> Perpetual light shall shine upon your Saints and an eternity of ages; here is rest, here is pleasure, after labors and watchings that were not long-lasting.
> <note>A liturgical antiphon often used in the Office of the Dead or feasts of Martyrs.</note>

Fifthly, *Eternity* <term>Eternity: the state of being timeless or everlasting</term> is shown by the ancients through the Basilisk <note>A mythical reptile, often called the "king of serpents," said to kill with a single glance or breath</note>. This animal is the most venomous of all, and it alone, says Horus Niliacus <note>Also known as Horapollo, the supposed author of a 5th-century treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note>, cannot be killed by human strength or hand. Indeed, it is so virulent that it kills plants by its breath alone, puts other animals to flight by its hissing, and makes the birds fall silent as soon as it is heard to be near. Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote on the nature of animals</note> reports that in the wilderness of Africa, a beast of burden died, and many serpents gathered at its corpse as if for a banquet; but at the mere hiss of a basilisk, they were put to flight and hid themselves in the sands. Eternity, which will be spent either in joys or in torments, by no...

<summary>This page contrasts the cyclical nature of the sun and moon with the linear finality of human life and eternity, using classical poetry and the symbolism of the basilisk to illustrate the permanence of the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Catullus, Basilisk, afterlife, martyrdom, Roman Church, Horapollo, Aelian, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the terrifying nature of sin or the devil, leading into this description of the "basilisk" of the conscience. Catchword "scum" from the previous page is not present here, but the theme of spiritual danger continues.</meta>

# FIRST PART

it can be cut back, diminished, and much less can it be removed or avoided. Nor is it a wonder if it strikes terror into all who possess sound reason, even when lightly considered; the coils of this **basilisk** <note>A mythical serpent king said to kill with a glance; here used as a metaphor for the deadly nature of sin.</note> are infinite, its twists and circles immense and inextricable. Alas, what a fearsome dragon! Let us turn aside to our own concerns for a moment. It happens sometimes, when someone descends into themselves and searches their conscience to prepare for **confession**, that they find copious nests of serpents and whole armies of vipers. Such a person marvels within themselves: "And I ask you, from where does so much poison come into my breast? From where come so many fat snakes, so many heavy, so many mortal sins? From where comes such an army of little lizards, from where so many foul and lustful thoughts? I myself shudder at such a numerous plague." Do not wonder; we shall easily reveal the cause of the matter. A place that is both damp and neglected is very well-suited for generating serpents. Behold, a double cause: the moisture of the place, and the negligence of those things which ought to be cared for. It is exactly the same in the human soul. If almost all care is spent on the body—if it is treated delicately, nourished elegantly, fattened with feasts, and soothed with pleasures—it must be admitted that the soul, the inhabitant of that body, dwells in a "damp" place. If, in addition, there is carelessness and neglect of divine matters, and hardly any thought is given to salvation—provided the body is safe, provided the body is well, whatever happens to the soul—and if, furthermore, **confession** is rare and mostly perfunctory, what wonder is it that many serpents are born here, and many lethal injuries are found? But, O good Christian, send into your breast this basilisk—

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a basilisk and a nest of vipers to describe the accumulation of sins within a neglected soul. The author argues that pampering the body while neglecting spiritual confession creates a "damp" environment where vices naturally multiply.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, confession, mortal sin, spiritual neglect, basilisk, metaphor of serpents, soul vs body, repentance</keywords>

# CONSIDERATION

with you, admit the thought of Eternity, and you will feel these venomous beasts gradually vanish. You admit yourself that your heart teems with these snakes; it is a sign, therefore, that you have until now rarely or never thought of Eternity. Amend this, and even now begin to turn that thought over in your mind diligently.

->*That which delights is momentary; that which tortures is eternal.*<-
<note>original: "Momentaneum quod delectat, æternum quod cruciat." A common medieval and Renaissance memento mori aphorism emphasizing the brevity of sin versus the permanence of its consequences.</note>

Sixth, Eternity was finally depicted in this manner: A huge, formidable cave was established; a serpent encircled this in its embrace, and in that revolution, it bit its own tail. <note>The Ouroboros, an ancient symbol of infinity and the cyclical nature of time.</note> To the right of the cave stood a youth of pleasant and blooming countenance, carrying a bow and double arrows in his right hand, and a lyre in his left. Opposite this very entrance of the cave sits an old man; fixing his attentive gaze upon a tablet, he writes down whatever either the motion of the celestial globe or the standing youth dictates to be written. To the left of this cavern sits a matron with a practiced face and white hair. At the mouth of the cave are four steps, each higher than the last: the first is iron, the second bronze, the third silver, and the fourth gold. Upon these, little boys wander about, playing and roaming, heedless of falls and dangers. This is the picture; the meaning of the picture is this: The Cave signifies the incomprehensibility of Eternity; the viper folded into a circle signifies Time; the youth signifies God, in whose hand are heaven, earth, and the lower world, and the underworld receives the weapons of the Deity <term>Numen: divine power or will</term>; heaven knows only the lyre and joys. That old man signifies Fate, or rather, what God has spoken from eternity; the woman signifies Nature.

<summary>The author provides a vivid allegorical description of Eternity as a cave surrounded by a serpent, featuring figures representing God, Fate, and Nature to illustrate the relationship between time and the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, allegory, Ouroboros, divine providence, Fate, Nature, symbolism of time</keywords>

# FIRST PART.

The distinct ages and eras are like separate steps. The children running about across these steps represent created things, and especially man, who plays games with the matters of his own salvation and jests at the very entrance of Eternity. Alas, mortals, we have played long enough in the midst of the greatest dangers: we are close to Eternity; we stand at its very doorway as long as we live. It happens by the slightest push of death, and suddenly we are entirely within Eternity. There is no need for death to fight against us with great strength or for a long time: in a moment we are cast down, and from those steps we are tumbled into the Ocean of Eternity. Think on this, you who play upon these steps and think of everything other than Eternity—perhaps today or tomorrow you will be within it.

### *II. The hidden sense of scripture is revealed.*

<margin>* Daniel 3.</margin>
The Holy Scripture, with its divine truth, will fitly interrupt this chapter concerning the picture and image of Eternity. King Nebuchadnezzar of Babylon <note>original: "Nabuchodonosor Rex Babylonius"</note>, when he had ordered the three Hebrew youths who refused his ungodly commands to be thrown into the glowing furnace, the flame poured out above the furnace to a height of forty-nine cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, roughly 18 inches.</note>. This is a thing altogether wonderful, and not without a certain mystery. For who could have measured the fleeting height of a most ravenous flame so accurately? Who climbed up there and applied a measuring rod, and discovered not fifty cubits, but exactly nine above forty? Indeed, we do not usually speak this way, but rather count twenty, thirty, or fifty, even if there are a few

# CONSIDERATION

Whether there be more or fewer whom we count, here a single cubit is said to be missing from the fiftieth number. There is a mystery here and a hidden meaning. The number fifty once signified the Jubilee <note>In the Old Testament, the Jubilee was a year of release from debts and slavery occurring every fifty years.</note>: and although the flames in that hellish Babylon <note>original: "Orciniana illa Babylonia" — a poetic reference to Hell as a place of confusion and exile.</note> rage beyond all measure against both bodies and even souls themselves, and grow immensely beyond all the torments of this life, they will nevertheless never reach so far as to touch the grace of the Jubilee. Among the inhabitants of the lower world, there is no Jubilee, no pardon, and no end to the torments. Now is the time of that Jubilee, not only every hundred or fifty years; it is every day, every hour, every moment. A single part of a brief hour can now obtain a pardon which all Eternity cannot obtain there. Now, one little day can dissolve more debts than entire years or ages could in the times of purification.

<margin>Joshua 3. Ecclesiasticus 18. Bernard of Clairvaux, in the Sermons on the Song of Songs.</margin>
To this, let us add another explanation of the Holy Scripture. When the people of God crossed the Jordan, the lower waters descended into the sea of the wilderness (which is now called the Dead Sea) until they completely failed. And the book of Ecclesiasticus says: *There is one who redeems much for a small price.* Geoffrey <note>Likely referring to Geoffrey of Auxerre, the secretary to St. Bernard.</note> joins these two testimonies of Scripture together and discourses upon them thus: If perennial bitterness is owed to you, and you are able to escape it by receiving a temporary bitterness, you have certainly redeemed great things for a small price. I confess, it is a sea in which you sail, but a dead sea; and how many thanks you owe to God, because you had deserved a sea that was salty, mad, and impassable,

<summary>The author reflects on the "Jubilee" of divine mercy, arguing that while repentance is possible in every moment of life, no such grace or release from debt exists in the eternal torments of the afterlife.</summary>

# FIRST.

And behold, he has mercifully exchanged that for this Dead Sea for you; would that you might be able to pass through the Dead Sea to the land of the living. Here the writer compares all the adversities of this life to the Dead Sea, but eternal punishments to a salty and impassable sea; no one escapes both—one must sail through either this one or that one. What are you doing, O man? (exclaims Saint Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom, c. 347–407 AD, an influential Early Church Father known for his eloquent preaching.</note>) You prepare to ascend to heaven, and yet you ask whether any difficulty might meet you? Whatever we do, this Dead Sea must be crossed. It is within our power whether we wish to arrive at the quiet harbor of happiness. The fountain of wisdom <gloss>Ecclesiasticus 1 <note>original: "Eccl. 1." referring to the Book of Sirach.</note></gloss> is the word of God on high, and its entrance is the eternal commandments. Through this Dead Sea, into the region of the living, no other way lies open than the way of God's commandments. We have a most clear divine oracle: *If you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments.* Through these boundaries, one approaches Eternity <gloss>Cornelius à Lapide <note>A Flemish Jesuit exegete (1567–1637) famous for his commentaries on the Bible.</note></gloss>.

But if it were asked of a Theologian of this age: *What then is eternity?* It is a circle (he will say) returning into itself, whose center is *Always*, and whose circumference is *Nowhere*—that is, *Never to be finished*. *What is Eternity?* It is a sphere round on all sides and like unto itself, in which there is no beginning nor end. *What is Eternity?* A wheel which is always turning, and will turn, rolling on through every age. *What is Eternity?* It is a year continually revolving, which, where it dies and sets, there it is born again and rises once more. *What is Eternity?* A perpetual fountain in which the waters flow back through winding paths <term>meanders <note>original: "mæandros," referring to the winding river Meander, used here to describe the cyclical flow of water.</note></term> always to their own source, so that they may flow again. *What is Eternity?*

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* Chapter 10, On the Divine Names <note>A reference to the work of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century Christian philosopher.</note></margin>

...an everlasting spring, flowing with perpetual waters, either the sweetest waters of blessing or the most bitter waters of a curse. **What is Eternity?** It is a labyrinth twisting itself into countless circles, which leads those who enter round and round forever, driving them about and losing them. **What is Eternity?** It is an abyss of coils and revolutions; it is a spiral always turning and circling into the immense. **What is Eternity?** It is a snake bent back upon itself in a circle, grasping its tail in its mouth <note>The Ouroboros, an ancient symbol of infinity and the cyclical nature of the universe.</note>, which always begins again at its own end, and will never cease to begin. **What is Eternity?** It is a duration always present; it is one perpetual **Today**, which does not pass into the past or the future. **What is Eternity?** It is the age of ages, says Dionysius, which does not perish, but always remains in the same state. **What is Eternity?** It is a beginning without a beginning, without a middle, and without an end. It is a perpetual, interminable beginning that is always starting anew: in which the blessed continually enter upon a happy life and perpetually overflow with new delights; in which the damned are always dying, and after every death and struggle with death, they continually begin to die again and to struggle with death once more. As long as God shall be God, so long shall the blessed be blessed, so long shall they reign and triumph; so long shall the damned burn and cry out in unspeakable ways: *We are tormented in this flame* <note>original: "Cruciamur in hac flamma," a reference to the words of the Rich Man in the parable of Lazarus (Luke 16:24).</note>, to be tormented and tortured **Forever**.

### III. The place of Eternity: why it is called a dwelling.

The Patriarch of Alexandria, John\* <note>John the Almoner (c. 550–619 AD), known for his extraordinary charity to the poor of Alexandria.</note>, a man of the highest piety, used to visit the sick quite often. He was sometimes accompanied by Bishop Troilus, a man who loved coins more than the sick. John whispered into his ear: "I beg you, brother, let us help the friends of Christ." At this, Troilus grew heated, but craftily concealing his spiritual sickness <note>The "sickness" here refers to his greed.</note>, he ordered all the money which his servant was then carrying for other purchases to be given to the needy. Not long after, he began to be gripped by a slight fever, which his greed had brought upon him. The Patriarch of Alexandria heard of this and, having guessed the cause of the illness with ease, brought with him to the feverish man the same amount of silver as had recently been distributed among the sick. After a bit of conversation, John said: "I was only joking recently when I asked you for alms for the sick because my servant lacked them; and look, in good faith I am returning the borrowed <term>Philippei</term> <note>Gold coins originally minted by Philip II of Macedon, often used as a general term for high-value gold currency.</note> and I thank you." Troilus saw the ready money, and soon that "teachable" fever began to subside, the heat was mitigated, and everything felt better; thus he rose for the midday meal with more strength and went to the table. After noon, when the feast was finished, Troilus began to sleep at home and take a sweet midday rest. Here, in a dream, he saw a most magnificent house, and on its facade, above the door—

---

<note>\* Concerning whom see Leontius of Neapolis, Bishop in Cyprus, in *The Life of Saint John the Almoner*, chapter 26, as translated by Anastasius, Librarian of the Holy Roman Church.</note>

<summary>This page recounts an anecdote about Saint John the Almoner and the greedy Bishop Troilus, illustrating how the latter's physical illness was tied to his attachment to money. It begins a visionary sequence regarding a "mansion" in the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>John the Almoner, Bishop Troilus, greed, charity, miraculous healing, mansions of eternity, Alexandria, Leontius of Neapolis</keywords>

# CONSIDERATION

He rejoices in his dream at the title: *The eternal dwelling and rest of Bishop Troilus*, but soon he sees something else and grieves. Someone is present who, having brought workmen with him, commands them in earnest: "Take down," he says, "this inscription from the building, and fix this one in its place: *The eternal dwelling and rest of John, Archbishop of Alexandria, purchased for thirty pounds.*" <note>This refers to Saint John the Almoner, known for his extreme charity; the story suggests that the heavenly mansion Troilus thought he had "bought" through status was reassigned to John because of John's actual works of mercy.</note> Troilus shuddered at this dream, and he did not wish it to be in vain; from being greedy and harsh, he turned out much more liberal, especially toward the needy. That vision of the *Eternal dwelling*, seen even in sleep, persuaded him.

<margin>John 14</margin>
O blessed dwellings, and blessed for this reason: because they are eternal! How Christ desires that we loathe our little booths and huts, so soon to fall into ruin, and that we love those others, and hasten toward those *Eternal Dwellings*. *In my Father's house,* He says, *there are many dwellings.* <note>original: "In domo patris mei... mansiones multæ sunt"</note> No one is barred from here except by themselves; the place excludes no one, being by far the most spacious; time expels no one, for it is a dwelling there, and indeed an *Eternal Dwelling*.

O eternal, merciful God, O eternal truth, O true charity, O dear eternity! Heal our blindness <note>original: "cætati," likely a typo for "caecitati" (blindness)</note> in such a way that from present and brief hardships we may recognize the future, horrible, eternal punishments. Lead us and teach us, so that we may possess perishable goods in such a way that we do not lose eternal ones; let us mourn the offenses we have committed so that we may escape eternal punishment; let us so conduct ourselves in this hospice <note>The author uses "hospitio" to describe earthly life as a temporary inn or guest-house.</note> that we do not fall short of the *Eternal Dwellings*; let us so advance on the way, that we are not driven back from our true fatherland.

# SECOND CONSIDERATION.

### In which things nature represents Eternity.

<margin>To the Romans, chapter 1.</margin>
Even the idolaters <note>The author refers to non-Christian Greeks and Romans</note> acknowledged Eternity, of whatever sort it was, which they described through certain signs; for God revealed it to them, so that they are without excuse. How much more highly recommended should this thought be to Christians, for whom Eternity is depicted in a far different and better way? For this reason, you are without excuse, O man, whoever you are, who—even when warned—consign this most soul-healing memory of Eternity to careless oblivion. Often before your eyes are rings and circles, small spheres and globes, chairs and couches; the sun and moon repeatedly meet your gaze. These things, when seen, bring the memory of Eternity to you. But Nature herself, that good mother, places those things in public view, by the sight and sound of which you are invited to meditate upon Eternity.

<margin>Saint Augustine, City of God, Book 21, Chapter 5.</margin>
Solinus <note>Gaius Julius Solinus, a 3rd-century Roman grammarian and compiler</note> records a stone of Arcadia (it is called *Asbestos* <note>original: "Asbestos"; here referring to a mythical "unquenchable" stone</note>) which, once it has caught the seeds of flame, burns with almost continuous fires. For that reason, lamps were formerly fashioned from it in shrines and tombs. Saint Augustine also mentions this. I add Pliny, Volaterranus, Dioscorides, and many others who report wonders concerning a certain kind of flax <note>The author is likely describing mineral fibers like asbestos which were woven into cloth</note>. It has various names, for it is called *Asbestinum*, *Carystium*, *Indic*, and also "living" flax. This is not only not consumed by fire, but is actually cleaned and scrubbed by it.

<summary>The author argues that even ancient pagans recognized eternity through nature and warns Christians not to ignore the signs of the eternal found in everyday objects and "unquenchable" materials like asbestos.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Nature, Asbestos, Saint Augustine, Pliny the Elder, Roman mythology, Christian morality, symbolism of circles</keywords>

# SECOND SECTION

<margin>* The Clock of Wisdom <note>original: "Horol. sapientiæ," a popular 14th-century mystical text by Henry Suso.</note>. † Isaiah, last chapter. ‡ Revelation, chapter 9.</margin>
Therefore, the corpses of certain Kings, when they were to be cast upon pyres and burned, were wrapped in such a linen cloth <note>The author refers to asbestos cloth, known in antiquity as "live linen."</note> to avoid the mixing of ashes and to distinguish their remains from the rest. From this same linen, Nero possessed a tablecloth which was valued more highly than gems or precious gold. Behold, nature is your leader and teacher, pointing her finger to that which does not find its death in the flames. In this same way, all the damned shall burn and yet not be consumed; they shall be scorched forever and never be destroyed; they shall seek death in these flames and shall not find it. Rightly does Amandus <note>A name used by Henry Suso.</note> exclaim: *O eternal woe, that shall never have an end! O end without end, a death heavier than all death; to be always dying, and never able to fully die.* Thus says the divine Isaiah: † *Their fire shall never be extinguished.* ‡ And that angel of the Apocalypse: *They shall desire to die, and death shall flee from them.*

<margin>* Psalm 8. † Psalm 48.</margin>
That the Salamander can endure alive in fires, at least for some time, was believed not only by Aristotle, Pliny, Galen, Aelian, and Dioscorides, but also by Saint Augustine. This animal is extremely cold and born from the rains; the sun and drought are death to it; hence, in the sense of Pliny, it endures in flames just like ice. From its skin, wicks are made to be fitted into perpetual lamps. The Good God, who produces the Salamander from the earth and mud, also formed man from that same mud, but of a far nobler nature*; he made him a little lower than the angels: † certainly the same [destiny] to him...

### I. Which things among the damned are eternal.

<margin>1.</margin>
The damned person himself is eternal and immortal. No one will be able to kill another, nor can anyone kill himself. They shall seek death and shall not find it <note>original: "quærent mortem & non invenient eam." A reference to Revelation 9:6.</note>. * Indeed, this very desire for dying, which they know will never be fulfilled, will be a massive torment to them.

<margin>2.</margin>
The prison itself is eternal: it cannot collapse, nor be broken open, nor undermined. It is barred by mountains and cliffs. These locks and bolts are too firm, granting exit to no one. If any of the damned should travel from there to us on the day of judgment, it happens by God's permission, but he carries his own hell around with him, not being immune from his torture. Here occurs a significant dispute between two theologians regarding the site and location of bodies in the underworld; one of them, who was also a skilled geometer <term>Geometra: a mathematician specializing in the measurement of space and shapes</term>, argued in roughly this manner: ‡ It is certain from the mouth of Christ that the greatest part of men are to be damned, so that the blessed will be few in comparison to that incredible number of the dam-

<note>original: "S E C U N D A"</note>

...to be [judged]. These are the words of Truth:
> *Enter through the narrow gate, for wide is the gate and spacious is the way that leads to perdition <note>destruction or eternal ruin</note>, and many are those who enter through it. How narrow is the gate and constricted the way that leads to life, and few are those who find it!*

I ask you, is this believed in the world? Are these truly considered to be the words of Christ? But back to the point. The middle and center of the earth is the seat of the lower regions <note>original: "inferorum," referring to Hell</note>; and how could this place—let us truly measure it—be enough to hold so many millions of people, unless we say that there one body is penetrated by another? This is not necessary, says another, but they will be like herrings in a barrel, between whom, tightly compressed, only salt lies in the middle: so too is the fire among the damned. Or they will be like bricks in a lime kiln; one clings to the other, while the force of the flame penetrates them all. Divine Scripture clearly agrees, though with a different metaphor:
> *Like sheep they are placed in hell; death shall feed upon them.* <margin>Psalm 48 <note>Modern Psalm 49:14</note></margin>

They will not be like sheep in the pastures, but like those already slaughtered in a butcher's shop and cast into a single heap. Therefore, the place of the damned is eternal.

3. No less eternal is that Fire, which shall never be extinguished in any age. Christ, in the Gospel of Matthew, warns all too clearly:
> *Depart from me, you cursed, into the eternal fire.* <margin>Matthew, chapter 25</margin>

Do you hear? *Eternal*. The divine wrath kindles this fire, never to be lulled to sleep; Isaiah bears witness: <margin>Isaiah, chapters 30 & 34</margin>
> *The breath of the Lord, like a torrent of sulfur, sets it on fire; it shall not be extinguished night or day; its smoke shall ascend forever.*

<margin>Book 12 of *The City of God*, chapter 23.</margin>
On one hand, says Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian.</note>, there is eternal punishment, and on the other, eternal life. To say, therefore, that eternal life shall be without end, while eternal punishment shall have an end, is utterly absurd. Who then would hesitate to be fully converted?

<margin>Isaiah, chapter 66.</margin>
4. Equally eternal as the rest is the Worm, which is the conscience, most afflicted and desperate because of a past life. <insert>Isaiah predicts:</insert> *Their worm shall not die.* <note>Isaiah 66:24; a traditional metaphor for the gnawing pain of remorse.</note> Long ago, the poets transferred this from the sacred scriptures into their fables; for what else is that Tityus of Virgil’s <note>A giant in Greek/Roman mythology punished in the underworld.</note>, who daily offers his liver to a flying vulture to be gnawed and torn—a liver which always grows back the following night for a fresh slaughter—what is this other than that worm and the conscience, destined to be torn apart by perpetual bitings?

<margin>Office of the Dead, Night 3, Response 1.</margin>
5. To this same eternity of Gehenna <note>A term for Hell or a place of fiery torment.</note> belongs the final sentence and the last decree of all by Christ the Judge—a decree, alas, irrevocable, immutable, and eternal. There is no appeal or challenge from this to another judge; there are none of what the <term>Jurisconsults</term> <note>Legal experts or lawyers.</note> call "replies" or "triples" <note>Technical legal terms for a plaintiff's response to a defendant's plea.</note>; no revision, no delays, and no truces. What has once been pronounced by the mouth of this Judge cannot be revoked through all eternities whatsoever. Our mother, the Roman Church, faithfully warns us and often sings that refrain:

-> *Because I sin daily and do not repent, the fear of death troubles me; for in Hell there is no redemption,* <-

no redemption, none at all; but only eternal despair. Even the blood of Christ—

# SECOND PART

The blood recently poured out on Mount Golgotha <note>The site of the crucifixion of Jesus</note>, though most efficacious for making satisfaction for sins, nevertheless did not reach those who are damned. If the yoke of the Lord—which is the yoke of repentance—does not seem sweet to you in itself, as Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and theologian</note> says, at least do not be ignorant of this: it is most sweet in comparison to that yoke of which it is said: *Depart into the eternal fire.*

6. That which lacks an end among those in the lower world is the "punishment of loss" <term>poena damni: the theological term for the pain caused by the soul's permanent separation from the sight of God</term>, as they call it—the eternal deprivation of the divine vision. This, along with all the other torments and tortures of the lower world, shall have no end, nor can any satisfaction <note>legal or spiritual restitution</note> be made here. And although these torments may last for some thousands of millions of years, yet not even a single day, nor indeed a little hour, nor a moment shall be granted for rest or breathing room. There will indeed be a variety and alternation of torments, but only to produce greater punishment and pain. Christ most clearly forewarned through Matthew more than once <margin>Matthew chapters 7, 22, and 24</margin>: *The sons of the kingdom shall be cast out into the outer darkness; there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth.* There is weeping in the fire, and the gnashing of teeth in the frost and ice. 

By what reasoning, therefore, can a man become so forgetful of both himself and God? How can he degenerate so far into a beast—indeed, harden himself into a stone or a cliff—that when he contemplates those inexplicable and unspeakable tortures that will last for all eternity, he fears nothing, does not shudder, and does not say to himself: "In truth, I am on the road to eternity, and perhaps this path will soon be ended; I am already sitting upon the steps of eternity, and by any slight impulse I may be cast down from here—" <note>The text cuts off mid-word "praecipitium" (precipice) as indicated by the catchword "cipi-"</note>

<summary>This page discusses the "punishment of loss" in the afterlife, emphasizing that the suffering of the damned is eternal and lacks any hope of relief or restitution. If it seems so troublesome and intolerable to sigh and groan through a single night with wakeful and sleepless eyes—even in a soft bed—on account of pains in the head or another limb, or from toothaches or the stone <note>likely kidney or bladder stones, a common source of agony in the early modern period</note>, when the late morning can hardly be waited for, when the sun is believed to be stuck on its path, and when one night seems like a week: and yet, one lies on feathers, there is hope for a gentler day, and comfort and aid are expected from the doctor. 

Ah! What will it be like, then, in the flames, to be awake nights and days for thousands upon thousands and again thousands of years; to hunger, to thirst, to burn, to be tortured to the extreme in every limb; to hope for no refreshment, not even the slightest rest; to despair of everything, and thus to be scorched and tormented through infinite myriads of ages—and not only is there no end to these things, but none can even be hoped for. * There, says Thomas in *The Imitation of Christ* <note>Thomas à Kempis, a 15th-century monk and author of this famous devotional work</note>, one hour in punishment will be more grievous than a hundred years here in the most bitter penance. There, there is no rest, no consolation for the damned. † O Lord, do not rebuke me in your fury, nor local correct me in your anger. ‡ Do not remember the sins of my youth and my ignorances. I am lost, unless you spare me, O God.

## II. *Why Hell is eternal.*

Here a question arises regarding this matter, most worthy to be known by all: how it can be that God—that merciful and good Being, whose mercy is above all His works—nevertheless punishes even a single mortal sin <note>original: "peccatum letale," literally a "deadly sin" that severs the soul's relationship with God</note>...

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological inquiry into the nature of divine justice and the proportionality of eternal punishment for temporal sins.</meta>

# FIRST PART

<note>The author is discussing the severity of God's judgment.</note>
...sin, whether committed in a single moment or through thought alone, He punishes most justly for all eternity; indeed, it is punished in such a way that it can never be sufficiently atoned for. No matter how many thousands of years pass by, it can never be said: "Now this crime has been punished enough; now this guilty man has made satisfaction; now he has washed away that wicked thought by which he offended God." And this is the opinion of the best theologians: that even a "lingering delight" <note>original: "morosam delectationem"</note> is a mortal sin. They call it a <term>lingering delight</term> <note>A technical term in moral theology referring to the act of deliberately dwelling on a sinful thought without necessarily intending to act on it.</note> when someone, revolving an impure and lustful thought in their mind, does not actually decide to perform the deed, but knowingly and willingly dwells upon and takes pleasure in that thought. Therefore, would God decree eternal fires for a single thought? And what kind of equality is this: to establish eternal punishment for a momentary sin that harms no one? To what end does the blessed David <margin>* Psalm 135 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 136.</note></margin> cry out:

> Give thanks to the Lord, for he is good, for his mercy endures forever.

Why does he repeat this twenty-seven times if God is so severe? <margin>‡ Volume 1, Part 2, Question 87, Article 5, Reply 2 <note>A reference to the Summa Theologica of Thomas Aquinas.</note></margin> To these points, Saint Augustine, Saint Gregory, Saint Thomas Aquinas, and others respond: The guilt in any mortal sin is, by its very nature, infinite, because sin is an injury against the infinite majesty of God. To this is added the fact that whoever dies conscious of a lethal sin and does not grieve for what was committed, does as much as if he had sinned eternally, provided that he had been granted to live forever. Such a man did not lose the will to sin, but rather lost his life; he would have sinned forever—

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the nature of eternal punishment and the impossibility of a damned soul ever settling its debt with God.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

...as if he were to live forever; thus he did not cease to sin, but only ceased to live. Furthermore, this must also be considered here: that a damned man, no matter how many of his debts he pays, can never truly provide satisfaction <note>In this context, "satisfaction" refers to the legal and theological act of making full amends for a transgression.</note>. For since he is an enemy and is not in a state of grace with God, his payment is not worthy of being accepted, since the man himself is not accepted. Moreover, such a person does not actually pay any of his debts, since he does nothing actively, but only passively suffers those punishments with which he is unwillingly afflicted. 

The matter will be more evident from an example. Suppose someone owes a neighbor a thousand *philippei* <note>A gold coin named after King Philip of Spain, commonly used as a reference for high value in the 17th century.</note> which he received from him, placing his own house under a perpetual tax, intending to pay a set amount every year. Within twenty years, he pays as many *philippei* to the neighbor as he originally received from him; is he therefore now free from all debt, with nothing left to be paid? Not at all; the entire principal sum of the money remains to be paid just as fully as if nothing had ever been discharged. For such is the law of these agreements: that the principal of the counted money always remains untouched, even if the annual interest is always paid. Just so, whatever punishments the damned may finally endure, they never emerge from their debt. They are eternal debtors, perpetual taxpayers to God.

<margin>Isaiah chapter 1</margin>

Christ is a most just judge, not a tyrant, not a Tiberius <note>Tiberius was a Roman Emperor known in historical tradition for his cruelty and reclusive tyranny.</note>; yet, even if someone who had been condemned were to ask for the ripeness of death <note>i.e., a final end to existence to escape pain</note> after spending a thousand years in the flames, He would still answer him: *I have not yet returned to favor with you.* Let him ask this same thing after another thousand years; he will hear the same: *I have not yet returned to favor with you.* Let him ask this after a hundred thousand years, no other answer will be given. Let him ask this after several million years, Christ the judge will always set the same against him: *I have not yet returned to favor with you*, nor will I return; I wished to return once. I offered my grace to you thousands upon thousands of times, but you rejected it. I wished to be a friend, indeed a father, but you did not wish to be a son. I looked away, I kept silent, I waited for forty, fifty, sixty years, to see if perhaps you would change your mind and your life, but no serious or constant repentance followed; you despised all my counsel, and you neglected my rebukes; you hated and withdrew from all my correction.* Eat, therefore, the fruit of your own way, and be filled with your own counsels. I shall laugh eternally at your destruction, nor will my justice answer you anything else after infinite ages, than that *I have not yet returned to favor with you.* O powers above! O God! ### III. *Other leads toward Eternity from nature.*

<margin>* F. Faber, book 2, On Swabian Affairs</margin>
But I return to the mastery of nature regarding the consideration of eternity.* Hot springs are found among mountains and cliffs with such a great crashing and murmuring of sounding waters that those who gather there to recover their health experience a strange phenomenon. If, upon first entering the baths, they imagine they hear a certain kind of symphony or musical instruments, their ears are so battered by the constant din that the music—which seemed so sweet at the start—turns into loathing and torture through the long-lasting imagination of it. If, however, they imagined they heard a drum or a harsher sounding instrument, they eventually almost go insane because of the most annoying noise constantly disturbing them. Here too we are led to contemplate Eternity: beneath those mountains, in the enclosures of the lower world <note>original: "inferorum claustris," referring to the depths of Hell</note>, that clamor, weeping, crashing, and howling which began at the first entry shall never cease. It will constantly torture the ears—and not just the imagination—nor can any of these things be softened by growing accustomed to them. On the contrary, among the blessed, that heavenly Thrice-Holy hymn <note>original: "Trisagion," the "Holy, Holy, Holy" (Sanctus, Sanctus, Sanctus) sung by angels</note> will not create even the slightest boredom, but will always be heard with fresh pleasure.

I will give to him, he shall not thirst forever, but it shall become in him a fountain springing up into eternal life. <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 4:14, where Jesus speaks to the Samaritan woman at the well.</note> If only we, along with the Samaritan woman, would thirst for these fountains and ask with earnest prayers: O Lord, give me this water, so that I may not thirst; give, O Christ, even a tiny drop of this water, or at least some desire for eternal life.

But Nature has something else to show. In the eighty-first year after the Virgin’s birth <note>original: "post Virginis partum" — a common way of dating years from the birth of Christ (A.D.).</note> (as Suetonius, Dio, and Pliny the Younger record more extensively), on the first of November <note>original: "Calendis Novembribus"</note> at the seventh hour, a horrible eruption of flames occurred at Mount Vesuvius in Campania. This was preceded by an unusual drought and heavy earthquakes; subterranean sounds were also heard, as if thunders were bellowing. Then the sea began to roar and grow great, and the whole sky resounded as if mountains were crashing together in conflict. Then indeed, huge rocks leapt up, mixed with smoke and flames; all the air was infected, and the sun itself was hidden. From this, many were persuaded that the world was to be reduced to chaos or consumed by fire. For such a great abundance of ash was poured out that it filled the land, sea, and air, bringing the greatest damage to men, estates, and cattle; fish and birds were killed, and two cities, Herculaneum and Pompeii, were utterly buried while the people were watching in the theater. And these and other cavernous precipices of this kind, and the ridges of mountains which burn with continuous fires yet are never consumed or burned away, are living examples from God to men—

# CONSIDERATION

It has been granted to all people to know of the eternal fire in the underworld, by which the bodies of the guilty shall be forever burned, yet never consumed. <note>The author refers to the concept of "palingenesia" or the perpetual restoration of the damned so they may suffer eternally without being destroyed.</note> **Tertullian** especially, along with **Minucius** and **Pacianus**, declare this to be true. See, my friend, how even here nature skillfully goes before us and leads us by the hand to contemplate Eternity.

Finally, our very time itself is a sign and, as it were, a footprint of Eternity. Nature desires that we learn the thing signified from the sign, and that we measure that Eternity by our own time. **Augustine** ‡ says: "Between temporal things and eternal things there is this difference: temporal things are loved more before they are possessed, but they become worthless once they have arrived; for nothing satisfies the soul except the true and certain Eternity of incorruptible joy. But the Eternal is loved more ardently when it is attained than when it was merely desired; because there, charity <note>original: "charitas"; in this context, the divine love or grace that binds the soul to God</note> will find more than faith believed or hope desired." Ah! How can the earth—which must soon be abandoned—not seem filthy to us when we look toward heaven, which is to be possessed in eternal dominion?

->*In what things especially the ancient Romans*<-

Pliny the Younger <note>original: "Plinius Secundus"</note> says: *I consider those blessed who either do things worth writing about, or write things worth reading; but most blessed are those to whom both are granted.* * Thus the Romans believed that the eternity of their name and fame could be transmitted to posterity in three ways. First, they wrote many excellent things—yet not all were excellent, nor were they all chaste and holy. Indeed, they committed even their own flaws, shames, and lusts to paper, which is not an honorable or royal road to Eternity. How many books have even died before their authors? According to the mind of Plato, such works were like the gardens of Adonis <note>A classical metaphor for something beautiful but short-lived and shallow, as these "gardens" were merely herbs planted in pots that withered quickly.</note>, dying as quickly as they were born; things that pleased quickly did not please for long. But even if the books of all the Romans were to live forever, none of those authors would be able to transfer any of that life from the book back into themselves.

Next, the Romans did not only write, but they also performed deeds worth writing about—actions worthy of a learned and eloquent pen. These were of various kinds, for they sought eternity in many things, yet our faith teaches us that they found it in none. We do not deny they were great in civil and military affairs, both at home and abroad; they were to be admired in arts and sciences, magnificent and lavish in spectacles and gifts, and astounding in their buildings, mausoleums, tombs, and sta-

to yours. Let these few examples, very briefly touched upon, serve as an illustration.

Augustus held games in his own name and at his own expense twenty-four times, and at the expense of the treasury twenty-three times; none of these cost less than fifty million or one hundred million sesterces <note>original: "H. S." or sestertius, the standard silver coin of ancient Rome</note>. In our currency, this is 2,500,000 gold Philips <note>original: "philippei," a gold coin named after Philip of Macedon, used here as a contemporary reference for the reader</note>, or two million five hundred thousand crowns; a million and a third. And such a vast sum of money was poured out for a single such spectacle: the lowest and most parsimonious expenditure for each of the games produced by Augustus was fifty million sesterces—that is 1,250,000 gold Philips, which is one million two hundred and fifty thousand crowns. Nero smeared the entire theater with gold; he made all the stage equipment and comic instruments out of gold. To this were added the "missiles" or "scatterings" <note>original: "missilia seu sparsiones"</note>, which were wooden tokens to be scattered among the common people, upon which were inscribed entire houses, fields, estates, farms, chattels, slaves, beasts of burden, and often a very great number of silver pieces and gems. Whoever presented one of these tokens immediately received what the inscription designated.

The same Nero ordered ten million sesterces to be paid as a gift <note>term: "donativum" — a large cash gift distributed by Roman emperors to their soldiers to ensure loyalty</note> to a common soldier. This amounts to 250,000 gold Philips, or two hundred and fifty thousand crowns. His mother, Agrippina, ordered this great sum of money to be displayed on a table, so that she might thus silently rebuke and restrain her son's extravagance. Nero noticed he was being warned, and, ordering another sum of the same size to be added, he said: "I did not know that I had given so little."

The daily expenses were set at twenty thousand gold coins <note>original: "philippeorum," likely referring to the gold stater or a similar high-value coin</note>; this amounted to five million and four hundred thousand crowns <note>original: "coronatorum"</note> over the course of those nine months. The Emperor Caligula built a bridge across the sea extending three miles. There were four hundred and twenty-four temples in Rome, most of them magnificent. Domitian spent seven million on the gilding of the Capitol alone.

On the stone tiers of the amphitheater <note>referring to the Colosseum</note>, 87,000 spectators could sit comfortably; in the upper section around the perimeter, 12,000 could stand; in total, 99,000 people.

Besides many other things, there were twelve public baths built by the Emperors, in which people could bathe for free. In the Baths of Antoninus <note>the Baths of Caracalla</note>, there were 1,600 seats made of polished stone, and that many people could comfortably bathe at the same time. In the bath of Etruscus, Pliny says that everything was made of silver: the pipes, the basins, and even the floor itself—but let us move to other matters.

In Rome, there were almost as many statues as there were people; besides the infinite number of bronze, marble, and ivory ones, there were even those of silver and gold. Domitian had a golden statue in the Capitol weighing one hundred pounds; Commodus and Claudius had golden ones, each weighing a thousand pounds. Claudius even had a silver one at the Rostra <note>the speaker's platform in the Forum</note>. For this reason, a specific official was called the "Count of Rome," and he had many soldiers under him to guard such a great "population" of statues.

The Appian Way took a fast traveler five days to traverse from the city to Capua...

As far as this road extended, it was so wide that two oncoming chariots could pass freely; it was so firm that nothing appeared loose or disconnected, just as if it were a single stone. There were many such roads. It is incredible what trustworthy authors write concerning the aqueducts. The Emperor Claudius <note>Reigned 41–54 AD; known for ambitious building projects</note> spent seven and a half million gold pieces on an aqueduct, and six hundred men were maintained just for the protection of the waters. These things were certainly great, though some are to be criticized for excessive luxury. However, there was something that surpassed even those most splendid buildings, though it hardly bears a dignified name. They had in Rome "Cloacae" <term>sewers</term> or subterranean vaults with flowing water to carry away the filth of the entire city. These sewers were so numerous, so large, and so long that they might not undeservedly be counted among the wonders of the world. Regarding these, this sample is sufficient; I know they are not at all incredible to one who has even a passing acquaintance <note>original: "primoribus labris libavit" — literally "sipped with the edge of the lips"</note> with the wealth and power of the ancient Romans, and has heard the witnesses ***Suetonius, Dio Cassius, Pliny, Livy, and others.***

And indeed, these things which I have mentioned are praiseworthy in themselves: the administration of the Republic was as prudent as they were for the most part unconquered in wars; they were distinguished in sciences and arts, and also illustrious in virtue. Thus, when *Cyneas*, the envoy of *Pyrrhus* <note>Pyrrhus of Epirus, a Greek king who fought Rome in the 3rd century BC</note>—an eloquent and intelligent man—had unsuccessfully urged the city toward a treaty not sufficiently worthy of the Romans, he returned and said to his king: ***The city seemed to him a temple, and the Senators seemed like kings.*** In such matters as these, therefore, the Romans are to be praised. <meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on eternity, specifically contrasting the worldly success of Roman emperors with the spiritual requirements for eternal happiness as defined by Saint Augustine.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

In this, they have wandered most seriously and as far as the heavens are apart, because men so prudent placed their entire eternity in things that are not at all eternal. <margin>* Book 5 of *The City of God*, chapter 24.</margin> If the Romans had chosen Augustine as their guide on the path to Eternity, he would have shown them a much more certain way to the very palace of Eternity itself.

> For we do not, he says, call those Emperors happy who have counted many years of empire and life, or triumphs of victory, or treasures of fortune: these gifts are bestowed even upon those who have no right to that eternal kingdom. <note>original: "Neque enim nos... dicimus..." Augustine argues that material success is no proof of divine favor.</note>

And who then, Augustine, are to be judged happy in your mind? Listen, Emperors, Kings, and Princes: in Augustine’s sense, true and eternal happiness will be obtained by you through the observation of these laws:

->Law 1. *Justice.*<-
To rule justly: to hate every pretense and false coloring of injustice. <note>The author uses "pigmenta" (pigments/makeup) to describe the deceptive ways injustice is often disguised as law.</note>

->2. *Modesty in one's senses.*<-
Not to be puffed up by the tongues of so many who honor and salute you, but to remember that you are a man.

->3. *Fear and Love of God.*<-
To make all human power a servant to the Divine Majesty for the sake of spreading the worship of God as widely as possible, through the fear and love of the Divinity.

->4. *Desire for Heaven.*<-
To love more ardently that eternal kingdom in which no rivals for power are feared. <note>In heaven, unlike earthly kingdoms, a ruler does not need to fear being overthrown by "consorts" or peers.</note>

<meta>catchword: 5. Forgiveness (Ignoscere)</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the Roman pursuit of worldly glory and presents Saint Augustine's four spiritual laws for rulers to achieve true eternal happiness. It emphasizes that justice, humility, and the fear of God are the only paths to a kingdom that does not perish.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Augustine, The City of God, leadership, justice, humility, piety, eternal life, Roman Empire, political ethics</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the virtues of a Christian prince, contrasting the ideal ruler with the historical errors of pagan Rome. It draws heavily on the works of Saint Augustine and Saint Leo the Great.</meta>

->### THIRD PART<-

*5. Readiness to Forgive.*
To be inclined and ready to grant pardon, slow to take vengeance, and to seek only that retribution which the necessity of protecting the State demands.

*6. Compassionate Generosity.*
To temper harsher decrees with the leniency of mercy and with a generous abundance of kindnesses.

*7. Self-Restraint.*
To keep luxury all the more strictly corrected, the more freely it might otherwise be enjoyed.

*8. Moderation of the Passions.*
To prefer to rule over one's own depraved emotions rather than over any number of foreign nations.

*9. Devotion to Humility and Prayer.*
To do all these things not out of a desire for empty glory, but out of a longing for eternal happiness; and never to neglect the most noble sacrifice of humility and prayer.

Augustine himself <margin>Book 5 of *The City of God*</margin> posted these laws at the gates of the world, as a brilliant mirror for all Princes. Now you, O Romans, how far have you wandered from the doorway of these laws? I shall remain silent on the fact that you held countless gods in veneration instead of the One. <margin>Saint Leo, Sermon 1 on the Birthday of the Apostles</margin> For Rome seemed to herself to have taken up a great religion because she rejected no falsehood; and while she ruled over almost all nations, she served the errors of all nations.

But even if I remain silent on all these things, how vain and ridiculous it is to wish to leave all one's eternity behind in parchment and papers, in marble and stones, in amphitheaters, pyramids, and sepul-

<meta>catchword: chris (likely referring to "christis" or "christianis" on the following page)</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the final five virtues of an ideal ruler—forgiveness, generosity, restraint, self-mastery, and piety—before critiquing ancient Rome for its idolatry and its futile attempts to achieve immortality through physical monuments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on the transience of earthly power, moving from general reflections on death to specific historical examples of fallen empires and ruins.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

...tombs and mausoleums. And where now is this "eternity" carved in stone? Almost the same thing happened to Rome as happened to Jerusalem. One of the Apostles, pointing out the structure of the Jerusalem temple to Christ the Master, said: "Master, look! What stones and what structures!" <note>original: "Magister, ait, adspice quales lapides & quales structuræ"</note> Christ responded: *Do you see all these great buildings? There shall not be left one stone upon another that shall not be thrown down.* <note>A reference to the Gospel of Mark 13:1-2, predicting the destruction of the Second Temple.</note>

Thus, nothing is eternal in this world. And where now is the old city of Rome? It is sought after, and it can only be said: "Here it once was." Where are those who built it? They have all perished; we do not even know where their ashes lie. And we ourselves shall follow by this same path; after a few years, we will be shadow, dust, nothing. Alas, how small are the affairs of mortals, even the greatest! Alas, how unstable are the affairs of the strongest! For what are all those things now, or where are they? They have vanished. Where are the riches accumulated beyond belief? They have flowed away. Where are the works that rivaled the heavens? They are nowhere to be seen. Thus all things which seem great to us are but a shadow and a dream if they are compared with the Eternal and with Eternity. The foundation upon which the entire fabric of vanishing glory is built is far too weak and made of mud. Eternity is not carved into rocks and marbles in such a way.

Lactantius <note>A prominent Early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325 AD) often called the "Christian Cicero."</note> rightly said: "The works of mortals are themselves mortal." <note>original: "Mortalia sunt opera mortalium"</note> We believe that Babylon, Troy, Carthage, and Rome once existed; yet there is scarcely a part of their "corpse" remaining to provide evidence to our eyes. So it is with the Seven Wonders of the World, so with the Golden Palace of Nero, the Baths of Diocletian, the Baths of Antoninus, the Septizodium of Severus <note>A grand, multi-storied nymphaeum or decorative facade in ancient Rome.</note>, the Colosseum of Julius, and the Amphitheater of Pompey—scarcely a trace of them [remains]...

<summary>The author reflects on the inevitable decay of all human achievements, citing the biblical prophecy of Jerusalem's fall and the physical ruin of great Roman monuments as proof of worldly vanity.</summary>
