<header>THIRD PART.</header>

...and they left their names in books, and for how long?

# I. *How far the Romans strayed from the true path of Eternity,*

In Nazareth, within the secret chamber of the most holy Virgin, the Angel makes mention of a kingdom *of whose kingdom there shall be no end.* <note>original: "cujus regni non erit finis." See Luke 1:33.</note> Such was not the kingdom of Solomon, which lasted only four hundred years until the Babylonian disaster. Such was not the kingdom of the Romans, nor that of the Persians, nor was that of the Greeks such a kingdom. For where are those kingdoms now, once so flourishing? Where are those most ancient monarchies? How great once was Nebuchadnezzar in Chaldea and Syria, and after him Belshazzar? From these, the scepters migrated into Persia and Media to Cyrus and Darius. Nor did they remain here for long; they were transferred into Greece to Alexander of Macedon, the Great King, long a warrior and long fortunate; but when his martial virtue fell, his fortune also failed, and from there the scepters migrated into Italy to Julius Caesar and Octavian Augustus. But where are all these men now? You, my fellow Christian, must seek a kingdom *of whose kingdom there shall be no end.* Numantia, Athens, Carthage, Sparta—their own ends destroyed them; but of the kingdom which is above us, there shall be no end. Its King is eternal, and its inhabitants are eternal. *The Lord shall reign forever, and beyond.* <note>original: "Dominus regnabit in æternum, & ultra." See Exodus 15:18.</note> Upon which words Origen <note>An early Christian scholar and theologian (c. 185–253 AD) known for his commentaries on Scripture.</note> says: Do you think that God shall reign only for an age of ages? He shall reign still, even beyond, and whatever you might call that...

# CONSIDERATION

The prophet will always say this to you regarding the reaches of his kingdom: *And still further*; or *beyond*. And now, says Isidore <note>Isidore Clarius (1495–1555), a Benedictine monk and scholar known for his work on the Vulgate</note>, this eternal kingdom—immense in every part, blessed, and promised to every race of men—is nevertheless met with a deep silence among us. For how many people are there who spend even the smallest fraction of the day weighing this within themselves? Who mentions this, or impresses it upon a wife, upon children, or upon the whole household? We chatter about all other things; of heaven there is scarcely any mention, or certainly it is very rare. In praising our native soil, almost all of us are eloquent; yet we are almost ashamed to praise that most true fatherland of ours. Indeed, many fall into such a disuse of pious conversation that unless they pour out the most barren trifles—and even, repeatedly, lewd filth with puffed-out cheeks—they consider themselves to be regarded as unsophisticated and dull. This truly is to wander "by the whole width of heaven" <note>A Latin idiom meaning to be completely mistaken</note>. We must long for eternal things with heart and mouth, with thoughts and conversations; nor is any true glory to be hoped for, except that which is eternal.

<margin>The Hebrew leaders, in order to pave a way for themselves toward that political eternity against the power of Christ, ordered a full senate to be present; and with the most foolish "prudence," they decreed upon their own heads exactly what they feared most. Regarding these men deliberating in a crowded assembly, Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, referring to his Tractates on the Gospel of John</note> elegantly says: The High Priests and the Pharisees, he says, were taking counsel for themselves, yet they did not say: Let us believe.</margin>

we give. These lost men thought more about how they might do harm in order to destroy, than how they might look out for themselves so as not to perish. And yet they were afraid, and as if taking counsel, they said: \*What are we doing, for this man performs many signs? And who are we? What things are ours? Today we flourish, we are commended, we please; tomorrow the flower will wither. We shall be as displeasing to those whom we have pleased until now, as we are to God, whom we never strove to please. We neglect heaven, yet we do not retain the earth; we do not acquire the favor of God, and we lose that of the world—miserable on both sides in a most lamented bereavement. If death spared the happy in this world, they might perhaps find here a certain glory—a "certain" glory, I say, for there is no true glory except that which is in heaven and is eternal. But death spares no one; it always sees in the darkness, yet is not seen; it waits to invade the unwary from ambush. Where will it lead us, if we have lived badly? To the kingdoms of Proserpina <note>The Roman goddess of the underworld; used here as a classical metaphor for Hell.</note> on the left, and to the judging Aeacus <note>In Greek and Roman mythology, one of the three judges of the dead in the underworld.</note>. There, nobility will free no one, power will cover no one, the applause of men will rescue no one; only the favor of the Lord and that glory which is obtained through the flight from worldly glory will remain. There is no true glory, unless it is eternal.

# THIRD

The value was at most thirty gold pieces; the location was private; the witnesses were few; the person was a woman of ill repute; and yet *it shall be preached throughout the whole world.* <note>original: "praedicabitur in toto mundo." A reference to Matthew 26:13, regarding the woman who anointed Jesus.</note> How many Emperors <term>Imperatores; supreme military commanders</term> have planted their victorious eagles in the camps of the enemy! How many warlike leaders <term>duces</term> have commendably managed the most numerous armies; how many far-sighted governors <term>gubernatores</term> have wisely ruled the widest populations? How many kings have established insignias, statues, fortresses, and cities? How many learned men have distilled their entire brains into new inventions through the pen—just like chemists <term>chymicorum</term>—onto paper, so that they might be placed among those worthy of memory, who nevertheless lie now enveloped in the night of silence and oblivion.

But a single work of the righteous man confers eternal memory, immune from all decay and envy; it is heard and read with reverence and silence by the wise, by leaders, by kings, and by Popes; *it shall be preached throughout the whole world.* Therefore, only to live and to die well is the path to eternal immortality. Go now, my Romans, and seek eternity in statues and marbles, which you shall never find. I would rather hope and prefer what Saint Jerome <note>Eusebius Sophronius Hieronymus, a Church Father known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> hoped for in his account of the life of Paul the Hermit <note>Paul of Thebes, regarded as the first Christian hermit.</note>:

> Remember, he says, Jerome the sinner, to whom if God gave a choice, he would prefer the tunic of Paul, and would rather have it with his merits, than the purple of kings with their kingdoms.

We Christians, lest we suffer the loss <term>jacturam</term> of our money, make a loan: indeed, we send it ahead of us into another world, and heaven receives it. And this is done safely, and it is carried there by the most faithful couriers <term>tabellariis</term>, but by none other than the beggars and the poor...

### II. *A better way to Eternity than the former.*

Darius, that most famous King of the Persians known for his defeat, had ten thousand Persians in his army whom he called "The Immortals." He did so (as ‡ Ludovicus Caelius Rhodiginus <note>A Venetian humanist scholar (1469–1525) known for his encyclopedic works.</note> explains) not because they would plainly never die—for where could such men be found?—but because if any of them diminished the number through death or disease, another was immediately substituted. There were never more nor fewer than ten thousand. In this way, Darius fashioned a certain "immortality" and an eternity of service; but this was quite brief, as Darius and his entire army utterly perished. The satraps <note>Provincial governors in the ancient Persian Empire.</note> once prayed to Darius the Mede: *King Darius, live forever.* Ah! How empty is this wish, and how short is this "Eternity"! We live seventy or eighty years at most, yet we wish in our dreams that we might live forever. Not without cause, certainly, did Xerxes—who (as Herodotus relates) led two armies with him from Asia to subjugate Greece by land and sea, namely two million, three hundred and seventeen thousand, six hundred men, excluding those who served the military—weep as he surveyed them all with his eyes from a mountain.

<summary>The author contrasts the true eternity gained through Christian charity with the false, "short" immortality sought by Persian kings like Darius and Xerxes through their massive armies.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Charity, Darius the Great, Xerxes, The Immortals, Herodotus, Persian Empire, Mammon of Iniquity</keywords>

# SECOND PART

<note>The header "SECUNDA" refers to the second section of the treatise.</note>

The man, overcome by this sight, said he wept because after 50 or 60 years, out of so many hundreds of thousands of men—so robust and select—scarcely one would be left alive.

We can imagine for ourselves some kind of eternity, yet in the meantime, we all die and, like water, we slip away into the earth.

<margin>2 Samuel, chapter 14 <note>original: "2 Reg. cap. 14." In the Vulgate, 2 Samuel is often cited as 2 Kings.</note></margin>

Another and better model of blessed Eternity was devised in Constantinople. In the year 459 after the Virgin gave birth, the Church of Constantinople, in the time of Bishop Gennadius, was increased by a new and most noble monastery dedicated to Saint John the Baptist, belonging to the *Acoemetae* <term>Acoemetae: from the Greek 'akoymetoi', meaning 'the sleepless ones'</term>; these were monks who were accustomed to sing praises to God continually, keeping watch day and night. They did so by this method: all the monks were divided into three classes so that when the first class finished its portion of the divine praises, the second would begin; and when the second finished, the third would start. By this pious institution, the city possessed its own version of heaven. Thus was represented that celestial Eternity, in which God shall be praised in the midst of supreme delights, without any weariness or distaste. Rightly does the divine Psalmist exclaim: *Blessed are they who dwell in your house, O Lord; they will praise you forever and ever.* <note>Psalm 84:4</note> The voice of all the blessed then shall be that which was once Peter's on the rock of Tabor: *It is good for us to be here.* <note>Matthew 17:4; referring to the Transfiguration.</note> For as Saint Bernard says in his sermons on the Song of Songs: "Eternity," he says, "is an abundance far too great." But he also adds this: "Eternity is not found unless it is sought with perseverance." But from you, most blessed Bernard, the way of seeking that—

Hear, therefore, the lesson of the best * Father: by *Poverty, Meekness, and Weeping* <note>original: "Paupertate, Mansuetudine, Fletu"</note> there is renewed in the soul a certain likeness and image of Eternity, which embraces all time. By *Poverty* we merit things to come: blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. Where the poor are despised and abandoned, where the heart is locked in a chest along with the money, where coins are spent according to the rules of greed, there is no affection or love for poverty, and thus none for eternity. By *Meekness*, however, we claim for ourselves the present: blessed are the gentle, for they shall inherit the earth. Here someone might ask: what does that man—in whom there is no gentleness or patience, from whom a kind word is hardly ever heard—what profit does he finally gain from that stubborn impatience of his? What advantage does it bring him to so often be indignant, to rage, to growl, to shout, to make an uproar, to want to ruin everything, and to speak to no one with even a little humanity? Certainly, such a man suffers the loss of either his goods or his reputation; frequently both. For what wealth he has, he does not truly possess, but guards like a watchdog <term>molossus: a large, ancient breed of guard dog</term> whose only job is to bark, attack, and bite. His reputation, if he has any, he does not increase under the title of impatience; and he loses heaven before he even has it. Finally, by *Mourning* <note>original: "Luctu"</note> we also recover past things which we lost through wasteful sinning. But it is necessary that this mourning not be for a single hour or day. ->### PART THREE<-

This is not mourning in earnest, to end one's grief so briefly. We do exactly the same. Ah, all too frequently today we are fortified by the confession of our sins <note>original: "confessione piaculari"</note> and the Holy Communion; we grieve that we have sinned, yet tomorrow we cheerfully sin again. Often we detest our former life with great severity, and yet we call it back; we abjure the crimes we have committed, and on the same day we perpetrate them again. Thus, with the very same tongue by which we proclaim Christ innocent, we also sentence him to the cross. We are the true brothers of Pontius Pilate, who with one and the same mouth pronounced that our Messiah should be granted his life, and that he should be snatched away to the cross. We are utterly unstable men, more constant in nothing than in repeating a vicious habit of life. 

Alas, we carry too much of the moon <note>A reference to the moon's changing phases as a symbol of human inconstancy or "lunacy."</note> in our hearts. At one moment, piety pleases us so seriously that almost no cheerfulness can interrupt such holy duties. With a downcast brow and a frown that rages against any relaxation, we put on a mask of holiness—but alas, it is a holiness that will not last. Soon we begin to hate this very piety, and with the torrent flowing in the opposite direction, we return to the excess and luxury we had abandoned. Thus, we dissolve a friendship made with God as easily as we were reluctant to make it in the first place. While we are doing these things, behold, piety is called back again, and with sad penance <note>original: "pœnitentia"</note> it expels wantonness once more, until we even begin to repent of that very repentance. Thus, rarely with continuous...

# CONSIDERATION

We behold a laborious image of **Uprightness** <note>original: "Probitatis"</note> through our studies; yet with a slight nod, we slide back into our former impurity. From this, the mind subjects itself to all kinds of pleasures and vices; in such great inconstancy of life, we seem to worship *Virtue* openly, while inwardly and in our souls, we revere *Vice* with industrious devotion. This is not the way to eternity, unless it be to that eternity which holds nothing but pure torture and the greatest, perpetual torments.

But let us select from among the Catholic Christians one who is most dedicated to the pleasures of the body; let us lead him to a furnace that is stoked and heaving with flames, and let us question him in this manner: "How much pleasure do you demand in exchange for spending one single day naked in this furnace?" He will undoubtedly answer: "I would not take the whole world, nor all the pleasures of the world as a wage or a gift, to permit myself to be tortured in these flames for one day." 

But let us bargain further: "What reward do you seek to endure this fire for only half a day?" He will say: "Set forth whatever delights or price you wish; I do not buy such torments at that cost." 

But let us finally come to an agreement: "What reward or pleasure do you demand to enter this burning oven and persevere in it for even a single hour?" He will surely answer: "Give whatever even the most greedy or most shameless man could ask for; it is not to be compared with the unspeakable burnings <note>original: "ustulationibus"</note> and pains of one such hour." If he has answered correctly and in accordance with reason, how does it happen, O God,

# FIRST.

It is a tragedy that for the sake of small and cheap profit, for deceptive and fleeting honor, or for filthy and short-lived pleasure, eternal punishment <term>supplicium æternum: the everlasting penalty of the soul</term> is valued so lightly by so many. If the entire world were offered as a reward, we could not be persuaded to spend even a single hour in a physical fire; yet we do not dread the eternal fires of hell, provided that profit invites us, honor smiles upon us, or pleasure entices us. "I hope for better things," you say, "and the infinite goodness of God bids me not to foretell such evil for my future and eternal state." We are accustomed to speaking this way, and while these words are not impious in themselves, our actions cry out against them. It is reckless and far too dangerous to spend a life in vice and yet expect an eternity among the blessed. For damnation <term>damnatio: the state of being excluded from God's presence</term>, even a single sin <term>peccatum: a moral failing or transgression</term> is enough.

Do you not know what Christ threatens? <note>Matthew 5:22</note> *Whoever says to his brother, "You fool," shall be liable to the fire of Gehenna.* <note>original: "gehennæ ignis"; Gehenna was a valley near Jerusalem used as a metaphor for hell</note> Do you not know what Christ forbids? <note>Matthew 5:28</note> *Whoever looks at a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her in his heart.* Do you not know what Christ forewarns? <note>Matthew 7:21</note> *Not everyone who says to me, "Lord, Lord," shall enter into the kingdom of heaven, but he who does the will of my Father who is in heaven.* Do you not know how many Christ excludes? <note>Matthew 10:37-38</note> *He who loves father or mother more than me is not worthy of me; and he who does not take up his cross and follow me is not worthy of me.* Do you not know how openly and seriously he insists upon this? <note>Matthew 20:16</note> *Many are called, but few are chosen.* Few, few! Do you not know how often he calls us to the amendment of our lives—

->THIRD<-

To expose oneself to such great danger, to live in such uncertainty, to hope with words while acting in ways contrary to that hope—this is the supreme evil of all evils, this is the greatest loss, this is truly the maximum damage, this is the most pernicious audacity and rashness, this is extreme foolishness and madness. * Understand these things, you who forget God: lest He seize you, and there be no one to rescue you. <note>original: "Intelligite haec qui obliviscimini Deum: ne quando rapiat, & non sit qui eripiat." A reference to Psalm 50:22 (Vulgate 49:22).</note>

### III. *That this path must be sought with great care.*

Therefore, let there be no person of Christian blood who does not frequently ask himself and others, whom he holds in God's place: What shall I do to attain a blessed Eternity? Is this the true path by which I go toward that Eternity which I seek? I do indeed perform some work, but how very little and how small is it? I aspire indeed to immortal and eternal joys, but are these works of mine—so few, so languid, and so cold—worthy of an eternal reward? I desire indeed to reach the harbor, but I dread the path if it is a little too troublesome; yet that path to heaven is the safest and best which is the roughest and narrowest. ‡ This the Truth of the divine mouth pronounces; Christ cries out and warns: Enter through the narrow gate, because wide is the gate and spacious is the way that leads to perdition, and there are many (alas, there are many) who enter through it; how narrow is the gate, and cramped the way, which leads to life, and there are few (alas, there are few) who find it. <note>original: "Intrate per angustam portam..." A translation of Matthew 7:13-14.</note>

* Nor does He cease to cry out or to warn: *Strive to enter through the narrow gate, for many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able.* <note>original: "Contendite intrare per angustam portam..." from Luke 13:24</note> Oh, how fearful and to be trembled at is that word *Many*, and that word *Few!* Meanwhile, we wretched people, far too reckless in matters of eternal consequence, deceive ourselves; I know not whether we truly hope or merely dream that we shall be numbered among those *Few* in heaven. If only, while these are the days of salvation and the acceptable time, we would frequently cast a more attentive eye toward Eternity, and reason with ourselves in this manner: "What, after all, is all this that I suffer, or see others suffer? Therefore, *all things are only until the time*. Eternity alone admits no laws of time. Whether, therefore, my body suffers or my soul; whether goods or honors are taken away; whether pains or sadness, cares or any other affliction, interior or exterior, should try me—all these things are but a painted image, and momentary compared to eternal punishments. # THIRD PART

He says that while they greatly fear temporal adversity, they expose themselves to the severity of eternal punishment. To this, Saint Paucianus <note>likely referring to Pacian of Barcelona, a 4th-century bishop known for his writings on penance</note> adds: "Remember," he says, "that in the underworld there is no confession <note>original: "exomologesis," a Greek term for public confession and penance</note>, nor can repentance be granted then, the time for repenting having been consumed; hasten while you are on the way." Behold, we fear worldly fires and we tremble at the claws of the executioners. Compare these with the eternal hands of the tormentors, and the peaks of flames that never die through any age.

Saint * Ambrose <note>Ambrose of Milan, a prominent Latin Doctor of the Church</note> beautifully records these sentiments in his address to a fallen Virgin <note>a woman who had broken her religious vows</note> in these words: "Repentance is not to be performed with words, but with action. Moreover, it is performed in this way: place before your eyes from how great a glory you have fallen, and from which book of life your name has been deleted; and believe yourself already placed near those outer darknesses, where there will be weeping of eyes and gnashing of teeth without end. When you have conceived these things in your mind with certain faith—as it truly is—you will see that it is necessary for the transgressing soul to be delivered to the punishments of Tartarus <note>a classical term for the deepest abyss of hell</note> and the fires of Gehenna <note>a biblical term for the place of fiery torment</note>. No other remedy has been established after baptism than the solace of repentance. Be content to undergo however much labor, however much affliction, provided that you are freed from eternal punishments."

The diseases of the body urge the sick man toward the cleansing <term>purgationem: a medical and spiritual term for clearing out impurities</term> of the body; let our diseases also urge us toward the cleansing of the soul. Let the greed for salvation urge us; let the horror of eternal death and eternal torture urge us; let the hope of attaining eternal life and eternal glory urge us—whatever the soul

# CONSIDERATION

Let us embrace whatever purifies us, and flee from whatever defiles us: but above all, the "muddy" body <note>original: "luteum corpus" – a common metaphor in early Christian thought for the physical body as being made of earth or clay</note> defiles us. This is a most faithful admonition from Saint Ambrose.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Jesus Christ, grant that we may possess all these vain and fleeting things in such a way that we do not lose those which are eternal: but let us keep to the path of those about whom Augustine spoke so excellently: "Many," he says, "out of a desire for salvation, come freely under the yoke; and those who were a little while ago sublime and proud, now strive for humble things; they desire to be what they previously despised, and begin to hate what they once were: guests of the present, but seekers of the future, they sigh for that Eternal Fatherland: they prefer abstinence to delights, vigils <note>term: vigilias – periods of staying awake for prayer during the night</note> to sleep, and poverty to riches; they consider the arduous labor against vices to be a pleasure: they love their enemies, and are broken by no injuries, for the sake of the Eternal Reward." Who would not labor to the extreme for love of you, O Eternal Reward!

# FOURTH CONSIDERATION

### *How Saint David meditated upon Eternity, and the method by which he should be imitated in this.*

<margin>Saint Augustine, *The City of God*, Book 21, chapter 23 and following.</margin>
That God will eternally punish the apostate angels and those men condemned at the final judgment has seemed so harsh and incredible to some, that even Origen <note>A prominent early Christian scholar (c. 185–253 AD) whose views on the eventual salvation of all souls were later declared heretical.</note>—a man of such great genius, such excellent doctrine, and so skilled in the Scriptures—dared to teach in his books *On First Principles* <note>original: "peri archon"</note> that the damned and the demons would be restored to grace after they had sufficiently washed away their contracted guilt in the fires. Saint Augustine, above all others, condemns and convicts him of this error. This error found not a few who agreed with it. Throughout Spain, the Anitian heretics spread it using various interpretations. Some thought all the damned would be freed from Hell <note>original: "Orco," referring to the underworld</note>; others thought only Christians; some only Catholics; and others only those who had been more generous in giving alms. Even if Augustine did not argue against their specific errors, the Divine Page <note>The Holy Scriptures</note> refutes them all too clearly.

<margin>Matthew, chapter 25.</margin>
> *Depart from me, you cursed, into eternal fire. And, these shall go into eternal punishment. And, the Just, however, into eternal life.*

<margin>Psalm 76.</margin>
No interpretations <note>original: "glossemata"</note> can be made here; no explanations can be set against these words. Wherefore the divine Psalmist <note>original: "Psaltes"</note>, King David, held two times especially in his heart, observing not so much the past as the future: *The ancient...*

# FOURTH SECTION

<margin>Psalm 77:4</margin>
"My eyes," he says, "anticipated the **watches of the night; I was troubled and I did not speak.**" <note>original: "vigilias oculi mei, turbatus sum & non sum locutus"</note> What was it, most blessed Prophet, that broke your sleep? What are these businesses before the dawn, and from where comes this silence and disturbance of the soul? Hear the cause: <margin>Psalm 77:5</margin> **"I thought upon the days of old, and I held the eternal years in my mind."** <note>original: "Cogitavi dies antiquos, & annos æternos in mente habui"</note> Behold, this is what broke his sleep; he compared the past with the future and the eternal years. Nor did he do this only during the day: <margin>Psalm 77:6</margin> **"And I meditated in the night with my heart, and I exercised myself, and I swept my spirit."** <note>The author uses the metaphor of "sweeping" the spirit to describe a thorough internal examination or cleansing of the conscience.</note> And what is the incentive for this nocturnal exercise? <margin>Psalm 77:7</margin> **"Will God cast us off forever, or will he not continue to be more favorable?"** Behold, how he dreads and shudders at eternity, how he greatly fears the divine judgments, lest God should punish him with perpetual penalties. And what is the end or effect of this meditation? <margin>Psalm 77:10</margin> **"And I said, now I have begun."** <note>original: "Et dixi, nunc cœpi"</note> Thus, in almost a single moment, he was made better than himself; he did not delay, he did not procrastinate, he did not cast his reformation off into worse years. **"Now I have begun,"** now I shall live more holily, not starting after an hour or a day. 

If only I were able, someone might say, to meditate on eternity as the blessed David meditated; perhaps I too would promptly and cheerfully pronounce that phrase, **"now I have begun."** But I am involved in daily cares, hindered by labors, distracted into a thousand parts by my duties; I live among men, I see and hear much evil, so that there is scarcely a place in my mind for a healthy thought concerning eternity. In our social circles, in assemblies, and at banquets <term>symposia: formal drinking parties or social gatherings</term>, our conversations are not of that kind, so that wandering—

# CONSIDERATION

Let us fix our wandering minds, through many distractions, upon the consideration of eternity. In banquets, indeed, business is conducted with trifles and wine cups; serious matters have long since been exiled from such places. Social gatherings and circles hardly admit such somber topics. Novelties are sought from Italy, the Gauls, and Spain; those "news" brought from heaven or from the underworld are considered too old and too often repeated; these things we already know. What need is there to repeat such things so often to the point of boredom? Thus, there is hardly a suitable place found anywhere, nor ever a convenient time, for thinking about eternity. May Truth love you, my fellow Christian, for how truly you have spoken these things! If only you would also amend the fault which you acknowledge. This is all too clear, and believed by our own eyes: that there is almost no care for eternity in the world, even though there is no lack of things which remind us of it, some frequently and some daily.

The ritual book of the Roman Church <note>original: "Rom. Eccleſiæ ritualis liber"</note> advises that in the inauguration of Bishops, these words must be recited to them: *Keep the eternal years in mind.* <note>original: "Annos æternos in mente habe."</note> When the Supreme Pontiff <note>The Pope</note>, having been recently elected, is led to the chapel in solemn rite, there is one who goes before him, and shaking a burning piece of flax <note>tow or hemp fiber used as a wick</note>, groans these words three times: *Holy Father, thus passes the glory of the world.* <note>original: "Pater ſancte, ſic tranſit gloria mundi."</note> With what a healthy and pious custom we ourselves should repeat to ourselves at the beginning and end of all our actions: *Keep the eternal years in mind;* especially when there is an opportunity for sinning, when the suggestions of the devil urge us on, when the danger of wounding the Conscience <term>Conscientia: the internal moral faculty</term> is imminent—oh, then, *Keep the Eternal Years in mind.*

<summary>The author laments the world's distraction by trifles and foreign novelties, urging the reader to adopt the Church's ritual reminders of mortality and eternity to resist sin.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, mindfulness, Roman Church rituals, papal inauguration, memento mori, conscience, vanity of the world</keywords>

# FOURTH SECTION.

<margin>* Isaiah chapter 38.</margin>

### I. Various small reminders for contemplating Eternity.

Philip, King of Macedon <note>Father of Alexander the Great</note>, ordered a noble youth to say to him every morning: *Philip, you are but a man.* He wished never to forget his own mortality, so that he might act more cautiously among mortal men. I believe that there should be no person of Christian and Catholic blood who is not his own daily monitor, saying to himself with deep reflection at least three times: *Eternity, Eternity, Eternity.* Therefore, *set your house in order, for you shall die*, says the Prophet to King Hezekiah, *and you shall not live.* <note>original: "dispone domui tuæ, quia morieris tu... & non vives."</note> There will be an evening whose following morning you shall not see, or there will be a morning whose subsequent evening you shall not have. Therefore, conduct your affairs in such a way that you do not wound your Conscience; entrust yourself to perishable things only so far as to ensure you do not perish through them and lose the things that are eternal.

There is a good custom in Germany that when a light is brought into a room at twilight, this Christian greeting is also added: *May God grant us eternal light.* In churches, before the most holy Sacrament of the Eucharist, a lamp of perpetual light keeps watch, announcing that the *Light of the Gentiles* <note>A title for Christ, original: "Lumen Gentium"</note> and the creator of light is present. What are these but small reminders for contemplating eternity? There is also a certain kind of eternity in prisons, but it is infamous and horrible; to be condemned to the perpetual galleys <term>triremes: ancient-style ships where convicts were sentenced to row for life</term> or to perpetual imprisonment is a heavy punishment, and to many, it seems more sorrowful than death itself.

<summary>The author encourages the reader to practice daily mindfulness of mortality and eternity, citing historical examples like King Philip of Macedon and pious German customs. He warns that while there is a blessed eternity in light, there is also a "horrible eternity" of perpetual punishment.</summary>

<keywords>memento mori, King Philip of Macedon, King Hezekiah, conscience, German customs, Eucharist, perpetual light, eternal punishment, galleys</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on the concept of eternity, transitioning from the perceived "eternity" of earthly suffering to the voluntary "eternal" seclusion of monastic life. Catchword "tioni" connects to the next page's opening word "cogitationi."</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Those weighed down by diseases and other hardships likewise imagine a certain eternity for themselves; hence we quite often hear those cries: "Must this truly be so unceasing? Shall I be fastened to my bed for an eternity? Must these perpetual pains be endured? Shall I always be so vexed and tormented?" These "eternities" are brief, however, and are closed by their own natural end. <note>The author distinguishes between the psychological feeling of "forever" during suffering and the actual theological eternity.</note>

But I return to those prisons—and indeed perpetual ones—which many enter of their own accord and by their own free will, and there they willingly spend their entire lives. And these are the religious men and women for whom it is not difficult, for the sake of God, to bid an eternal farewell to the world and to deliver themselves into sacred workshops <note>original: "ergastulis," literally work-houses or private prisons; here used to describe the disciplined life of a cloister</note>. There is a monastery in Bavaria which takes its name from Saint Alto <note>Altomünster Abbey</note>, and it is divided into two classes of men and women, so that it almost equals that distinct and double monastery of Constantinople mentioned previously, in the continuous chanting of God's praises by day and by night.

Here, therefore, it is the custom that when a virgin receives the sacred veil in the temple and is initiated as a bride to Christ, once all the public rites are finished, the new bride of Christ is led into the monastery through the vestry. Four of the nuns go before her and, in a laudable ceremony, carry a bier <note>original: "feretrum," a funeral litter used here to symbolize the initiate's death to the world</note> in front of her. Once all the sacred virgins have entered, the doors are closed and bolted, exactly as if it were being said to the new bride of Christ: "See, my Virgin, that what you now begin, you perform duly and with constancy. Consider that you now enter upon this path, not to return unless dead. The world, and whatever belongs to the world, must be forsaken by you, and not even in thought..." <note>The text cuts off here, following the catchword "tioni" to the next page.</note>

<summary>The author compares the perceived eternity of physical suffering to the voluntary "eternal" commitment of monastic life, specifically describing the funeral-like initiation rites at Altomünster Abbey in Bavaria.</summary>

# FOURTH PART

Indeed, there is no more returning to those former things; let your Christ be everything to you. And behold, through this door you pass into the house of **Obedience**, and you shall not return through it, except upon this bier which you see, when you make your final journey to the tomb upon the shoulders of your sisters. The way to heaven is always open to you; there you may roam and run freely—but not toward the world, nor to your native land, nor to your paternal home. For we bury you now while you are yet living, and you yourself attend your own funeral rites.

<note>Isaiah chapter 33.</note>
To many onlookers, this appears to be a mournful and gloomy spectacle. "Alas," they say, "to be thus enclosed in an eternal cage, to hide away forever in one single place, to have all return to one's parents blocked off, to live far from pleasures, and to be so buried before death? We shudder at it; let others go first, we shall not follow." But tell me, I pray you, little foster-children of the World, there is something that the prophet Isaiah* asks of you:

> Who among you can dwell with everlasting burnings? <note>original: "Quis ex vobis poterit habitare cum ardoribus sempiternis?" - Isaiah 33:14. The author contrasts the "confinement" of a convent with the eternal fire of hell.</note>

That enclosure seems intolerable to you for a few short years—even though it is for leading a holy life, for obtaining heaven more safely and surely, for meriting a blessed **Eternity** in heaven, and for attaining the eternal sight of God. Yet, those who live religiously and chastely perceive a more sincere joy than those who abound and overflow with daily delights and pleasures. But tell me also: is not a coffin <term>sandapila: a plain bier or coffin used for the poor in antiquity, here symbolizing the death to the world</term> preferred to the damned? For that coffin signifies that they are being sent away to the funeral pyres and the punishment of fire; but after some centuries of years, the dead must be carried out on a bier,

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>\* Job, chapter 3.</margin>
Oh, how desired and mild this sentence would be! But *they wait for death, and it does not come* <note>original: "exspectant mortem, et non venit"</note> \*. For thousands upon thousands, and yet thousands of years more they will wait, but it shall not yet come; nevertheless, they continue to wait, even though they know they wait in vain. Whoever you are that loves heaven and yourself, keep the *Eternal Years* <note>A common meditative theme regarding the vastness of time in the afterlife.</note> early in your mind.

<margin>‡ Rufinus, book 2, chapter 17.</margin>
Regarding a monastery not unlike this one—if one considers the entrance—Rufinus of Aquileia ‡ <note>A 4th-century monk and historian known for translating Greek church texts into Latin.</note> relates: In the region of Thebaid <note>An area in ancient Egypt famous for its early Christian hermits and monks.</note>, he says, the monastery of Isidore is as large as it is famous, enclosed by very wide walls and generously supplied with gardens, wells, and everything necessary for the use of life, so that no one is given any handle or reason for thinking of an excursion. For this is the unchangeable law of the community <term>cenobium: a communal religious house</term>: that once a person has set foot inside, they shall never carry it out again. The only exit open was to heaven or to the grave. And it may seem a wonder, says Rufinus, that those who entered under this law are held back not by the necessity of the law, but by the happiness of the life itself. Their entire work is to pray, to praise God, and to devote their efforts to religious studies. They perform all these things with such industry and holiness of life that many among them are wonder-workers <term>thaumaturgi: individuals capable of performing miracles</term> and those who excel in the grace of performing miracles. Indeed, they live with such temperance and singular excellence of life that there is no disease among them except the last—that is, death itself, which everywhere tears away the bars and bolts of all enclosures, and does not allow itself to be shut out by any doors, however much they are reinforced with iron. And did not even this monastery bear some shadow of Eternity? But let us move forward.

# FOURTH [PART]

## II. *Eternity leaps beyond all the numbers and laws of Arithmetic.*

A common and very well-known mathematical problem is usually proposed regarding Eternity—one that children are taught in schools—and it is this: imagine, they say, a mountain made of the tiniest grains of sand, as large as the entire world, or even greater in mass and magnitude. Suppose that every single year, only one tiny grain is removed by an angel. How many thousands of years, and thousands upon thousands, and thousands again; how many hundreds of thousands; indeed, how many millions of millions will pass away before the mountain appears to sink and decrease? Let the master of Arithmetic sit down now, move his pebble-counters, and gather the sum of how many years will pass while half the mountain, and then the whole, is carried away so slowly by the angel. Indeed, we can hardly grasp this in any other way than as if there would be no end at all. 

But our imagination errs most gravely, and it wanders shamefully in this: that it cannot sufficiently attain the truth. In reality, the very last grain of that great mountain would eventually be counted. But Eternity exceeds this by an incomparable length (nothing is more certain), because there is no comparison, no proportion, between the finite and the infinite. Eternity admits no limits, no boundaries. Therefore, the damned <note>original: "damnati"</note> will burn in unceasing flames for such a long and incomprehensible space of years, during which a mountain of such magnitude could be moved elsewhere in the manner described. Yet, so far is it from there being a limit or end to their torments at that time, that it could then be said: now it only begins.

<summary>This page uses the famous "mountain of sand" analogy to illustrate that eternity is not merely a long duration of time, but an infinite state that defies mathematical calculation.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Arithmetic, infinity, divine judgment, allegory of the sand, damnation, time vs. eternity</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological meditation on the nature of time and the afterlife, specifically focusing on the concept of eternity as an immeasurable span.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

*Eternity*; nothing has been taken away from Eternity; it is still whole. After a thousand years, after a hundred thousand years, it is not yet the end, nor the middle, nor even the beginning of *Eternity*. Rather, its only measure is *Always*.

A theologian of this present age explains this same logic of arithmetic regarding the matter of Eternity, in exactly the same sense but with slightly different words. I shall measure these out to you, Reader, in their entirety and in good faith, for these things can never be sufficiently spoken of, nor commended, nor impressed upon the mind.

> \* See, he says, how long is *Eternity*? How long shall God and the Saints reign? How long shall the damned burn in Gehenna <note>original: "gehenna"; a term for hell or a place of fiery torment</note>? For *Eternity*. How much is Eternal? Think of a hundred thousand years; you have thought of nothing in respect to *Eternity*. Think of ten hundred thousand years, or rather, of ages; you have still plucked nothing away from *Eternity*. Think of a thousand million years; *Eternity* remains just as whole as before. Think of a thousand cubes of a thousand million years—that is 1,000,000,000,000,000,000,000,000,000—which are a thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand, thousand thousand years. Think, I say, of spending so many years in the flames, and you have not yet even begun *Eternity*. Think of as many millions of cubes as there are drops in the sea; you have not yet reached the beginning of *Eternity*. *Eternity* remains equally *Eternal*, whether in joys for the Saints or in torments for the damned. O Jesus spare us; O Jesus, Jesus save us!

<insert>
\* *Cornelius à Lapide <note>A prominent Jesuit exegete (1567–1637) known for his biblical commentaries.</note> on chapter 15 of Exodus, verse 18.*
</insert>

<summary>This page explores the mathematical impossibility of measuring eternity, using vast numerical scales to emphasize that even trillions of years do not diminish its duration. It concludes with a sobering reflection on the eternal nature of both heavenly joy and hellish punishment, citing the theologian Cornelius à Lapide.</summary>

<keywords>eternity, arithmetic of time, salvation, damnation, Gehenna, Cornelius à Lapide, Book of Exodus, divine judgment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the meditation on the nature of Hell, specifically focusing on the concept of eternity as a weight that crushes the damned.</meta>

# FOURTH <note>original: QUARTA; likely referring to the fourth meditation or section of the work</note>

mercy; have mercy on me, O good Jesus, lest I be cast headlong into this Eternity of the damned.

For if God were to say to the damned: "Let the earth be filled with the finest sand, so that the whole world is filled with these grains of sand from the earth even unto the highest heaven <note>original: coelum empyreum; in medieval cosmology, the highest part of heaven, the abode of God</note>, and every thousandth year an angel shall come and take away one single grain from this heap of sand; and when, after as many thousands of years as there are grains, he has exhausted them all, I will free you from Gehenna <note>a term for Hell derived from the Valley of Hinnom</note>." Oh, how the damned would exult! They would not consider themselves damned at all. But now, after all these thousands of years, there remain others, and yet more thousands, unto infinity, unto Eternity, and beyond. This is the heavy weight of Eternity which oppresses the damned: reflect, O sinner, reflect that this weight hangs over you, unless you repent.

William Peraldus <margin>Volume I, *Summary of Virtues*, Part 7, regarding the 8 Beatitudes</margin>, Bishop of Lyon, a man most religious and learned, presents this same innumerable count of years among the damned for us to meditate upon in another way: "If the damned," he says, "were to shed but one tiny tear every single day, to be preserved with all the drops yet to be shed, they would eventually pour out more tears than the sea has waters; for all the droplets of the sea can be reduced to a number and a measure, since it is not difficult for God to say: 'There are this many drops in the sea, and no more.' But truly, the tears of the damned reject all number and measure." Alas, how we fail to reflect on these things, and how freely we sin!

<summary>This page uses vivid mathematical metaphors—the grains of sand in the universe and the droplets of the sea—to illustrate the terrifying and infinite nature of eternal punishment in Hell.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Hell, Gehenna, William Peraldus, repentance, divine judgment, infinity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the meditation on the nature of eternity, specifically focusing on the disproportion between fleeting earthly pleasure and everlasting punishment. ->CONSIDERATION<-

and we make ourselves guilty before all of Eternity, usually for the sake of a brief and filthy little pleasure.

But let us also settle the account in this way, while we weigh the years of the damned: imagine a sheet made of parchment, a palm's breadth wide, but so long that it circles and embraces the entire globe of the earth. Let this sheet be covered in nines <note>original: "novenariis" - the number nine was often used in such metaphors as the highest single digit to represent the largest possible quantity</note> written continuously and pressed closely together along its entire length. Who is the mathematician <term>Arithmeticus</term> who could express this number? What mountain contains so many grains of sand? What ocean possesses so many drops? And yet, even this is not Eternity; it stretches further still, nor does it allow itself to be circumscribed in such a way. It is longer; it is more extensive. How far, then? *Into Infinity*.

If your heart, my fellow Christian, has not yet hardened into stone and rock, it is impossible for you to think on these things and not shudder at that immense abyss <term>barathrum</term> of Eternity. If there is any feeling in you, it will be found here; but, as we have demonstrated above, too few people think on these things. Consequently, they live so careless of their salvation as if there were no heaven, no God, no hell, and no eternity. They let no day pass that they do not burden with sins, as if they were working for this alone: that on the final day of their life, they might find the sum of their sins to be as great as possible. Thus, they approach eternity playfully, no differently than if it were a matter of a prison sentence of only a few weeks. Such people, says Gregory <note>Saint Gregory the Great, a Doctor of the Church known for his writings on morality and the afterlife</note>, carry out the causes for tears while dancing, and execute the business of their own death with a laugh. Oh, truly, what blindness and forgetfulness!

<summary>The author uses a vivid mathematical metaphor involving a parchment wrapped around the earth to illustrate that even the largest imaginable number cannot encompass the scale of eternity. He rebukes the spiritual blindness of those who sin playfully without fearing the infinite consequences.</summary>

->FOURTH<-

...destructive madness! For a very brief life, for a mere shadow of eternity, we labor beyond our strength; yet for the true eternity of a most blessed life, I do not say we labor beyond our strength, but we do not even deign to labor according to our strength. Moreover, to fail to acquire this life is to incur eternal death, which, besides being a torment heavier than all the torments of this life, also possesses this quality: that in the whole of eternity there will be no single hour to bring rest, much less an end to the evil.

### III. *The effects of contemplating eternity.*

And this is what made so many Christians, and so many holy martyrs, so ready and willing for any tortures whatsoever, and for any kind of death, so that when they were in the greatest pains and rolling in their own blood, they nevertheless—so courageous and cheerful, with such a constant expression and smiling face—insulted their torturers: *They kept the Eternal Years in their minds.* <note>original: "Annos Æternos in mente habuerunt." A reference to Psalm 77:5.</note>

And this is what drove so many thousands of men—and among these many who were previously impious—into the wilderness, and shut them up in monasteries and <term>cloisters</term> <note>A cloister (cœnobia) refers to a place of religious retirement or a conventual establishment.</note>; it led them from a soft and voluptuous life to a severe and rigid one: *They kept the Eternal Years in their minds.* And many religious men could be named who are still living today and confess that, from this single thought of eternity, the whole world began to seem foolish and displeasing to them; such a one was the blessed Teresa <note>Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582), the Spanish mystic and reformer of the Carmelite Order.</note>, the parent of a new religious way throughout the land of Spain.

And this is also what, for religious men and women and all others, [makes] the severe [laws] of life...

<summary>The author argues that the fear and hope of eternity motivate the faithful to endure extreme suffering and renounce worldly pleasures, citing the martyrs and Saint Teresa of Avila as primary examples.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, martyrdom, Saint Teresa of Avila, monasticism, penance, eternal death, Spain</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflection on the concept of Eternity, likely following a discussion on the nature of time or divine judgment.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

For those who embrace the laws <note>referring to divine or religious laws</note>, it renders all things easy and pleasant. This contemplation of **Eternity** persuades us that every labor is light and brief; this same thought commends prayers, studies, and vigils, making them lovable. It seasons hunger and thirst, and mitigates every deficiency of poverty. It makes hair-shirts <note>term: cilicia; garments made of coarse animal hair worn against the skin as a form of penance</note>, rigid beds, scourges, and whatever harshness of life there may be, tolerable and pleasing. Whoever keeps the *Eternal Years* <note>original: "Annos Æternos"</note> in mind, and impresses them deeper and deeper upon themselves through daily little meditations, yields to no labor and is broken by no inconveniences. 

If you were to offer such a person a kingdom, or the greatest delights and pleasures, he would nonetheless not exchange all these for his own state, however poor. Such a man complains of no thing or person; he endures all things and submits himself to all. For he constantly thinks: "How small a thing is this, and that, and this other!" I shall therefore suffer and I shall endure; this will not last forever. It is but a little hour in which my enemies oppress me. 

Go on then, you detractors! Tear me apart, you envious ones! I shall not withdraw myself from you; *this is your hour and the power of darkness* <note>original: "haec est hora vestra & potestas tenebrarum"; a citation of Luke 22:53</note>. I await the Day of the Lord; the eternal day. And why should I consume myself with lamentations? This whole life is but the death of a single little hour, and the victory—the triumph—is not difficult. Why should I turn pale at the threats of a raging sea? I already see the harbor. 

Now indeed the rains and the fury of the storms thunder down upon the heads of the upright, but it will not always be so hereafter, except for the enemies of God. Daniel prophesies: *Many, he says,* <insert>from those who sleep in the dust of the earth shall awake</insert> <note>The text breaks off here, referencing Daniel 12:2</note>

<summary>This page argues that meditating on eternity makes earthly suffering, poverty, and persecution easy to endure. The author encourages the reader to view life's hardships as a brief "little hour" compared to the everlasting peace of the "Day of the Lord."</summary>

# FOURTH SECTION

<margin>* Daniel, chapter 12.</margin>
*Concerning those who sleep in the dust of the earth, they shall awaken; some to eternal life, and others to reproach, that they may see it forever.* <note>original: "de his qui dormiunt in terra pulvere..." This refers to the biblical prophecy of the resurrection of the dead.</note>

<margin>† Wisdom, chapter 2.</margin>
In the ancient law, God, commanding Moses, said: "Make for yourself two trumpets of hammered silver; if you sound them once, the princes and heads of the multitude of Israel shall come to you; but if a longer and more rhythmic blast resounds, they shall move the camp." <note>The author is referencing Numbers 10:2, using the silver trumpets as a metaphor for spiritual calls to action.</note> These two trumpets are to be compared to two twin words: † *Now*, and *Forever*. The law of the world is: *Now* let us be joyful, *now* let us pluck the fruits of joy; *now* let us enjoy good things while they last; come, *now* let us crown ourselves with roses before they wither, *now* let us leave signs of our gladness everywhere. Those who attend to only this one trumpet, and prick up both ears to the word *Now* alone, often live just as if that *Forever* were never going to follow. Therefore, they do not move their camp; they most pleasantly forget that they are pilgrims. Wherever the lustful flesh invites them, they follow willingly, devoted entirely either to amassing wealth or to enjoying pleasures. Indeed, the blast of that *Now* so deafens their ears that, being deaf to the best warnings, they do not hear the *Forever* that is to follow. But those who admit both of these trumpets into their ears and souls—just as the Church sounds them six hundred times daily—and who compare that briefest *Now* with the longest and eternal *Forever*, do not deliberate for long. They move their camp, they live as pilgrims <term>pilgrims: those who view earthly life as a temporary journey toward a heavenly home</term>, they restrain the flesh, and they remember they are on a journey, sending their riches and pleasures ahead to their true fatherland.

\* *Daniel, chapter 12.* † *Wisdom, chapter 2.*

<summary>The author uses the biblical imagery of Moses' silver trumpets to contrast the worldly focus on the present moment ("Now") with the spiritual focus on eternity ("Forever"). He warns that those who ignore the call of eternity fail to live as "pilgrims" and instead become trapped by earthly pleasures.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the contrast between the temporal "Now" and the eternal "Always," focusing on the preparation for death and the specific example of the scholar Justus Lipsius.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

They prefer to enjoy those things *Always* in heaven, rather than *Now* on earth. It is certain that whoever perceives with a serious and attentive mind this short and prolonged clangor of both trumpets <note>The "two trumpets" likely refer to the calls of Time and Eternity</note>, and compares present things with future ones, and transitory things with eternal ones, he soon prepares his departure, looks about for his sepulcher, lays out his funeral shroud, places his own bier, and arranges all his affairs for the journey. Everywhere he remembers that he is on the road to eternity, and for that reason, he continually ponders this within himself: "Will I be able to give an account to God for all my words, deeds, and thoughts? And when am I to give it? What sentence will He pronounce upon me?" Let me therefore die to myself *Now*, so that I may live for myself and for God *Always*. It is well for that man who already, in a timely manner and indeed daily, thinks upon Eternity in this way. Whatever we may do, we shall arrive by this path—and perhaps shortly—at the gate of Eternity, where we shall see all of Eternity before us. Death, the final hour of life, will place us at this door and compel us to enter. Those who are dying teach us this quite often, both by their words and their gestures.

In the Christian year 1606, on the tenth day before the Calends of April <note>March 23, 1606</note>, *Justus Lipsius* died, a man most famous for his erudition and writings. He had often been accustomed, like Augustus <note>The Roman Emperor Augustus famously wished for a quick and painless death</note>, to wish for himself a <term>Euthanasia</term> <note>original: "Euthanasian"; here used in the classical sense of a "good" or "easy" death rather than modern medical intervention</note> and an easy death, as well as a free and intrepid mind in that moment of necessity. Both wishes were granted, for in only four days, he performed this greatest task: *To Die*. During this entire time, he spoke not a single word of his own writings or studies. Indeed, when some sought to offer him comfort from the Stoics <note>A school of ancient philosophy that emphasized endurance and logic; Lipsius was a famous "Neo-Stoic" scholar</note>...

<summary>This page reflects on the necessity of preparing for eternity by living a virtuous life and recounts the peaceful death of the famous scholar Justus Lipsius in 1606.</summary>
<keywords>Justus Lipsius, Eternity, Euthanasia, Stoicism, preparation for death, 17th century philosophy</keywords>

# FOURTH

If anyone whispered to him that he still had much to live for, he replied, "Those things are vain." Pointing his finger intently at a nearby image of the **crucified Christ** <note>original: "Christi crucifixi"</note>, he said: "*This is true patience*," and soon added with great spirit: "*Lord Jesus, grant me Christian patience.*" At the beginning of his worsening illness, his first care was to be cleansed, fortified, and comforted by the holy **Sacraments** <note>The sacred rites of the Church</note> of **Penance**, the **Eucharist**, and **Extreme Unction** <note>The anointing of the sick and dying</note>. Having assiduously and ardently sought the prayers of the pious, and with his dying eyes and hands raised toward the heavens, he himself prayed thus piously:

> O Mother of God, be present for your servant as he struggles with all eternity, and do not forsake me in this hour, upon which the eternal salvation of my soul depends.

We have here, my fellow Christian, a noble example of a Catholic man: let us likewise daily entreat the same from Christ our God, and from the Most Blessed Virgin, the Mother of God, and say many times a day with a burning soul:

> O Christ Jesus, O Mother of God, be present for your servant who is about to wrestle with all eternity, and do not forsake me in that hour, upon which the eternal salvation of my soul depends.

<summary>This page describes the final pious acts of a dying Catholic man who seeks strength through the Sacraments and the intercession of the Virgin Mary. It concludes by offering his prayer as a model for all Christians to use when contemplating their own death.</summary>

# CONSIDERATION
## THE FIFTH.

->*How others, even the wicked, have meditated upon Eternity.*<-

The ancient history of the Fathers relates that a religious man, while meditating upon the eightieth Psalm <note>In modern bibles, this is typically Psalm 90:4.</note>, finally came to those words: *For a thousand years in your sight are like yesterday when it is past* <note>original: "Quoniam mille anni ante oculos tuos, tanquam dies hesterna quæ præteriit"</note>; and here he paused, unable to sufficiently grasp by what reasoning a thousand years and a single little day could be compared to one another. At this point, they say a small bird was sent by God, which so soothed the man with the sweetness of its song that he thought the very long time during which he listened had scarcely been an hour. *The Spirit blows where it wills* <note>original: "Spiritus, ubi vult, spirat" - a reference to John 3:8.</note>. Not only have many of the righteous meditated upon eternity alongside Saint David, but even the wicked have done so, almost against their own will.

Benedict Renatus ‡ reports that there was a vain and impious man named Fulco, fashioned exactly according to the laws of the World. Just as he was unaccustomed to fasting, so too was he unused to keeping vigils; in sleep, as in other things, he allowed himself to lack for nothing. However, one night caught him in which he was unable to snore soundly on either ear as he usually did. Entirely unusual bouts of insomnia forced him to stay awake; he turned himself from side to side, yet still could not find sleep. He wished it were already day. Here, the Spirit of the Lord began to blow, albeit in an unknown land, for good thoughts—

In this man, such qualities were quite rare; therefore, out of the weariness of his sleepless nights, he began to ponder various things. This thought occurred to his mind: "What reward do you expect, lying here for two or three years continuously, in darkness, without friends, in a mild illness, without games and revelry, in these chains—though they be made of feathers—and yet also without the joy of banquets and the theater?" And certainly, I alone shall not depart exceptional and exempt: I must lie in sickness, whether I wish it or not, unless I should die by a sudden and unexpected death, which God forbid (<note>original: "bonus hic ventus, bonae cogitationes" - a metaphor suggesting a favorable wind or a change in spiritual direction</note>). But what kind of bed shall I have when death casts me down from this one? The body will rot under the earth; so I see others, so I see everyone treated after death. But what will become of the soul in the other world? I do not believe all mortals gather in one place. Where then are those people, and where are these? Besides heaven, are there not also purging flames <note>original: "flammae piaculares" - referring to the fires of Purgatory</note>, and is there not also Hell? Ah, what kind of bed will the damned have there? For how many years will they lie there? In what year, after the beginning, will those flames cease? Christ certainly not only threatens that the wicked are to be sent into eternal fire, but He actually sends them: the matter is too clear, too certain. Therefore, will they burn in flames forever? Therefore, will a thousand and again and again a thousand years not suffice to wash away the sins of a short life? Therefore, will they never see the sun, nor heaven, nor God, forever most miserable? Ah! Ah! By such thoughts as these he was made so sleepless and watchful, and he progressed so far, that for all the following nights and days, this contemplated eternity pursued him. He wished to shake—

# FIFTH SECTION

He was not able to drive away these troublesome worms of the soul; he did not stop his games, his drinking, his companions, or his banquets. Thus, while he was among others, he heard the morose complaints of Conscience <note>Latin: *Conscientia*; here personified as a nagging internal judge.</note> less often; yet when he returned to himself in solitude, he could not escape them. Eternity clung to his soul and pressed upon him. At last, he decided to amend his life and devote himself to better pursuits. 

"And what am I, a miserable man, doing here?" he said to himself. "I enjoy the world, and yet I do not enjoy it. I suffer many things I do not want, and I lack many things I wish to have. I serve: but who gives me my reward? I have easily seen thus far what the rewards of the World are, even when you have served it for a long time. And suppose I should enjoy whatever delights I could wish for—how long will this last? I am not certain of my life even for tomorrow; daily funerals prove this well enough. O Eternity, if only you did not exist! O Eternity, if you are found outside of heaven, you will be unpleasant and bitter even on the softest bed. We are indeed pulled away with difficulty from those things to which we have become accustomed; it is hard to be torn away from more sumptuous feasts, more generous cups, and dearer companions. But if we hesitate, death comes first and snatches all these things away. Why then do you delay? Why do you not declare this honorable necessity for yourself? Come, I shall be another man, or no man at all. This life is too short for me; eternity is too long. I must go elsewhere now. I am not merely changing: I am proceeding. Hail to you now, O Divine Perennity <note>Latin: *Perennitas*; a synonym for eternity, implying an unending state.</note>."

He did what he said, and devoting himself to the holy family of the Cistercians <note>A Catholic religious order of monks and nuns (the White Monks) known for their austerity and manual labor.</note>, he lived uprightly and died piously.

O Eternity, how few there are who think of you so seriously! How much fewer are those who weigh you in the balance and cling to the thought! All things...

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* *Bernard, Epistle 3.*</margin>
Other things are sought after, while eternity alone is held cheap. Riches are contemplated, but they are fleeting and must be left behind; honors are pursued, but they are soon to be taken away; pleasures are loved, but they have an evil and bitter end; rest is desired, but it is not long-lasting; friendship is sought, but death tears it apart; social life is everywhere in our prayers, but not that which is in heaven. Abundance is sought, but in a place where it is destined to fail. If we were to think of eternity more often, certainly our desires for things of such a short lifespan would grow less fervent. I give Saint Bernard as a witness*:
> To one gasping for eternal things, transitory things are a source of loathing. <note>original: "Æternis inhianti fastidio sunt transitoria."</note>

There are, however, those who have "eternity" on their lips—I know not what kind—and who swear off certain things as to be avoided forever. Promises of this sort are heard from time to time: "I will avoid this suspicious place that encourages sin forever; I will no longer approach that man, that woman, or that evil companion for eternity; I will avoid those banquets and those dances forever; it is enough to have sinned once, enough to have sinned so many times here and there, with these people and those." You propose well, my friend, and because you fear sin, you rightly flee even the danger of sin; but if only you would keep your word as holily as you promise it so easily. Sometimes scarcely a few days or hours pass before you do the exact same thing you so strongly swore off and cursed. One must act with consideration here, and with strength. Nothing should be promised to God rashly; but what has been promised must be kept holily and constantly, as shown by examples...

# FIFTH.

We are taught in a most brilliant manner how God punishes us if we break the faith given to Him.

### I. *The labors of man and the spider compared.*

<margin>* Isaiah chapter 28.</margin>
<margin>‡ Psalm 89.</margin>
There is another kind of eternity, but of the worst sort, which those promise to themselves who wish to build a heaven for themselves outside of heaven, and to be blessed before they are dead. Because of this, says Isaiah*, "Hear the word of the Lord, you scornful men: for you have said, we have struck a bargain with death, and with hell we have made a pact." <note>original: "percussimus fœdus cum morte"</note> O madmen, how nonexistent is this eternity of yours! Nothing is stable or perpetual in this prison <note>The author refers to the mortal world or the body as a prison.</note>. The Royal Prophet <note>A traditional title for King David, the purported author of the Psalms.</note> explains this elegantly: ‡ *Our years,* he says, *shall be considered as a spider.* <note>original: "Anni nostri sicut aranea meditabuntur." This is a Latin Vulgate translation of Psalm 90:9 (89 in the Douay-Rheims), often translated in English as "we spend our years as a tale that is told," but here interpreted literally as the "meditation" or "work" of a spider.</note> He could not have said it more briefly or better. For what else are all our years but a continuous exercise and a laborious meditation? The whole time of our life is exercised in vain labors, many sorrows, various fears, frequent suspicions, and almost innumerable anxieties; just as the spider weaves thread into threads, our labor is chained and continuous, a cycle of sighs, now so that we may enjoy this thing, now so that we may push that thing away from us. We do many things and undertake laborious tasks, and alas, we do not know that we are weaving a spider's web—with great effort, rare success, and no lasting effect. *Our years shall be considered as a spider.* The spider begins its web with great toil; it runs about for a long time and over great distances; it circles here and there, returning often to the same place; it consumes its very self while it completes that little wheel of manifold threads—

### **I. *The labors of man and the spider compared.***

There is another kind of eternity, but of the worst sort, which those promise to themselves who wish to build a heaven for themselves outside of heaven, and to be blessed before they are dead. Because of this, says Isaiah <note>original: "Isaias. cap. 28."</note>, hear the word of the Lord, you scoffers: for you have said, we have struck a treaty with death, and with hell we have made a pact. Oh insane men, how null is this eternity of yours! Nothing is stable or perpetual in this prison. The Royal Prophet <note>A traditional title for King David, the purported author of the Psalms.</note> explaining this elegantly says: *Our years shall be considered as the spider.* <note>original: "Psal. 89." This refers to Psalm 90:9 in modern numbering, comparing the fragility of life to a spider's web.</note> He could not have said it more briefly or better. For what else are all our years, if not a continuous exercise and a laborious meditation? The whole time of our life is occupied by vain labors, many sorrows, various fears, frequent suspicions, and almost innumerable anxieties; just as the spider weaves thread to thread; our labor is chained and continuous, a cycle of sighs, now so that we may enjoy one thing, now so that we may drive another away from us. We do many things and undertake laborious tasks, and alas, we do not know that we weave a spider's web, with great labor, rare success, and no lasting effect. *Our years shall be considered as the spider.* The spider weaves its web laboriously, it runs about for a long time and a great deal, it circles here and there, it returns often to the same place, and it consumes itself while it completes that little wheel of multiple threads—

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the futility of worldly pursuits; this page continues the metaphor of the spider to illustrate the vanity of human labor.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

dissolves. * It disembowels and eviscerates itself so that it may skillfully fashion its own airy little tent. To hang it high, to fasten it, and to make it firm, the creature goes back and forth six hundred times; it does not spare its own internal organs, but willingly spends them on this thinnest of textures. Yet, when this translucent and most delicate canopy finally hangs bound in place, and this woven work is finished, the entire labor collapses and perishes at the light stroke of a broom. The wretched spider is either killed within its own web, or is dragged to its death by it as if by a noose, to be crushed underfoot. Thus the unwise little animal has either spun its own funeral shroud in which to be wrapped, or has manufactured the rope by which it is killed.

In exactly this way, humans—just like the spider—exhaust themselves with many labors so that they might rise to high status, overflow with pleasures, and build, retain, and increase their wealth. Upon these attempts they expend their entire intellect and often their health; they run about, they are fatigued, they sweat, and they are weakened, just like the spider by the emptying of its bowels. And when they have done everything, they possess only spiders' webs and woven work fit for catching flies. Often they die within their own work; the days they hoped would be joyous, they find to be fatal; what they judged to be a palace is instead a tomb. Thus "our years pass away like a spider's web" <note>original: "anni nostri velut aranea meditantur" — a reference to the Latin Vulgate translation of Psalm 90:9, suggesting that life's efforts are as fragile and fleeting as a web.</note>: truly, for the most part, they are only "meditated" or planned, for we propose to do many things, yet we accomplish few. We strive with great effort, and for the most part, we do not arrive. Thus, there is no pact with death, no treaty with hell. We all waste away and die. The worst part is that we approach eternity so blindly—an eternity from which we shall never depart.

<margin>Genesis, Chapter 5</margin>
Guerricus <note>Guerric of Igny, a 12th-century Cistercian abbot known for his sermons</note>, when he heard the reading in the church from the book of Genesis: *And all the time that Adam lived was nine hundred and thirty years, and he died. And all the days of Seth were nine hundred and twelve years, and he died. And all the days of Enos were nine hundred and five years, and he died. And all the days of Methuselah were nine hundred and sixty-nine years, and he died,* etc. He so took death to heart, and the fact that he too would die was so powerfully impressed upon his mind, that he finally swore himself to the rules of Saint Dominic <note>The Dominican Order, known for its focus on preaching and study</note>, so that he might meet death more holily and more safely enter a blessed eternity, since no Eternity is to be found here on earth.

You know the precepts,* Christ responded, *if you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments.* Philip <note>Likely a reference to the jailer in Philippi, though the text names him Philip</note> in the city of Macedonia, the keeper of the prison, asked Paul—

# CONSIDERATION

Falling at the knees of Silas <note>original: "Silæ"</note>, he asks: *
**"Lords: what must I do to be saved?"** <note>original: "Domini: quid me oportet facere, ut salvus fiam?"</note> This is the best of questions; nothing better, nothing more useful could they have asked. But, O good God, where is this question found in the world? Perpetual questions fly everywhere; this one is found almost nowhere. Generally, men betray themselves by their questions, and drag into the light either their simplicity, or their curiosity, or some other hidden disease of the soul. He who so diligently asks where good wine is for sale, sufficiently reveals what occupies his care. Another asks something at which the listener cannot help but blush: here it is clear enough that the heart is full of that which the mouth overflows with. The world is full of such things. It is rare that one person asks another: **"Do you think this path leads to heaven?"** This is true of all vices, but especially of lust and luxury: when someone seeks the depths and begins to be submerged, they do not easily ask with a serious and sincere mind: **"By this way of living, shall I obtain a blessed eternity? Is this the way to the stars?"** <note>original: "siccine itur ad astra?" — a classical poetic phrasing for achieving immortality or divinity.</note> Yet those who ask this least of all are those for whom life is pleasant and sweet; who feel little sorrow and affliction, or if they do feel it, they do everything they can to avoid feeling it. To suffer, among them, is the extreme of evils: provided it goes well for them now, whatever happens to others, and whatever may happen in that eternity of which they think nothing. Their daily song is: **"The heaven of heavens belongs to the Lord, but the earth he has given to the children of men."** <note>original: "Cælum cœli Domino terram autem dedit filiis hominum" — a quote from Psalm 115:16, often used here to justify worldly focus.</note> Nor are they lacking in comforts of body and soul— <note>The text cuts off at "ani-", the catchword for "animi" (of the soul) on the following page.</note>
