But if God, by His most secret judgments, should reject and condemn a man, and permit him to live according to the lust of an unrestrained soul, He allows almost everything to turn out prosperously and happily for that man. This is done so that the wretch is not punished twice—both here and in the hereafter—and so that, if he has done any good, he receives his reward immediately. Concerning such "unluckily fortunate" men, the Royal Psalmist <note>The "Royal Psalmist" refers to King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> truly declared: *They are not in the labor of men, neither shall they be scourged with men: they shall go according to their own inventions.* <note>original: "In labore hominum non sunt, & cum hominibus non flagellabuntur: ibunt in adinventionibus suis." For whomever God has destined to lead into the way of that blessed eternity, He certainly does not spare, but continually applies the scourge to him. From among countless witnesses, I present a single but great one; I believe nothing similar has been seen or heard for many centuries past.

### III. *How God punishes here, so that He may spare there; an unheard-of example.*

In the year one thousand one hundred and eighty-five after the Virgin's birth <note>The year 1185 AD.</note>, Andronicus, Emperor of the East <note>Andronicus I Komnenos, Byzantine Emperor.</note>, in the third year of his reign, was defeated by Isaac Angelus. Two heavy iron chains were thrown around his neck, and, burdened with shackles, he was greeted with the most insulting words; thus adorned, he was finally brought before Isaac...

<column-break/>

<meta>catchword: led</meta>

->* Psalm 72. † Ecclesiasticus chapter 30.<-

<summary>This page argues that worldly prosperity can be a sign of divine rejection, while earthly suffering is often a tool used by God to prepare a soul for eternity. It introduces the historical fall of the Byzantine Emperor Andronicus I as a primary example of this divine discipline.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Justice, Punishment, Prosperity, Andronicus I Komnenos, Isaac II Angelos, Byzantine Empire, Book of Psalms, Suffering</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a fallen ruler's public humiliation and torture, following his removal from power.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

He was led away. Here, he was reproached for his cruelty and tyranny, and everyone was given the license to dare whatever they wished against him. It was a joy for those he had provoked to take their revenge upon their enemy. And so, blows were struck against him, his back was ignominiously beaten, his beard was plucked out, his hair was torn, and his teeth were knocked out. Then, he was dragged out into public and exposed to the mockery of all; he was even beaten with fists by the common women. Afterward, his right hand was cut off, and thus mutilated, he was thrust into a prison <term>ergastulum: a Roman term for a private prison or workhouse for slaves and debtors</term> for thieves and bandits, without food, without drink, and without anyone to attend to him. 

After a few days had passed, one of his eyes was gouged out. Thus foully treated—deformed by the loss of one eye and one hand, poorly clothed in a short and skimpy little tunic, and rendered infamous by his shaved and bald head—he was placed backward upon a mangy camel. A mock crown made of garlic was cinched around his head, and the camel's tail was placed in his left hand to serve as a scepter. In this manner, he was led through the forum in the style of a triumph <note>original: "triumphi forma" — a bitter parody of the Roman victory parade</note> in a slow procession. 

Here the most insolent commoners and the filthy, loitering crowd of the marketplace made an attack on him with wild spirits. They took no account of the fact that only the day before yesterday <note>original: "nudius tertius"</note> he had been Emperor <term>Imperator: the supreme commander or sovereign</term>, crowned with the royal diadem, praised, honored, celebrated, and adored by all; nor did they care that they themselves had bound their own faith and goodwill toward him by oath. Fury provided weapons to everyone: some struck his head with clubs, others stuffed his nostrils with filth; others squeezed sponges filled with the excrement of humans and cattle and with liquid filth into his face, while others pricked his sides with spits. Some pelted him with stones, others with mud.

<meta>This page continues the account of the downfall and public execution of the Byzantine Emperor Andronicus I Comnenus (reigned 1183–1185), following his capture and humiliation by the populace of Constantinople.</meta>

# FIFTH SECTION

<note>The text continues describing the mob's assault.</note> ...they cursed him; some shouted that he was a rabid dog, others a worthless fool <note>original: "fungum & bardum," literally a mushroom and a dullard</note>. One shameless woman snatched a pot full of boiling water from the kitchen and poured it over the head of the Emperor as he passed by; there was no one who did not seek to do him harm. Thus, at last, he was led into the theater amidst the greatest mockery, and soon dragged down from the camel and hung by his feet between two columns. Although he had already endured six hundred evils, he conducted himself plainly as a man, a hero, and a Christian. He was not heard to lament, or wail, or accuse his fortune; indeed, he would have done so in vain. Instead, he began to settle his accounts with God and pray for the forgiveness of his sins, for he repeatedly groaned this one thing: *Lord have mercy, Lord have mercy.* <note>original: "Domine miserere," the Latin rendering of the Greek "Kyrie eleison."</note>

Wretched Andronicus, that you are compelled to bear such great things; yet happy are you, that you bear them patiently and acknowledge that sins must be so expiated. But the infuriated crowd did not spare him even while he hung there alive: having torn away his tunic, they struck and mangled him with their hands in various ways. One more ferocious than the rest drove a sword through the belly of the hanging man into his intestines; two others, wishing to make a trial of whose sword was sharper, leaned in with both hands and pierced through the back of his body. At this, the most miserable Emperor with difficulty moved his mutilated right hand to his mouth; many thought he did this to draw out the warm blood still dripping from the fresh wound; in such a wretched way he broke off his life. After several days had passed, he was taken down from the infamous gallows and cast under the arch of the theater like a slaughtered beast, until some more humane persons moved him to another place,

# CONSIDERATION

Isaac, however, permitted him to be buried by no one. <note>Isaac II Angelos, who overthrew Andronicus in 1185.</note> O Andronicus, O Emperor of the East, how great a grace God granted you, who willed that you suffer these things for a few days, so that you might not perish for all days; for a short time you were miserable, so that you would not be so for eternity. Nor would I doubt that you held the *Eternal Years* <note>original: "Annos Æternos"; a reference to Psalm 77:5, often used in meditations on eternity.</note> in your mind, when you endured these things so bravely.

Nicetas Choniates, <note>A Byzantine Greek historian (c. 1155–1217) who witnessed these events.</note> who lived in that age, wrote these things; he also commends Andronicus for being most tenacious in the Catholic faith, and at the same time most devoted to the Holy Apostle Paul, whose Epistles he was accustomed to have at hand and always open. Nor indeed did the Apostle allow that devotion to be offered to him in vain; he took care of his follower Andronicus, for when his end was imminent, an image of the Apostle adorned in gold and placed in a sacred building was seen to weep abundantly. Andronicus himself immediately accepted this omen as nothing other than a messenger of coming disaster.

O Christians, keep the *Eternal Years* in mind; thus, whatever evils must be borne, you will endure them far more easily: whatever is compared with eternity will seem brief. *For that which is at present the momentary and light burden of our tribulation, works in us an eternal weight of glory, beyond measure in its sublimity.* <note>original: 2 Corinthians 4:17. The author translates the Latin Vulgate text into his argument.</note> For this reason, Augustine cries out and prays so earnestly: *Lord, here burn, here cut, provided that you spare me in eternity.* <note>A famous prayer attributed to St. Augustine regarding divine discipline in this life versus punishment in the next.</note> And Saint Fulgentius, <note>Fulgentius of Ruspe (c. 462–533), a North African bishop.</note> although most holy, when he was closer to death, seventy days before he finished his life, [repeated] the same...

# CONSIDERATION
## SIXTH.

->*How the Holy Fathers, the Church, and the Scripture urge us to meditate upon Eternity.*<-

<margin>* Psalm 12</margin> <note>The original cites Psalm 11, following the Vulgate numbering; in modern Bibles, this is Psalm 12.</note>
It is a Catholic rite to offer supplications and go about in procession on fixed days of the year to increase the honor of God and the Saints. The demon emulates this: he has his own followers who offer supplications and go about in circles. The royal prophet <note>King David, traditionally credited with the Psalms.</note> saw these people: *The wicked walk in a circle* \*. They arrange their lives in such a way that they move from banquet to banquet, from luxury to luxury, and from crime to crime. This is their "circuit"; and when they think this circle of wickedness is nearly complete, and the sphere of their lust is somehow rounded off, they begin anew, returning to their former and ancient ways, until death runs up uninvited to those circling in this manner.

<margin>‡ Job 1</margin>
The children of Job had established this law among themselves: that the days of feasting should pass in a rotation, with one being invited by the others. Their excellent parent rightly noticed that this circular transition of banquets would scarcely happen without sin: *Therefore he sent to them and sanctified them, and rising up early, he offered holocausts for each one* ‡. <note>Job 1:5. Job feared his children might have "cursed God in their hearts" during their revelry.</note>

# SIXTH <note>original: "SEXTA," likely referring to the sixth section or discourse of the work</note>

<margin>* Psalm 76 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 77:17-18</note></margin>
Therefore, just as the wicked rejoice in the circularity of their pleasures and spend their days surrounded by goods, so God will establish a circuit for them—but one that is perpetual, consisting of torments, and eternal. The blessed David foresaw this very thing: *For your arrows pass through; the voice of your thunder is in the wheel.* <note>original: "Etenim sagittæ tuæ transeunt, vox tonitrui tui in rota." The "wheel" here symbolizes the cyclical, inescapable nature of divine judgment.</note> Hunger, war, plague, hardships, diseases, calamities, death itself, and finally all adversities that press upon us before the first death, are God's arrows; but they fly past. They are winged and leap very quickly from one person to another. But truly, the voice of thunder—the voice of divine wrath and fury in the dungeons of the underworld—will be driven around like a wheel, and that for eternal ages. This wheel, as if it were packed with gunpowder <note>original: "pulvere tormentario," a 17th-century metaphor comparing divine wrath to the explosive power of a cannon</note>, once it has caught fire, will burn for all eternities.

<margin>‡ Deuteronomy 32</margin>
‡ *A fire is kindled in my fury, and it shall burn even to the lowest depths of hell.* <note>original: "Ignis succensus est in furore meo, & ardebit usque ad inferni novissima."</note> There is another circuit here, and it too is eternal: there will be a passage from inexplicable cold to immense heat, and from these a return back to the former.

<margin>* Job 24; Matthew 22</margin>
Let him pass, says * Job, from the waters of snow to excessive heat. This is more expressly designated by both the gnashing of teeth and the weeping of eyes. <note>A reference to the "pains of loss and sense" often described in medieval and early modern theology as alternating between extreme ice and fire.</note> In order to more fully depict this horrible and incomprehensible wheel of eternity, the proper order requires that we show by what reasoning the Holy Fathers agree with the Church, and the Church with the Divine Scripture, on this matter. There are various admonitions from them which, if we pay attention to them, will ensure we do not easily forget eternity.

# CONSIDERATION

### *I. The responses of the Holy Fathers and the Church regarding this matter.*

From the entire number of the Holy Fathers, five in particular ought to be heard who lived at different times: Augustine, Chrysostom, Gregory, Bernard, and Lawrence Justinian. <note>These represent a chronological span of Church authority from the 4th to the 15th century.</note>

The first question here is one that might seem vain and foolish. What is easier or more tolerable: to suffer for three days the pains of the head, eyes, teeth, or a kidney stone <term>calculi: a common historical term for painful mineral deposits in the body</term>, or the twisting of the bowels, or (if there be any other sharp pains) to see no sleep by night or day, being tortured almost without any breathing room; or, on the other hand, to eat a small piece of a fish in which the gall bladder has been crushed? <note>Gall was used as a common metaphor for extreme bitterness.</note> This is an utterly ridiculous and most idle question. It is much more pleasant to devour an entire bitter fish of that kind than to endure such great pains for even a single day; the bitterness of that fish will neither take away life nor create diseases of itself; only the mouth, which rejects bitter things, will complain the most.

This is the correct answer. Yet, in the meantime, how many thousands of men choose the former, whether by their words or by their very deeds? How often is it cried out in exhortations, in confessions, and in sermons; how eloquently and openly is it foretold: "Behold, the matter concerns the eternal salvation of your soul; eternal torments await you if you continue on this path." Christ showed another way, and walked another way. Repent! You have wandered long enough. It is placed within your own will to enjoy heaven or to be excluded from it; God is not lacking to those who are willing. These things do indeed have <insert>something</insert> of bitterness...

<summary>The author argues that humans irrationally choose the "bitterness" of eternal damnation over the temporary "bitterness" of repentance, using a comparison between physical illness and eating a bitter fish.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, eternal salvation, Church Fathers, Augustine, Chrysostom, suffering, free will</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral and theological reflections on the necessity of suffering and self-discipline as the path to eternal life, following the themes of spiritual endurance established in the preceding sections.</meta>

# SIXTH

Indeed, abstinence and fasting, the confession of crimes <note>original: "criminum confessio," likely referring to the sacrament of Penance or general admission of sin</note> and the chastisement of the body, the guarding of the senses and the victory over oneself; a chaste and continent life—this is not an easy task for everyone. Whatever it may be, something must be endured. *Was it not necessary for Christ to suffer, and so enter into his glory?* <note>A reference to Luke 24:26</note> Let not the brief labor terrify you: for a few years, or perhaps only for a few days, it is necessary to both Act and Suffer bravely; the rest and joy will be eternal. He conquers all who conquers himself, who restrains himself, who violently resists his own depraved affections—and does so for the sake of heaven, for the sake of Christ, and for the sake of a most blessed eternity.

After his resurrection, Christ served his followers fish placed upon hot coals, by which he might teach how much was still to be endured: that a pleasant and sweet life was not to be expected. They were to be stoned, whipped, crucified, and flayed; for this was the way to a joyful resurrection in the future, and to a life spent among the blessed in eternity. All things that we see are modest and small, and not to be compared with the immortal happiness which is not yet seen.

These things are said, but they are not cared for. And here is that "bitter fish" <note>original: "felleus piscis," a metaphor for the difficult truths of the Gospel that are hard to swallow</note> which is so often served in sermons and in books; for these and other similar things are repeatedly forced upon us and drummed into us, read and heard, and yet they are not believed; they are valued little, and are buried by other cares and by forgetfulness. And how often even Conscience itself acts the part of the preacher, and thrusts forward these healthy warnings, insists, discourages [vice], and nudges us—yet it accomplishes nothing.

# CONSIDERATION

...it is all in vain. For neither the preacher nor **Conscience** can persuade many people. On the contrary, they raise objections and even throw back their own maxims: *“As long as things are well here: the future is uncertain: let the present be comfortable: none of the dead return, for there is no one known to have come back from the underworld: come therefore, let us enjoy good things.”* <note>The author is quoting the common "eat, drink, and be merry" attitude of skeptics, likely referencing Wisdom 2:1-6.</note> These are their repetitive songs. But let **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential theologian.</note> define the question at hand: *It is better,* he says, *to have a small amount of bitterness in the throat than eternal torment in the bowels.* Indeed, it is truly better to pay the debts one has contracted in this world rather than the next; it is far more preferable to discipline the body here for sixty or seventy years with continuous and daily fasts, <term>scourges</term> <note>The use of whips for self-discipline.</note>, and <term>hair-shirts</term> <note>Coarse garments made of goat hair worn against the skin as a form of penance.</note>, than to be held in torments there for even a single day. But let another of the Holy Fathers follow.

**St. Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), known as "Golden-Mouthed" for his eloquence.</note> proposes a second question*. *If someone,* he says, *within a hundred years, had seen a sweet and happy dream for only one night, and for that reason was punished for a hundred years, would that dream be worth seeking? Furthermore,* he says, *what a dream is to a hundred years, this present life is to the future life—indeed, much less; and what a drop is to the sea, a thousand years are to the future eternity.* And confirming this elsewhere: *What will you compare,* he says, *to infinite time?* ‡ *What are ten thousand years when you consider infinite ages! Is it not as much as the smallest drop of water compared to the abyss?* Do not hope after this life—

And certainly, whoever has let this thought of Eternity sink a little deeper into the soul will be able to boast: *He set charity in order within me*; for he will preserve a better order of love, loving himself less, and God more and more fervently, and for the sake of God, loving even his enemies. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the "wine" of divine wisdom.</note> ...tion, so that whoever tastes it a little too deeply becomes drunk, but with a drunkenness of the best desires, which lead only to the amendment of life, to the heavenly homeland, and to eternal pleasures. The Apostles were reproached for being drunk on new wine <note>Acts 2:13</note>; they certainly were, but from this cellar. Although Saint Gregory mentions eternity quite often, he said this most briefly and truly: ***That which delights is momentary, that which tortures is eternal.*** <note>original: "Momentaneum quod delectat, æternum quod cruciat." A famous maxim in medieval ascetic literature.</note> Here, anyone might rightly wish with Job: *Who will grant me that these very words be engraved in a book with an iron pen?* <note>Job 19:23-24</note> Namely, these very words: *That which delights is momentary, that which tortures is eternal.* The book suited for this writing is the heart of man; the reed or iron pen is serious meditation; the purple ink is the blood of Christ. And these words, thus engraved upon the breast, are to be turned over especially, and repeated more frequently, when pleasure entices, when lust stimulates, when luxury invites, when the flesh resists, when the spirit flags, or when there is an occasion or danger of sin.

Saint Bernard comes in the fourth place, soon to respond to the question set before him. Human life is such that men are of the most diverse feelings, just as they are of the most diverse faces. Some are found who are continually and heavily afflicted, so that they think they must almost succumb to the affliction. Poverty presses one, disease another, hidden debts a third; cares disturb one, while injuries or slanders so press and perturb another,

# SIXTH SECTION

Just as those who are faint-hearted <note>original: "pusillanimes"</note> and impatient sometimes wish for death upon themselves, rushing to the river or to the rope—for in this way, the wretched believe an end must be placed upon their hardships, when in fact they are making a beginning to those for which no end will be granted. On the other hand, there are others who are upright and most prepared for every expression of God’s will; they neither desire to die quickly, nor to live long. Does God want them to die? They want the same themselves. Does He want them to die soon? They want that very thing too. Does He want them to live long? They do not resist. Thus, to will and not to will the same as God is their way. Besides these two kinds of men, there is a very large group that desires a long life; there is hardly anyone so old that he does not hope and desire to live yet one more year. Among these, there is almost no satiety of life; death hastens too much for them, it arrives too early, and it is believed to come before its time.

<margin>* On Psalm 91, sermon 17. <note>The original cites Psalm 90, following the Vulgate numbering.</note></margin>
At this point, it could be asked: which of these men live, or are going to live, the longest? Saint Bernard, commenting on this divine promise—*I will satisfy him with length of days*—exclaims in wonder: "What is so long as that which is eternal? What is so long as that which is cut off by no end? A good end to life is eternity; a good end is that which has no end." He adds: "The true day is that which knows no setting; eternal truth, true eternity, and therefore a true and eternal fellowship." Thus it must be said that only those will live a truly long life who will never die in heaven; and those—alas!—will die a death all too prolonged who will be always dying and will never truly live in hell <note>original: "tartaro," a classical term for the underworld used here for the Christian Hell</note>, even though they live forever.

<summary>This page contrasts the desperate desire for death among the impatient with the holy indifference of the righteous, ultimately defining "long life" not as earthly years but as the eternity of heaven compared to the eternal death of hell.</summary>
<keywords>patience, suicide, divine will, Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, eternity, heaven, hell, Psalm 91</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological reflection on the nature of eternity, likely following a discussion on the distinction between the earthly realm and the afterlife.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Saint Lawrence Justinian <note>The first Patriarch of Venice (1381–1455), known for his ascetic writings.</note> will explain our final inquiry to us. There are many things in this world which nature has assigned to one specific place in such a way that they cannot be found elsewhere, or at least not in their entirety. From the flowers of the New World <note>A reference to the Americas, common in 17th-century Jesuit and devotional literature.</note>, one can only obtain the seed; from many living creatures, only the pelt. Eternity is a thing so uniquely belonging to the other world that we can possess nothing of it here except its seed. What, then, is the seed of Eternity? The seeds of eternity, says Lawrence Justinian, are these: *Contempt of oneself, the Gift of Charity, and a Taste for the Works of Christ\**.

To despise others is a tree that covers the whole world and grows most abundantly to fuel the fires of hell. To despise oneself is both the smallest seed and the one most unknown to the world. This is what Christ brought from heaven, *who emptied himself, taking the form of a servant and becoming obedient* <note>original: "qui exinanivit semetipsum..." quoting Philippians 2:7-8</note> not only as far as the stable and the manger, but even unto Calvary, even to the cross, to death, to the grave, and into Limbo <note>The "Harrowing of Hell," where Christ was believed to descend to liberate the souls of the just.</note>, *because of which God also exalted him*. Behold, this seed grew into the widest branch, into the tallest tree of all.

Regarding Charity, the same blessed Patriarch, making mention of it, says: *The glory of eternal reward is according to the measure of Charity†*. For he to whom less is forgiven, loves less. He obtains less grace who has less charity; and where there is less grace, there will also be less glory. Thus, it is entirely certain: the more you love God, the more eternal—

---

\* *Book on Obedience, chapter 26.*
† *On the Discipline and Life of Monks; Luke 7.*

It emphasizes that Christ's self-emptying is the ultimate model for the humility required to achieve eternal glory.</summary>

# SIXTH SECTION. 103

you heap up rewards for yourself. The whole law is *Love*, but a love that is pure, chaste, and divine. The third point was the *Taste for the Works of Christ*. There is a well-known saying regarding those who study Rhetoric <note>The art of persuasive speaking and writing, a cornerstone of classical education.</note>: they are judged to have made progress when they truly savor Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator considered the master of Latin style.</note>. In the exact same way, it can be affirmed that those who find the divine teachings of Christ flavorful are progressing in virtue. 

Whoever finds almost no taste in the words, deeds, and life of Christ—who is not affected, delighted, or moved by those things which concern the soul, piety, blessedness, and the divine—but instead finds that eating, drinking freely, walking, laughing, joking, and playing are what truly "taste" good; such a person may safely say to himself: "O my God, how completely the seed of Eternity is lacking in me!" 

For when I descend into myself, I clearly perceive within me what spirits I endure <note>original: "quos ego patiar manes" - a classical reference to the spirits of the dead or one's internal disposition.</note> and where my desires invite me: to lead dances, to grow merry at banquets, to stay awake over wine cups, to move the dice-box and the tiles <note>References to gambling games common in the early modern period.</note>, to listen to vanities and fables, to read impure pages, to applaud suggestive love songs <note>original: "cantiunculas Venereas" - literally 'little songs of Venus,' the goddess of love.</note>, and to keep pace with my companions—this I can do; this is pleasant and delightful to me. 

But to hear or read much about Christ, or about the vigils and fasts of the saints—this is unpleasant and disagreeable. I can hardly endure a whole sermon; a single little hour of it seems longer than a fixed age, and therefore must be cheated away by either sleep or conversation. It is easy to pronounce judgment on such a man: *here there is no Taste for the Works of Christ*. 

But let us now look at the Church’s understanding of Eternity.

# CONSIDERATION

The Church values the memory of Eternity so highly that there is no psalm, prayer, or hymn which it does not conclude with a reminder of Eternity; hence the perpetual and solemn closing of the psalms: *Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit: As it was in the beginning* <note>original: "Sicut erat in principio" — the author clarifies this refers to the state before any beginning, from eternity, without any origin</note> *and now, and always, and unto the ages of ages* <note>original: "in sæcula sæculorum" — a Latin idiom for forever and ever</note>, which are infinite, innumerable, and incomprehensible. I say nothing of the vestments, rites, and sacred ceremonies, many of which instill this same memory of Eternity, but let us move from the streams to the fountain itself.

### II. *Clear testimonies of the divine Scriptures concerning Eternity.*

I produce only three witnesses: a Prophet, an Apostle, and an Evangelist.

How many groans go up daily from men who are cast down and despised by all? You may find people everywhere saying: "Wretched me! Because I am poor, I count so few friends; I lie dishonored before everyone, I am spurned everywhere, and trodden under the feet of almost all." Wait, my friend, for a little while and be patient; the suns of all your days have not yet set. Attend to the divine promise; Baruch <note>A deuterocanonical prophet often associated with Jeremiah</note> declares <margin>Chapter 5, verse 2</margin>:
> *God will clothe you in a double cloak of justice, and will place upon your head the crown of eternal honor.*

There are others who accuse nature, complaining that a long life is granted to crows, but one far too short is granted to man. Listen, you complainers: there remains yet another life, where the brief—

this force and vanity shall have ceased; believe the Blessed Paul <note>original: "Beato Paulo"</note> as he testifies*: *For we know that if our earthly house of this habitation be dissolved, that we have a building from God, a house not made with hands, eternal in the heavens.* What great loss is it, therefore, if this little hut of clay <note>original: "luteum tuguriolum," a metaphor for the mortal body</note> of our body should collapse, when a golden palace has been prepared for us, never to fall? To the Prophet and the Apostle, the Evangelist Matthew <note>original: "Euangelista Matthæus"</note> comes as a witness, through whose mouth Christ speaks in these words †: *If your hand or your foot causes you to stumble, cut it off and cast it from you; it is better for you to enter into life maimed or lame, than having two hands or two feet to be cast into eternal fire. And if your eye causes you to stumble, pluck it out and cast it from you; it is better for you to enter into life with one eye, than having two eyes to be cast into the hell of fire <note>original: "gehennam ignis"</note>.* O fire! O hell! O Eternity <note>original: "Æternitas"</note>! Whatever loss of time there may be, when compared to the loss of Eternity, it is a gain, not a loss. In this sense, Christ gives a handwritten bond <term>chirographum: a formal legal document or promissory note written in one's own hand</term> of pledge or agreement, recorded in the words of Saint Matthew ‡: *And everyone,* he says, *who has left house, or brothers, or sisters, or father, or mother, or wife, or children, or lands for my name's sake, shall receive a hundredfold <note>original: "centuplum"</note> and shall possess eternal life.* Is this promise regarding a blessed eternity not clear enough, when that hundredfold is given as a pledge? Indeed, when Christ warns of the final judgment in the same Evangelist,

# CONSIDERATION

Eternity is mentioned distinctly three times: eternal punishment, eternal fire, and eternal life.

Pachomius <note>Saint Pachomius (c. 292–348 AD), the founder of Christian cenobitic monasticism in Egypt.</note> weighed these matters with profound meditation. After undertaking so many labors in the vast desert, he devised a new way of sleeping—or rather, of staying awake. For fifteen years, he never allowed sleep to take him while reclining or lying down in his hut; nor did he lean against any support. Instead, he sat in the middle of his cell, free on all sides, so that he did not even rest his back or side against the wall. If sleep wished to conquer him, it would have to conquer one who was not lying down, not resting sweetly, and who refused to be defeated. This holy man was greatly afflicted by this stubbornness of vigils <note>Vigils: the practice of staying awake for prayer and spiritual discipline.</note>, yet he bore this most difficult struggle against sleep with a calm mind, tasting in advance the hope of that eternal rest in heaven. The meditation on perpetual rest led Pachomius to the point where he counted even lying on the ground and having the earth for a bed among his delights.

Since, therefore, the holy Fathers, the Church, and the Scriptures propose that Eternity should be weighed in various ways, it is now our duty—for whoever wishes to be eternal in heaven—to think seriously:

> O my God, how rarely and how coldly have I thought of Eternity until now! Even though I become closer to Eternity with every passing hour, from now on I shall play less with such a serious matter. And if I notice many things flowing according to my wishes, and my affairs turning out prosperously and happily, the next thing I must do is think: "And for how long?"

<summary>This page discusses the ascetic discipline of Saint Pachomius, who deprived himself of comfortable sleep to meditate on eternity, and exhorts the reader to reflect on the fleeting nature of earthly success.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Pachomius, eternity, asceticism, vigils, desert fathers, spiritual discipline, meditation</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on the brevity of earthly life compared to the vastness of eternity, focusing here on the endurance of suffering.</meta>

# SIXTH SECTION. 107

How long will this last? Will the weather always remain so serene? Will Apollo <note>original: "Phœbus"; the sun god, used here as a metaphor for good fortune</note> and Fortune always smile? And what reward will the Eternity that soon follows repay me for this sweet but brief, for this pleasing but dangerous happiness? 

But if, indeed, my affairs become troubled—if many things happen contrary to my wishes, if I am afflicted, if I am tortured, if evils and misfortunes conspire against me from every side, rush upon me, and harass me—I shall diligently think upon this one thing: Let it be, just let it be; let the storm carry out the commands of the fates. Let this great and angry sea shudder, let the winds of affliction roar, let the waves of disturbance push forward, let the clouds of temptation threaten, and let the darkness of sorrow envelop me. Even if the shattered world should fall upon me: ruins do not last forever, nor do storms. These winds will eventually drop, the waves will subside, and the clouds and hail will be dispelled. Whatever I suffer here at last will not endure eternally. My cross will fall, at least when I fall as a victim to death. 

But truly, more turbulent than all storms is to be delivered to eternal flames; that is a long torment. All other things that exist outside of Eternity are brief, fleeting, and momentary. They are shadows and a dream (as Chrysostom <note>St. John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquence</note> says), whatever they may be: let us hope for and look forward to those eternal things. 

And how does Christ impress that "Little While" <note>original: "Modicum"; a reference to John 16:16, "A little while, and ye shall not see me"</note> upon his disciples? He calls all his tortures and his most bitter death on the cross a "Little While"; he labels all the labors of the Apostles and their violent deaths through execution a "Little While." Why, therefore, should whatever I suffer not also seem a "Little While" to me, even if for a hundred...

<summary>The author reflects on the temporary nature of both earthly joy and suffering, arguing that even the greatest worldly trials are merely a "little while" when compared to the weight of eternity.</summary>

->CONSIDERATION<-

I shall endure this for years. *“For yet a little while, and he that shall come will come, and will not tarry.”* <note>original: "Adhuc enim modicum aliquantulumque qui venturus est, veniet, & non tardabit." Quoting Hebrews 10:37.</note> Thus I shall proceed, and I shall judge this one thing further to be necessary: that I do nothing against my conscience, and nothing against God. All things are safe for him, for whom a blessed Eternity is safe.

### III. *All things are but a Little, except Eternity.*

Truly, it is far too much a "Little" <term>Modicum: a small amount or a brief moment</term>, whatever labor or pain must be endured here. Augustine says: <note>original: "Hoc modicum, inquit, longum nobis videtur, quoniam adhuc agitur ; cum finitum fuerit, tunc sentiemus quàm modicum fuerit." From Tractate 102 on the Gospel of John.</note> This little while seems long to us because it is still passing; when it has ended, then we shall feel how small a thing it was.

The wisest of men, in order to depict the entire span of human life—even if extended to a hundred years—with his own brush, chose the smallest of things to express it. For thus Ecclesiasticus <note>A book of the Biblical Apocrypha/Deuterocanon also known as Sirach.</note> clearly affirms: *“The number of a man's days at most are a hundred years: they are accounted as a drop of water of the sea, and as a pebble of sand, so are these few years in the day of eternity.”* <note>original: "Numerus dierum hominum, ut multum centum anni : quasi gutta aquæ maris deputati sunt, & sicut calculus arenæ, sic exigui anni in die ævi." Quoting Sirach 18:8.</note> Behold, why do you applaud yourselves, O long-lived elders of a hundred years? What are all our years? A tiny grain of sand and a small drop of the sea. And what is a pebble compared to the highest mountains of sand? And what is a small drop compared to the deepest abyss of the ocean? So your fifty, sixty, or hundred years, O elders, are but a Little; they are nothing compared to that—

Our life is a pebble, but not a precious pebble, not of gold or gems, but of sand. Our life is a drop, but not a drop of sweet water, but of the most salt-filled sea. *All his days are full of sorrows and hardships, nor does his mind rest at night,* says the Preacher <note>original: "Ecclesiasticus," here referring to the author of the book of Ecclesiastes</note>. Augustine truly says †: *Recall,* he says, *the years from Adam even unto this day; run through the Scriptures: it was almost yesterday that Adam fell from paradise. For where are the times that have passed? If you had lived through that whole time, from when Adam was sent out of paradise until today, you would certainly see that your life had not been long, having flown away so quickly. But how long is the life of any single man? Add as many years as you like, lead the longest old age—what is it? Is it not a morning breeze?* All these things are most true. For tell me, I ask, where is Adam now? Where is Cain now? And where is long-lived Methuselah? Where is Noah? Where is Shem? Where is Eber? And where is the most obedient Abraham? Where is Jacob? Where is Joseph? They lived; they were Trojans <note>original: "fuerunt Troës," a classical idiom meaning "they are no more," or "their glory is a thing of the past."</note>. So passes life, so passes the glory of the world. Oh dew! Oh vanity! What do you strive for, what long things do you hope for? It is brief, it is a small thing, it is vile, it is a mere point—whatever you see here. Truly said Gregory the Great ‡: *Every length of the present life is known to be a mere point when it is ended by the finish.*

# CONSIDERATION

In the blink of an eye, all things are closed: I have seen the end of all perfection, but your commandment is exceedingly broad <note>original: "omnis consummationis vidi finem, latum mandatum tuum nimis" — a reference to Psalm 119:96</note>. Why then do we say any time is *long*? For the past no longer exists; the future is not yet; and the present—what is it? That single little hour is driven by fleeting particles: whatever of it has flown away is past; whatever remains of it is future. * Where then is the time that we call *long*? Not without merit did Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential Cistercian abbot</note> (and I to you, my Reader) emphasize that most true warning of Saint Jerome:
> No labor should seem hard, and no time should seem long, by which the glory of Eternity is acquired.

<margin>‡ Ecclesiasticus, chapter 41.</margin>
However small and brief the span of living may be when compared to eternity, none of the damned will be able to accuse God because He did not give a longer life; they will condemn themselves because they *did not live a better life*. ‡ *For in hell there is no complaining about life.* <note>original: "Non enim est in inferno accusatio vitæ" — meaning one cannot argue for more time once the judgment is passed.</note> Those destined for damnation would have lived long enough, if only they had lived piously enough.

At this point, my Christian friend, I would like to deal with you a little more freely and confidently, and place a matter before your eyes to be seen most clearly. You say that you think of heaven quite often, and that you aspire to Eternity with great vows. Do you say this? I, however, deny and flatly deny that you do so; if I were to affirm this about myself, I would bid you not to believe me. And how could it be, O my good Christian, that you and I should think of heaven often and seriously, and with such [great vows], as we—

# SIXTH SECTION

We claim to aspire to Eternity with our prayers, yet in the meantime, we live so sluggishly and coldly; so slow and broken when it comes to doing good, yet so lively and fervent toward evil, and so ready and willing for every kind of insolence. We are a complaining and yawning people, never less lazy than when there is an occasion for anger, and never more small-minded than when we must endure suffering. We are disturbed and cast down by any affliction or any little word; at a single remark, all our patience melts and flows away. We are never more prone to despair than when many things happen against our will. I shall remain silent regarding the other hidden sores <note>original: "vomicas," literally boils or abscesses</note> of lust and envy lurking in the soul. And yet, being such as we are—such "fine" men; so timid when we ought to be daring, and so bold when we ought to be afraid—we nevertheless boast that the joys of Eternity are often in our minds and in our prayers. Believe me, this is most difficult to believe: that we revolve Heaven and Eternity in our minds so frequently, and yet meanwhile live with no better morals? Pardon the expression, but this is impossible. And I shall now demonstrate this.

<margin>Genesis, chapter 29.</margin>
The Patriarch Jacob served his uncle Laban for seven years for his daughter Rachel, *and they seemed to him but a few days, because of the greatness of his love.* <note>Genesis 29:20</note> Do you hear this, you complaining man? You serve not a deceiver, such as Laban was, but God your creator and most faithful promisor. Nor do you serve for a mere wife, but for the whole kingdom of heaven; not for a woman’s beauty, but for the eternal sight of God.

# CONSIDERATION

...not for the sake of wifely charms, but for the sake of heavenly, eternal delights. And yet, the trouble of even one short day so weakens you that immediately all your love for heaven and for God begins to grow cold. As soon as adversity attacks, you quickly break out into the most profuse complaints; you cry out to heaven and earth, you breathe vengeance, and perhaps your grumbling does not always spare even divine justice itself. At other times, a coaxing pleasure so softens you that, having forgotten your divine service and having entered the labyrinth of sin—which appears pleasant from the front—you are dissolved into a lethal sleep. Look, is this your vigilance? Look, is this the heroic greatness of your love? Where is that arduous seven-year period <note>A reference to the seven years Jacob served for Rachel in Genesis 29.</note> spent in divine service? O Simon, Simon! <note>original: "O Simon, Simon!" — A reference to Christ's words to Peter in the Garden of Gethsemane (Matthew 26:40).</note> Can you not watch one hour with your Lord?

But hear more concerning the Patriarch Jacob. Having been deceived by Laban through the marriage of the bleary-eyed Leah, he served that same deceiver for another seven years for Rachel, who had been promised to him. There is no doubt that these seven years also seemed to him as but a few days, because of that same greatness of love. It is believable that, whenever he was fatigued from work, he turned his eyes toward the beauty of Rachel and said silently to himself: "This beauty is certainly worthy, even of a troublesome seven-year service; and if necessary, I will not refuse to serve this hard servitude for another seven years for her sake." Thus the impulse of love mitigated every sense of labor.

Do you grasp these things, you delicate soldier of Christ? <note>term: "soldier of Christ" (miles Christi) — a common metaphor for a Christian struggling against temptation.</note> And do you still murmur? You serve God, for God himself...

# SIXTH SECTION. 113

You are commanded to labor for eternal rest; you are called to endurance and patience for the sake of immortal blessedness—and yet, like a lazy sleeper, do you still complain? Count, I ask you, the years that you have spent entirely in the service of God; see if in twenty years you have served God as faithfully and industriously as Jacob served Laban. You will scarcely find as many months or days. Count the nights spent awake in prayer; review the days exhausted in pious labors. Could you say to God what Jacob said to his father-in-law:

> By day the heat consumed me, and the frost by night, and sleep fled from my eyes. Thus for twenty years I served you in your house: fourteen for your daughters, and six for your flocks. <note>A reference to Genesis 31:40-41, where Jacob defends his work ethic to Laban.</note>

Have you, my fellow Christian, served God in such a way for twenty years? You know what the reward for your labor will be; it is not the daughters of Laban, nor flocks of sheep. The prize for your service will be God Himself, the blessedness of soul and body. It will be well for you in every way; you will overflow with countless, greatest delights; without any lack, without any weariness, without end. You will swim as if immersed in the clearest pleasures. 

And yet, look at your hands, so sluggish for work; behold your feet, so slow to go to the temple; look into your heart, wasting away with envy, burning with anger and revenge, crawling with filthy thoughts, idle with sloth and impatience. Is this how you serve God for the sake of heaven, for immortal life, for eternal happiness? Why do you not do as Jacob did, and look toward the Rachel promised to you <note>Rachel represents the ultimate spiritual reward or the "contemplative life" in traditional commentary.</note> whenever the weariness of the labor you have undertaken creeps in?

Therefore, whenever you are in distress, and either adversity presses upon you or prosperity leads you astray, or when your labors become a burden, lift up your eyes to heaven and console yourself in this way: Behold, there is your Rachel <note>The author uses the biblical figure of Rachel (Genesis 29) as a metaphor for the ultimate reward or "beloved" that makes years of hard labor seem like only a few days.</note>, exceedingly beautiful and lovely! She is entirely fair, and there is no flaw in her. Behold heaven; behold the home of rest and eternal delight. Endure now for a little while—not the greatest pains, nor the heaviest labors—and shortly you will be there. You will be all the more joyful and blessed then, as you are now more sorrowful and afflicted. That rest will be more pleasant to you then, the more toilsome your life has been now. Therefore, both **Act bravely** and **Endure with constancy**; a blessed Eternity is worth everything. 

If you would encourage yourself more often in this way, my fellow Christian! If you would gaze up at heaven more frequently with such eyes; if you would think upon Eternity every day with this affection—believe me, the days of your servitude would seem few to you, *because of the greatness of your love for this Eternity*. You would call every labor easy, you would count all troubles as a grace, and you would reckon all adversity among your gains. Each person establishes this present life more holily, the more attentively they weigh the Eternity to come.

# CONSIDERATION
## THE SEVENTH.

->*How Christians may depict Eternity.*<-

One must walk step by step through a dark house, feeling along the walls, if the human intellect <term>intellectus: the power of the mind to understand</term> wishes to venture into eternity; it will find the path blocked by darkness and gloom, never to be fully penetrated in this life. The way to it is indeed short, but the end is endless. And although no man has ever conceived of Eternity in his mind so clearly that he could say plainly what it is, one may nevertheless set forth its immensity through similarities and pictures, as if by certain lines of shadow. It is utterly a shadow, and a shadow is whatever is either written or painted concerning Eternity; for not even all the ages of time heaped into one will exhaust its breadth: not hours, not days, not weeks, not months, not years; not five-year lustrums, not four-year Olympiads, not fifteen-year Indictions <note>original: "Indictiones"; a Roman chronological cycle used for taxation and dating</note>, not Jubilees, not centuries, not Platonic years <note>original: "anni Platonici"; the "Great Year" of roughly 25,800 years when the stars return to their original positions</note>, nor the slowest movements of the eighth sphere <note>original: "octavæ sphæræ"; in the old geocentric model, the sphere of the fixed stars</note>. Even if these were multiplied by any counting number, a thousandfold or a millionfold, or compared to any numbered thing—such as the stars of heaven, the grains of sand in the Ocean, the blades of grass on the earth, or the droplets of the rivers—the number of Eternity will still not be tracked down.

Sailors, having cast down a lead line <note>original: "bolide"; a sounding lead used to measure depth</note>, explore the depth of the sea; we likewise, with submissive and reverent thought, explore the impenetrable depth...

<summary>This page explores the impossibility of the human mind fully grasping the concept of Eternity, suggesting that all descriptions are merely "shadows" compared to its true, infinite nature. The author lists various units of time and astronomical cycles to demonstrate that even the longest durations cannot measure the eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, time, Platonic year, eighth sphere, infinity, metaphors for time, measurement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of eternity, moving from abstract concepts to a specific visual allegory used by Christians to illustrate the choice between worldly pleasure and eternal life.</meta>

# SEVENTH [SECTION]

Let us attempt to reach the depths of Eternity; and so that we may weigh this more effectively, the following image proposed by Christians will be of assistance. It is as follows:

The Christ-child, almost naked and unclothed as if just from the manger and cradle, stands among the clouds, carrying a small cross fitted to his shoulders; upon the clouds, this single word is seen inscribed: **Eternity**. Beneath the feet of Christ, on the ground, sits a skeletal corpse, stripped of hair and skin, recognizable only by its beard; in its left hand it holds a parchment on which is written: *That which delights is momentary*; in its right hand it holds up an apple <note>A traditional symbol of temptation and the Fall of Man</note>. Nearby is a raven, biting a snail with its beak. Its own cry is inscribed beside it: *Tomorrow, Tomorrow* <note>original: "Cras, Cras" - the raven's croak was often interpreted by Latin speakers as the word for "tomorrow," symbolizing procrastination in repentance</note>. From the gaping earth, a flame emerges upward, in which these words are inscribed: *That which torments is eternal*. Two people of different sexes, representing all of humanity, adore Christ as he emerges from the heavens with bended knees; behind them is a flowing hourglass, and a book is open with two faces: on one page is written*: *They spend their days in wealth, and in a moment they go down to hell.* <note>Job 21:13</note> † On the other: *Who shall deliver me from the body of this death?* <note>Romans 7:24</note> Nearby stand heavenly spirits <note>Angels</note> who guide the eyes of the onlookers with their hands, commanding them to raise their gaze toward the divine boy. This is the picture; its meaning is this:

### I. Christ Inviting.

Christ, the eternal Son of the eternal God, entered into this world with no other clothing than ours—that is, naked. The garment of immortality and innocence [was lost] through disobedience

<margin>* Job chapter 21. † Romans 7.</margin>

<summary>This page describes a complex allegorical engraving featuring the Christ-child and a skeleton to illustrate the contrast between fleeting earthly pleasures and the permanence of eternity. It begins to explain the symbolism of Christ's poverty and nakedness as a model for humanity.</summary>

<keywords>Eternity, Christ-child, allegory, memento mori, skeleton, raven, hourglass, Job, Romans, salvation, temptation</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on the suffering of Christ and the ascetic practices of the saints, specifically focusing on the rejection of physical comfort in sleep as a preparation for eternity.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

...we have lost. Now, alas! how poorly clothed we all come forth into this light; Christ suffered with us, or rather for us, for what He did not commit. But why is there a cross on the shoulders of this most divine Boy? <note>The author is likely reflecting on an image of the Christ Child carrying a cross, a common motif in Baroque devotional art.</note> It is the bed in which He fell asleep into death; instead of a bedroom He had Golgotha, instead of a pillow, thorns, and instead of a mattress, the cross. Many of the saints, led by this example, voluntarily chose to lie uncomfortably for several years and to sleep poorly and most sparingly, provided they might rise joyfully to the eternal day. 

Certainly, the Abbot Saint Bononius <note>original: "D. Bononius Abbas"</note> had the earth for a feather bed, haircloth for a blanket, and a rock for a pillow. Saint Lupus the Bishop, for twenty years, and Saint Edmund the Archbishop, for thirty years, slept without any bed at all. I shall remain silent regarding the many others—the Nicholases, Basils, Ulrichs—to whom it did not seem worthwhile to cherish a sleep of a few years with such luxuries. They had another and longer rest in mind; therefore, it did not please them to rest outside of heaven, neither to the point of satiety nor for pleasure. 

How many women also possessed this same spirit? Saint Clare placed a small wooden log under her head for a pillow. Saint Hedwig did not admit a straw mattress except as a luxury. Saint Bridget, from the death of her husband, for thirty years both constantly wore a hairshirt <term>hairshirt: a garment of rough animal hair worn against the skin as a penance</term> and never slept except on the ground. I omit others.

And why do we speak of the ancients? Even today, religious men sleep willingly and sweetly upon half-eaten straw, and do not seek feathers when [they hope for] blessed Eternity...

# SEVENTH

they hope for, than what they daily contemplate. But let us return to Christ; He encountered death, and that of the most bitter and shameful kind, but He did so in order to keep eternal death away from us. Indeed, we all die, but this death is very brief. In the blink of an eye, in a moment, the soul is torn from the body, and this entire event is what we call "To Die." Matters are not conducted this way in the lower regions <note>original: "inferos," referring to the underworld or hell</note>. Those torments far surpass all the anguishes and pains of death, not only because they are much heavier, but because they are incomparably longer-lasting, since they are eternal. Therefore, to suffer those things there is to die continually. And from this perpetual death, we were liberated by that Boy who proceeds, depicted in the clouds. Beneath the feet of this divine little Boy sits a decayed corpse, which can be recognized as being that of the first of all parents by several signs. Listen now, you descendants and late-born grandsons of Adam, to your parent speaking to you thus:

### *II. Adam Lamenting*

O Sons, you would have been happy if your parent had known how to make use of happiness: but now you are truly miserable by this very fact, that as my sons, I killed you before I fathered you; because of me, you were condemned before you were born. I wished to be God, yet I hardly remained a man: you all perished in me before you were able to perish <note>Adam suggests that his descendants fell into spiritual ruin through his sin before they could even commit their own</note>: thus I know not whether you call me father, or parricide and tyrant. Why do I wonder or complain that you are full of vice? You learned to be such from me. I grieve that you are disobedient, but nevertheless, I taught you to be so, being myself disobedient to God. The powers above turn away from you as intemperate and gluttonous;

<column-break/>

<summary>This page contrasts the momentary nature of physical death with the eternal agony of the soul, presenting a dramatic lamentation by Adam who takes responsibility for the fallen state of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Christ the Redeemer, eternal death, original sin, Adam's lament, mortality, salvation, disobedience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moral reflection on human frailty, transitioning here to a dramatic monologue from the perspective of Adam, the first man, lamenting his fall.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Ah, this is the vice of the fathers: your **pride** makes you hateful to God. I was the first of you to fall, triumphed over by this monster; and through my defeat, pride became even more arrogant. This is the inheritance you have from me—a heap of miseries. Heaven had been signed over to you by the will of the Great God, and bequeathed by a most certain promise. I disturbed everything, and squandered the inheritance of you all with a single morsel <note>original: "bolo unico," referring to the bite of the forbidden fruit</note>. I valued a wife and an apple more than all of you, more than heaven, more than God. Oh, what an evil and cursed meal, after which I had to dine among the inhabitants of the Underworld <note>original: "inferos"</note> for several thousand years!

I lived in a garden more pleasant than can be imagined or fashioned in the mind. There, I was permitted to enjoy everything the heart desired, if only I had turned away from a single tree. I was the king of all living things, wise and beautiful, strong and robust. I overflowed with innumerable delights; heaven favored the new spouses with steady mercy, and shining in its blue garment, it smiled continually. We saw nothing there but the purest suns; nothing met the eyes but what was lovely and blooming; the ears had their own musicians, the little birds. The earth breathed out saffron and cinnamon; on every side, an incredible pleasure surrounded me. I lived far from all care, weariness, fear, labor, disease, and death. I was a kind of God on earth; the heavenly beings <note>original: "cælites," the angels</note> themselves congratulated me on this happiness. I alone was the one who most begrudged it to myself: and because I did not obey the divine law, and bit into the forbidden evil, all evils rushed in upon me.

# SEVENTH <note>Likely the seventh section or chapter of the work.</note>

Driven out from Paradise, a fugitive from God and full of shame, I sought out hiding places. Labor, pain, grief, fear, tears, calamities, and a thousand miseries began to weary me; you feel them too, as many of you as belong to my family <note>Referring to the human race as the descendants of Adam.</note>. And what is believed to be the end of hardships—death—is often the beginning, and indeed the beginning of eternal ones. Oh my sons! Grow wise through my loss and yours, and learn at least this one thing from me: *To hate sin*. Behold the flame breaking out beside me; this began to burn at the same time as the sin, never to be extinguished for all eternity. All other punishments are to be considered light and end quickly; this one will torment the guilty forever. But we can still escape it, if only we will. For more than five thousand years <note>A common chronological estimate in the early modern period for the time between Creation and the life of Christ.</note> the gate of heaven was closed to me and all my children: now it has been unlocked by Him who—because I, a free man, wickedly snatched fruit from the tree—was Himself fastened to a tree <note>The "tree" of the Cross.</note>, paid the whole debt most generously, and *restored the matter to us all by dying*. Heaven now lies open to all, but only the way of repentance leads there, and only the gate of the cross admits us. Whoever comes to these joys by this way and through this gate comes securely, and is certain of an eternal dwelling. He shall be excluded from there no more; no one there can be harmed or cause harm. Oh posterity, think upon Eternity, for you will shortly be immortal, either among the damned or among the blessed. These are the warnings to posterity from Adam, who:

*The penalty he alone deserved, he distributed among all.*

# CONSIDERATION

When he could not be swayed in any way to grant his assent to the King's lust <note>A reference to King Henry VIII's desire to annul his marriage and break with the Catholic Church</note>, his wife was sent to him in prison, to see if she might perhaps soften his manly spirit with her prayers and tears. She began, therefore, to offer the most charming entreaties, and to beg him by all things sacred not to go and destroy himself, his children, his country, and a life which he might enjoy for many years to come. When she had reinforced her words with tears and urged him further regarding a longer life, More replied: "And for how long, my Alice <note>original: "Aloysia"</note>, will I be able to enjoy this life?" Then she said: "O my best husband, easily for twenty years." To which More replied: "Do you wish, then, that I should exchange eternity for twenty years? Truly, you are an unskilled merchant. If you had said twenty thousand years, you would indeed have said something, and you might seem to be insane with some reason; but what are these twenty thousand years themselves compared to eternity? *A small and brief thing, a point, a moment, nothing.* Therefore, I prefer to endure prison and whatever evils may conspire against me for my whole life, rather than suffer even the slightest loss of blessed eternity." Thus, unshaken, he withstood the object of Eternity and this battering ram. But let us now consider the black bird.

### III. The Croaking Raven.

Next to this corpse of the first-formed man <note>original: "protoplasti," referring to Adam</note> a raven is depicted, which contributes in no small way to representing Eternity in its entirety. The saying of Saint Augustine is well known: *"Tomorrow, tomorrow" is the voice of the raven. Moan like a dove and beat your breast.* <note>Augustine compares the raven's "cras, cras" (Latin for tomorrow) to the procrastinator who delays repentance</note> For almost this very reason, the blessed...

# SEVENTH SECTION

Many lose Eternity because they decide that they will seek tomorrow what ought to be sought today; for there is nothing we more readily cast off into an uncertain Tomorrow than repentance, which we promise repeatedly only to push it away from us under a respectable name.

<margin>* Persius, Satire 5.</margin>
> *“This will be done tomorrow,” you say. The next day, it is the same: “Tomorrow it shall be done.” What? Do you grant me a day as if it were a great gift? But when the next light comes, we have already consumed yesterday’s tomorrow. Behold, another tomorrow has driven these years away, and it will always be a little further beyond.* <note>original: "Cras hoc fiet idem..." Persius was a Roman Stoic satirist; here he mocks the habit of delaying moral reform.</note>

Thus, that "Tomorrow" lingers through such long-winded promises while the time of life is snatched away, and suddenly the wretched are tumbled down into Eternity—but one that is horrific and subterranean. It is this very thing, says Augustine, which kills many, when they say: "Tomorrow, Tomorrow," and suddenly the door is closed. Solomon likewise cries out:

<margin>‡ Ecclesiasticus 5.</margin>
> *Do not delay to be converted to the Lord, and do not put it off from day to day; for his wrath shall come suddenly, and in the time of vengeance he will destroy you.* <note>The author attributes this to Solomon, though it comes from the Book of Sirach/Ecclesiasticus, often associated with Solomonic wisdom in the 17th century.</note>

The Roman sage, Seneca, spoke truly: A great part of life slips away from those doing evil; the greatest part from those doing nothing; and the whole of it from those doing something else. And just as Archimedes, while Syracuse was being captured, was calmly drawing circles with his compass in the dust at his home: so most men, while their eternal salvation is at stake, busy themselves with their own "dust," distracted by vain disputes, financial business, and labors that will not profit them. They never think of Eternity, or only very rarely, and—

which they do only superficially, just as dogs drink from the Nile <note>A common ancient proverb referring to doing something in a hurried or distracted manner, based on the belief that dogs lapped water quickly from the Nile to avoid crocodiles.</note>. O Martha, Martha, how vainly you are troubled and distracted by so many things! *Furthermore, only one thing is necessary: Blessedness* <note>original: "Beatitudo"</note>, yet not this brief happiness on earth, but that Eternal happiness in heaven.

Before we undertake any labor, we often apply that well-known rule and ask ourselves: Is this work for the sake of earning bread? Should not a Christian, with even greater right at the beginning of any task, earnestly ask themselves: Is this for the sake of winning heaven? Does this lead toward meriting a blessed Eternity? But we cast such cares aside from one time to another, surely intending to ask this about our labor only when we are no longer able to work. But this is the way of all sinners, says Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, a foundational Christian theologian.</note>; every sinner has said: *at some point, but not right now.* Why not now, if indeed at some point?

Dionysius, the King of Sicily <note>Dionysius I, known as a tyrant of Syracuse.</note>, while stripping a golden cloak from the statue of Apollo, said: "This garment is suitable neither for summer nor winter; it is too heavy in the heat, and it does not provide warmth in the cold." In the same way, says Ambrose <note>St. Ambrose, the 4th-century Bishop of Milan.</note>, many play games with their own souls and with God. They say that a young man must live according to the laws of the world: he is trained for drinking cups and dances, for horses and the gymnasium; let him enjoy the sunshine and his companions. We leave the wilderness and the temples to gloomy old men. And when cold and sickly old age has crept up, they say: "These things should not be demanded of the elderly; their strength does not allow it." Rest must be granted to them; let them care for their health, and they have done enough. Thus the summer of our life

# SEVENTH.

The winter passes, and yet no work is done for the eternal spring to come. Therefore, while we have time, let us do good; let us not allow today, tomorrow, and eternity to slip away through that crow-like cry, *Tomorrow, Tomorrow* <note>original: "Cras, Cras" - the author likens the procrastinator to a crow, whose caw sounds like the Latin word for tomorrow</note>. Tomorrow is not ours; only today is.

<margin>* James 4:13-14</margin>
* *Behold now,* says the Apostle, *you who say: Today or tomorrow we will go into such a city, and there we will spend a year, and will traffic, and make our gain. Yet you do not know what shall be on the morrow. For what is your life? It is a vapor which appears for a little while, and afterwards shall vanish away.* <note>The author is quoting the Vulgate translation of the Epistle of James.</note> Messodamus <note>A figure from medieval exempla or moral stories</note> spoke rightly, as Guy of Bourges <note>Guido Bituricensis, a medieval chronicler or hagiographer</note> relates, when he was invited to a banquet the following day: "My friend," he said, "why do you invite me for tomorrow? For some years now, I have never dared to promise myself the following day, because I expect death at every hour." Certainly, no one guards against death sufficiently unless he guards against it always. That man is far too reckless, and a manifest despiser of Eternity, who goes to bed with a mortal sin <term>mortal sin: a grave sin that, according to theology, cuts the soul off from God's grace</term>, casting himself upon his feathers <note>i.e., his down bed</note> and breathing forth sleep from his whole chest. Ah! How great is both the blindness and the recklessness of the human mind? We know how frequent and common sudden death is; we have even known men who went to bed healthy and sound the day before, only to be found dead in the morning—dragged, perhaps, from their feathers into the flames (God knows!). We ourselves have even seen those who, struck by a sudden force, began to be sick and to die at the same time; within the fraction of an hour they were healthy, sick, living, and dead. And yet we still procrastinate, and from day to day (though this is a small thing) and from year to year, we delay our amendment—

# CONSIDERATION

While we delay the reformation of our life, death meanwhile arrives unexpected, and delivers us entirely to Eternity.

Manfully fighting against this hesitating slowness within himself, Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose 'Confessions' describe his struggle with delayed conversion.</note> said: "I felt that I was held by them, and I uttered wretched cries: How long, how long, tomorrow and tomorrow? Why not this very hour an end to my uncleanness?" I said these things and wept in the most bitter contrition of my heart.

A quote from Ephesians 4:26.</note> And he said that the sun ought not to set not only upon anger, but also upon all other sins, lest the Sun and Moon ever depart as witnesses to our crimes.

# SEVENTH [SECTION] 127

The meeting ended with the dispute still pending and unresolved. The Patrician <note>Nicetas, a high-ranking Byzantine official</note> thought it unfair to yield his financial rights, while the Patriarch <note>John the Almoner, Patriarch of Alexandria</note> believed he was acting for the cause of God. Nevertheless, after they had departed, the most upright Prelate, condemning himself for his own stubbornness, said to himself: "You should not think that this inflexible, stubborn anger pleases God, even in the best of causes." And now, as deep night was already approaching, he thought: "Shall we thus allow the sun to set upon our anger? This is the way of the ungodly, and against the precept of the Apostle." <note>A reference to Ephesians 4:26: "Let not the sun go down upon your wrath."</note>

The excellent Bishop could not rest, and as soon as possible he sent several high-ranking priests to Nicetas the Patrician, ordering them to deliver only this message: ***"Lord, the sun is at its setting."*** <note>original: "Domine, Sol ad occasum est."</note> These few words attacked the Patrician’s soul with such an unexpected assault and so thoroughly conquered all his persistence that, with swelling eyes, he could hardly keep his bursting tears in check. He did not delay; following immediately behind the departing clerics, he rushed to his Pontiff, John—as welcome as he was sudden—and immediately exclaimed: ***"Holy Father, from this moment I shall be in your power."***

With this said, they fell into each other's arms and entered into harmony together. Thus, this single saying restored the peace which a long skirmish of words could not: ***"The sun is at its setting."*** Truly, whoever is conscious of mortal sin should frequently revolve this same thought in his mind in the evening: ***"Lord, the sun tends toward its setting; and perhaps your life does as well."*** For if you should die tonight—which is neither a new nor a rare occurrence—

# CONSIDERATION

In which state do you think you will be in Eternity? Among the saints or the damned? Your conscience will tell you this; therefore, mind what you are doing. *The sun tends toward its setting* <note>original: "Sol ad occasum tendit"; a metaphor for the end of one's life</note>, beware lest it set upon your lust and luxury, upon your envy and blasphemy, upon your detraction and theft, or upon even a single mortal sin. Good God, if a garment, if a face, or if a hat is stained by a slight speck of dirt, we immediately wipe it away or wash it out; if we cannot tolerate a stain on a garment, a hat, or a face, why should we tolerate such foul blemishes on the soul for so long? Let that day, that very hour of sin, also be the hour of atonement. Penance must be performed, says Ambrose* <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397), an influential Bishop of Milan and Doctor of the Church</note>, not only solicitously, but also maturely, lest perhaps that head of the household from the Gospel <note>A reference to the Parable of the Barren Fig Tree in Luke 13:6-9</note>, who planted a fig tree in his vineyard, should come and, seeking fruit upon it and finding none, say to the dresser of the vineyard, *cut it down*. This final wound inflicted upon the tree is utterly incurable; therefore, while we are permitted, and while there is still time, let us apply a remedy. Even the animals teach us this: the stag, when pierced by an arrow, hastens to the dittany <note>term: dittany; a medicinal herb anciently believed to have the power to draw out weapons from wounds</note>, a herb well known to it. The swallow, if its chicks are blinded, knows how to give them sight again using its celandine <note>term: celandine; from the Greek 'chelidon' (swallow); a flower traditionally believed to heal the eyes of birds</note>. We, alas, miserable as we are, are wounded almost daily, and often mortally, and yet there is no care for medicine. We hasten to the table, to conversation, and to bed as we are accustomed: but who hastens to penance and confession? If we would listen to the warnings of our guardian Angel (for these too are represented standing by in this image of Eternity), we would never [rest] unless first recon-

# SEVENTH SECTION

When we, having been reconciled <note>original: "ciliati," likely a contraction of "reconciliati," referring to being at peace with God before sleep</note>, go away to our beds to sleep, this guardian of ours frequently warns us of the water-clock <term>clepsydra: an ancient device that measures time by the flow of water</term> running to its end; he warns of the judgment already looming. But we continue on the path we have begun; let the water-clock flow, let judgment loom, let the Underworld <note>original: "Orcus"</note> threaten, let the Angel warn, let death press in—we truly continue, and we lower our eyes into sweet sleep. O, wretched man, whoever you are!

<margin>Virgil, Aeneid, Book 4</margin>

-> Can you lead yourself into sleep under such a fall? <-

Can you carry a Conscience guilty of mortal sin to your bed? Can you, in such great danger of eternal death, admit slumber, the brother of death? "I can," you say, "I can; nor has anything evil ever happened to me." Do not be confident! In a single hour, that can happen which has not happened in a thousand hours. You have not yet escaped; and I implore you, consider by how great a distance your soul is separated from death, from the depths of hell, from eternity. Truly, with a single breath, you too shall say: "By only one step are I and death divided." There is no need for great preparation for death to lay you low, nor must the entire quiver of arrows be exhausted to pierce your heart; the point of a single little arrow will rob you of all life. 

Behold, a more malignant and corrupted breeze blows upon you, or some fluid sinks from the brain into the chest, or some little door <note>original: "ostiolum," referring to a valve or vessel</note> somewhere in the body is blocked, or the heat of the heart is suddenly suffocated, or the pulse of the artery is intercepted, and your whole living state collapses. You will marvel that you have been snatched away to eternity before you even feared you were to be snatched. There are a thousand ways, not only of slow death, but of sudden death. That death is the most unforeseen whose preceding life was negligent.

<summary>This passage serves as a memento mori, warning the reader that death is always imminent and that one should never go to sleep while in a state of mortal sin. It emphasizes the biological fragility of the human body and the suddenness with which eternity begins.</summary>
