# CONSIDERATION

It was not a prudent thing. An unprovided death is the worst death: therefore, it is healthily advised for every age that one should believe every day to be their last; indeed, one should think every hour to be the final one. How many people have been suddenly extinguished by a fall, a snare, poison, the sword, flames, waves, the claws of a lion, the tusks of boars, or the kicks of horses? There are a thousand causes of death; as many as there are, I will not say senses, but even pores in the body, so many are the windows for death, through which it may creep in and slit the throat. You were born, says Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a central theologian of Western Christianity</note>, and it is certain that you will die: and in this very fact that death is certain, the day of death is uncertain. None of us knows how near the end may be. *I know not,* says Job <margin>Job, chapter 32</margin>, *how long I shall remain, and whether after a little while my Maker may take me away.* In the midst of life we are in death <note>original: "media vita in morte sumus," a famous medieval Gregorian chant antiphon</note>, which we always carry around in our own bosom, and for whom is it proven that they shall live until the evening? For this thief of men—I mean Death—there are a thousand arts of harming: innumerable lightning bolts, thunders, Phrygian and Greek fires <note>referring to ancient incendiary weapons</note>, arrows, swords, siege-spears, scythes, and slings. There is no need for examples sought from the ancients, we have enough recent ones: have not many, not unknown to us, while they slept, fallen asleep into death itself, not to be awakened before the supreme day? Death does not always send messengers or heralds <term>Feciales: ancient Roman priests who acted as heralds to declare war or peace</term> to signify its arrival: most often it is present unexpectedly, and without any ceremonies it invades and prostrates the helpless man. Watch, therefore, because you know neither the day nor the hour. There is indeed in hell...

# SEVENTH

repentance, but it was not true, and it was far too late. Now is the time for a timely cry: ***Produce fruit worthy of repentance*** <note>original: "Facite fructum dignum pœnitentiâ"; a reference to Matthew 3:8</note>. The night comes when no one can work: work, therefore, while it is day. The "Day," says Origen †, is the span of this world; a day which indeed seems long to us, but if it be compared with Eternity, it is the shortest of moments. Yet, after this brevity of a day, there follows the infinite duration of Eternity. O my fellow man—you especially, who are conscious of a more grievous sin—contemplate the door of Eternity, and think upon death. It is most uncertain in what place death awaits you; you, therefore, should await death in every place. Just as the Lord finds you when He calls, so likewise shall He judge you.

\* *Matthew 3. John 9.* † *Origen, Homily 10 on Matthew.*

# CONSIDERATION

While in almost all matters, especially where a money-related case is involved, we wish to be certain, yet when it comes to eternal treasures, no certainty is sought, even though some assurance—or as they call it, <term>assecuratio</term> <note>original: "assecuratio"; a legal or commercial term for insurance or a formal guarantee</note>—can be obtained. Who lends money without demanding a written receipt or collateral in return? This is on everyone’s lips: "I want to be sure; I desire to be insured; I will take the safer path; I do not care to take a risk." Present and certain things everywhere outweigh those that are uncertain and far off. One sparrow in the hand is better than ten or twenty on the roof; a wren in a cage is dearer to us than an eagle in the clouds. Truly, we feel as Plautus did: our hands only believe when they have eyes. <note>A reference to the Roman playwright Plautus’s famous line "oculatae sunt nostrae manus, credunt quod vident"—our hands have eyes, they believe what they see.</note> Is it so, O mortals, that you seek certainty in the most uncertain things—which fail most often even when held in the hand—yet you care so little to be certain of the eternal path? Christians know what assurance or certainty the King of Heaven, Christ, has established here: *If you wish to enter into life, **keep the commandments.*** <margin>*Matthew 19. Luke chapter 18.*</margin> He who keeps them, enters. It is a most certain thing, a most secure way. But truly, he who sins against even one of these precepts, and moreover postpones repentance, nor reconciles himself to the offended God in the same hour in which he sinned—he commits himself and all his belongings, the eternal health of his body and soul, to open danger. He is but three fingers' breadth (if even that much) from death, while containing a thousand causes of death within himself; and yet, in that state of damnation, he rashly persists—

<summary>The author critiques the hypocrisy of people who demand legal and financial guarantees in trade but live recklessly without spiritual "insurance" by neglecting the commandments and delaying repentance.</summary>
<keywords>certainty, insurance, spiritual security, commandments, repentance, risk, Plautus, salvation</keywords>

# EIGHTH CONSIDERATION
<note>original: "OCTAVA"</note>

...remained, in which if he were to meet death, he would perish for all eternity. Is this not acting rashly, and committing eternal riches to a most dangerous gamble, which he could easily have avoided?

But if as many years had to be filled with the torments of the underworld <note>original: "inferorum," referring to the state of the damned</note> as the sins one had committed, or even as many hours as one had lived, how much more tolerable would that be? But in truth, if the enemies of God lived longer, if that fire lasted only for a fixed number of years—for many are not even terrified now, though they know those punishments will last forever and for infinite years, and that they are so great and so monstrous that a fraction of an hour spent in them seems like a year. For if all that a man can suffer in this life over a hundred years were fused into one mass, as it were, do you think it would equal one year to be spent among the damned? Assassins, robbers, parricides, and those guilty of any crimes whatsoever exhaust all the punishment due to them in three or four days: they are indeed tortured most dreadfully, but within a few days all this torture stops, and is finished within the span of a single week. But truly, the torments of the damned are not confined to one year, nor to one or two centuries: God punishes them always, and never punishes them enough, even though He punishes them for all Eternity.

# CONSIDERATION
## EIGHTH.

### *How Eternity should not be so much painted by Christians, as it should be meditated upon.*

Order requires that, following the example of the Royal Psalmist <note>original: "psalte regio," referring to King David</note> and others who meditate on eternity, we should descend into our very selves, dwell with ourselves, and at least occasionally be at home within our own minds. He wanders too far from himself and his own salvation who sees only that which passes away, and forgets those things which are eternal.

Legal experts <note>original: "jurisconsulti"</note> know well that a legal right involving only three farthings <note>original: "obolis," a small Greek coin of negligible value</note> is not to be abandoned if it concerns a perpetual and eternal tax. A great price is considered to reside in a mere three quadrants <note>original: "quadrantibus," the smallest Roman brass coin</note> if they must be paid to their lord every single year; such is the value of perpetuity, even in the cheapest of things. But if you pursue a legal claim for three farthings, my friend, how is it that you are so sluggish and yawning in the pursuit of the inheritance of the eternal kingdom? You wage war against another over three farthings and initiate a long-winded lawsuit: yet you allow heaven to be snatched away by others. Surely heaven is a cheap thing; at least it is to you, who labor so little for heaven while seeking all other things with such great ambition, effort, and sweat. Regarding eternal matters—as they seem to be far off—you scarcely have the leisure to think, and when you do have leisure, you have no desire. It seems a tedious thing to weary the eye of the mind on those things which cannot be fully seen. Things that are near and present are more pleasing.

In this matter, if we are wise, we will marvel at our own blindness, or more truly, our insanity.

<summary>The author argues that humans foolishly prioritize trivial, temporary legal disputes over the infinite value of their eternal salvation. He uses the metaphor of a perpetual tax on small coins to show that even small things become valuable when they last forever.</summary>

<keywords>eternity, meditation, spiritual blindness, legal metaphors, salvation, King David, heavenly inheritance</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological reflection on the nature of hope and despair, likely following a discussion on the trials of the soul.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Nothing is more effective than hope. Divine gentleness usually leaves some hope of salvation remaining even in the midst of adversity. While there is life in a sick man, there is hope. After this life, however, this final consolation in the face of misfortune is utterly taken away from the damned: once hope is removed, only eternal despair remains. The Prophet Daniel saw and heard an angel crying out: *Cut down the tree, and lop off its branches: shake off its leaves, and scatter its fruit: nevertheless leave the stump of its roots in the earth.* <note>Daniel 4:14-15. The author uses the story of Nebuchadnezzar's dream to illustrate that even in punishment, God leaves a "root" of hope.</note> Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), an influential Bishop of Milan.</note> says that he strikes down the flower and the leaves but preserves the root; here, delights are taken away and punishments are inflicted, yet hope is not removed: behold, the root is preserved, hope remains. Among those in the lower world <note>original: "inferos" — referring to the state of the damned or hell.</note>, it is torn out by the roots and the stem. *It shall leave them neither shoot nor root,* cries Malachi†. Job also, in his grieving‡, says: *And he has stripped away my hope like a tree pulled up.* The hope of the wicked shall perish. Therefore, while we are able, let us hope, but let us also hope for that which we ought. Ovid <note>The Roman poet Publius Ovidius Naso.</note> once warned:

> *All human affairs hang by a slender thread,*
> *And by a sudden fall, those things which were strong come to ruin.*

Therefore, one must not trust in these things, nor place hope in them. Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and mystic.</note> shows a better way with these words*: Faith says, great and unthinkable goods have been prepared by God for his faithful; Hope says, those things are being kept for me; and now the third, Charity <note>term: Charity — from the Latin "caritas," meaning divine or selfless love.</note>, says: I run toward them. True hope, as Saint Gregory affirms, lifts the soul toward eternity,

# EIGHTH

And for that reason, one feels no outward evils among those they endure. True hope knows well that all things are but a small matter and a mere moment. But *O moment*, upon which all *Eternity* hangs! The hour of death and the day of the final struggle is properly that "moment," and that "precious pearl" <note>A reference to the Parable of the Pearl in Matthew 13:45-46.</note> for which the wise merchant sells all things so that he may buy it; yet few know the price of this union. *Saint Jerome* <note>original: "D. Hieronymus"</note> affirms this, saying: "Regarding eternal salvation, every man is altogether too negligent." But from where does this neglect in a matter of the highest importance arise? We wretches have eyes that are weak and diseased. We see well enough at close range, but at a distance, we see scarcely anything. I shall not speak of adults and the elderly: even boys and girls fresh from their cradles, whose teeth have not yet all grown in, receive the first elements of vice and learn by heart the smallest filth of greed. Soon they are taught an insatiable desire for acquiring; thus they know what makes for profit, what fills the purse, and what stocks the larder. They can speak of merchandise, judge wines, and dictate the foreign fashions of clothing. Truly, as *Juvenal* says in his *14th Satire*:

> *This is what the old women show to boys begging for a penny,*
> *This is what all girls learn before their ABCs:* <note>original: "ante alpha & beta"</note>
> *From this source come almost all causes of crimes ——————*

From this also comes such great ignorance or forgetfulness of eternal things; the young and the old alike understand the price of a coin well enough, but they are ignorant of heaven and of Eternity. *But let us proceed further.*

# II. *Eternity is a sea, it is a three-headed hydra, but it is also a fountain of all joy.*

It is asked of you especially, my fellow Christian—you who are often present at the sermon, but perhaps rarely attentive: If you were to stand by the sea and resolve to drain it with a tiny spoon into a nearby stream running beside you, which itself nevertheless flows back into the sea, so that everything you had taken from the sea was swallowed up by the sea again: tell me now, how long will you labor in draining that ocean? But even if you were allowed to draw it out with a large barrel and pour it into a river diverted elsewhere, how many years would you have to draw water in this manner before you could transfer the sea into another channel? To sit in flames during that time would be an unspeakable torment; yet for the damned, this would be a most welcome bargain, and they would call the delay "not long," provided it would finally end so that they might escape Eternity.

The ancients fashioned a story of a certain three-headed <term>hydra</term><note>A mythical water serpent that grew back two heads for every one cut off.</note>, which, when one head was cut off, soon another and another would grow in its place. This hydra is truly in the lower regions, if it is anywhere at all; for there, eternity is three-headed, presenting its triple heads as the <term>punishment of loss</term><note>Latin: pœna damni; the spiritual pain of being eternally separated from the sight of God.</note>, the <term>punishment of the senses</term><note>Latin: pœna sensus; the physical torment of the fire.</note>, and the <term>worm of Conscience</term> that shall never die. Alas, how wretched we are and how heedless of the greatest matters! We travel a path as short as it is dangerous, yet we are so cheerful and insolently joyful upon it, just as if we were strolling through paradise or the most pleasant valleys, secure from enemies, and about to be placed safely in our fatherland. Yet we cannot remain ignorant without being at fault.

<summary>The author uses the metaphors of draining an ocean with a spoon and the three-headed hydra to illustrate the terrifying, inexhaustible nature of eternal punishment. He warns the reader against spiritual complacency during the short journey of earthly life.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Hell, Hydra, Punishment of Loss, Punishment of Senses, Conscience, Repentance, Metaphor of the Sea</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the meditation on the nature of Eternity, specifically focusing on the two paths available to the human soul at the end of life's journey.</meta>

# EIGHTH

...that we shall finally arrive at the twin gates of a double Eternity, one of which belongs to the blessed, the other to the damned; we must enter through one or the other of these, depending, of course, on how we have conducted ourselves along the way.

Lawrence Justinian <note>Saint Lawrence Justinian (1381–1455), the first Patriarch of Venice, known for his ascetical writings.</note>, marvelling at this cheerful madness of travelers, said: *“O lamentable condition of mortals, which, though it is exiled from its fatherland, exults upon the road!”* Let us defer these most vain joys for a little while until we reach our fatherland, and let us do this one thing: strive to be admitted through that gate which is the beginning of eternal happiness. God indeed created us for delights rather than for labors, but here is neither the place nor the time for delights, but for labors. God has assigned Eternity for delights, but he granted the span of this life for the sake of earning those delights. 

And by what service, you ask, shall I earn them? Do you know the Divine Oracle? <note>original: "Oraculum"; referring to the words of Scripture.</note> *The kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent take it by force.* <note>A reference to Matthew 11:12.</note> Think to yourself: Am I that "violent" one? Certainly I am—when it comes to down feathers <note>Symbolizing luxury and sleep.</note>, to drinking cups, to dances, and to pleasures. 

Ah! We must fight nobly and run strenuously while we live and thrive, so that in the final moment of life—on the very horizon of Eternity, which shall separate us forever from this life and transfer us into the other, which belongs to the whole of Eternity—we may be able to rejoice over the life we have lived and have the highest hope for the future. Let us labor, therefore, let us labor and do violence to ourselves; thus we shall attain that everlasting rest hidden within time, and that eternal glory concealed beneath these few days.

<summary>The author reflects on the two gates of eternity, urging the reader to forgo worldly pleasures and "do violence" to their own vices to earn a place in heaven. Using the words of Lawrence Justinian, the text contrasts the temporary hardships of life with the permanent joys of the afterlife.</summary>

<keywords>Eternity, Lawrence Justinian, salvation, spiritual struggle, Kingdom of Heaven, afterlife, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral reflections on the fleeting nature of worldly pleasure versus the permanence of eternity, transitioning from biblical allegory to a hagiographic example.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* Jonah 4. † Job 20.</margin>
True and solid joy is not to be sought in these vain little delights, but in eternal pleasures. An ivy plant grew up over the head of Jonah, that it might be a shadow over his head. * *And Jonah rejoiced over the ivy with great joy.* <note>original: "Et lætatus est Ionas super hedera, lætitia magna." Referring to Jonah 4:6.</note> And what, after all, is all the pleasure or vanity of this world? Is it not a green ivy, which creeps along the wall in a woven forest and spreads a joyful shadow over the head? The wealthy rejoice with great joy under the shadow of their ivy—their riches; the gluttonous and the drunkards rejoice under the shadow of their tables; the sensual under the shadow of their delights. But at the end of joy, grief takes hold. God prepared a worm, and it struck the ivy, and it withered. Where now, my Jonah, is your ivy, and where is your joy? Both that and this have withered. In exactly this way our ivies wither, and along with them all that shadowy joy. For the joy of the hypocrite is like a mere point <note>meaning a tiny, fleeting moment in time</note>; † the eternal joy of the good is eternal.

---

### III. *How great the taste of eternity is, declared by an illustrious example.*

<margin>‡ Life of St. Pachomius ch. 29.</margin>
Theodore ‡ saw this, and saw it early; a youth in years, but certainly an old man in judgment, born of Christian parents. On a day of festival for all Egypt, when laughter, loud guffaws, and dances resounded in the very spacious house of his parents, and when the genial tables invited them to feasting, he himself, groaning with a hidden but chaste wound <note>a metaphor for spiritual longing or conviction</note>, thus expostulated with himself—

### EIGHTH SECTION

He began to ask himself:
> Unhappy Theodore! And what gain is there for you, if you should gain the whole world <note>original: "si mundum lucreris universum"; a reference to Matthew 16:26</note>; you have many things, but how long will you have them? You have abundance, you feast, you dance: and how long will all these things last? Indeed, this way of living would be pleasing and wise, if only it could be pleasing and wise for a long time. But what then? Should I keep these delights and lose the eternal ones? Tell me, Theodore, is this the way of the Christian law, to fashion a heaven for oneself on earth, and to wish to pass from delights to delights? Either I am greatly deceived, or Christ said we must go through thorns. Break away, and prefer eternal joys to brief ones.

He spoke, and withdrawing into the inner part of the house with weeping eyes, prostrating himself there alone upon the ground, he began to pray thus:
> Eternal God, my heart lies open to you; my groan is my advocate before you. What I should ask of you, or in what manner, I myself hardly know: this one thing, O my God, I especially pray, that you do not let me die the eternal death. Lord, you know that I love you, and I desire to be with you, and to praise you forever: Lord, have mercy.

While he prayed these things, his mother arrived; as a curious observer, she immediately noticed his reddish and wet eyes, and said:
> From where, my son, does this sorrow come? And why this solitude of yours on this day? The table calls you now; you alone are missing.

Theodore replied to her:
> I beg you, mother, clear my absence from the table with some honorable excuse: you yourself would not advise thrusting food or drink upon a nauseated stomach.

Thus, with a merciful deception, he removed his mother from his presence; he himself remained alone with God alone, deeper into the thoughts of etern—

<meta>The catchword 'mnium' from the previous page completes the word 'omnium' (of all things), leading into the discussion of the inner sanctuary of the soul.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Having entered the innermost sanctuary of his soul, he began to examine the entire state of his life with attentive <term>thought<note>original: 'cogitatio'—referring here to a formal meditative practice</note></term>; and he said to himself: "Who have I been until now? Or rather, who do I wish to be hereafter, if indeed I do not wish to be cut off from the kingdom of Eternity? The way to heaven is varied; one person goes this way, another that way. As long as one arrives, what does it matter by which path? Yet, since not all ways are alike, and we ourselves are also different, each person must choose the one he knows is suitable for himself. One path is shorter, another longer and more dangerous. If I should fear the long and dangerous one, there remains the short and secure path; if I choose it, the angels will undoubtedly rejoice with me. But will my kin be saddened? At first, perhaps, but later they too will likely rejoice. Come now, Theodore, only do not delay for long, and do not yield too much. I truly hope that I shall be brave when I encounter brave adversaries. But what if I am to face those who are soft, flattering, and weeping? It is here that I fear more for myself. But steady your soul, and ask Christ for a spirit that is unbroken, to replace the flexible one you have by nature. But what if your mother should show you her breasts <note>A classical and hagiographic trope referring to a mother's emotional appeal to her son to stay home</note>? You know what Saint Jerome says: 'Fly to the standard of the cross; it is a virtue to be cruel in this matter.' <note>original: 'Ad vexillum crucis evola, virtus est in hac re esse crudelem'—Jerome's famous exhortation to abandon family for the ascetic life</note> You have another and greater mother, who likewise shows her own, and she is your patroness, the Most Blessed Virgin, who hereafter will be to you like a thousand mothers, if you will be her son. But this change of life is a difficult thing, especially in the very flower of youth. It is difficult, I admit. But experience speaks: most people serve God poorly for the very reason that they serve Him too late." Most rightly...

### EIGHTH

Those who have learned to bear the yoke of the Lord from their youth <note>original: "jugum portare didicerunt"; a reference to Lamentations 3:27 regarding early discipline</note> find it easiest of all. But, I have been nourished delicately until now; must I now enter upon a harsh life? Will I be able to endure it? I hope so. But for how long? For one year or two? That will not be enough; I must stretch further, even to the end of life. Consider this carefully, therefore: either do not begin, or finish the task. I think that I shall not be alone in overcoming these things, but rather that I shall be with God as my companion. That is indeed true, yet it is most difficult to strive against what one is accustomed to. Until now I have led a noble and free life; shall I now lead one that is humble and servile? How long shall I lead it? One must not imagine a brief comedy <note>The author uses "comedy" in the classical sense of a stage play with a happy ending, contrasting it with the gravity of his soul's salvation.</note> here, as if I were to sustain this persona for only a day or two; here the final act is performed, since it concerns the soul. The burden of this theater will be long, and it is not permitted to return to silk or purple <note>Symbols of wealth and high social status.</note> except when you are clothed in immortality and the garment of glory. 

O Theodore, what will you do on this stage of such long duration? I shall look to the other actors; among the rest, I see Christ the Son of God—undistinguished, humble, and simple—suffering and performing all manner of servile tasks. What must I do? Am I any better than He? Yet, in truth, it is hard to reduce oneself to these straits and to enter, as it were, into a certain prison. See, your will hereafter shall belong to another, and you shall do what another wishes. Here too I have Christ to follow, who came not to do His own will, but the will of the Father who is in heaven. Nor shall it be difficult for me to follow another’s lead, if I keep my eyes on the one leading. Who is it that commands? A man brings forth the voice, but God gives the order; if I obe- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "obe-" suggests "obediens" (obedient) or "obediam" (I shall obey).</note>

<summary>Theodore reflects on the difficulty of abandoning a life of luxury for one of religious service, concluding that he must follow the humble example of Christ's obedience. He frames the spiritual life as a lifelong theatrical performance where the final stake is the soul itself.</summary>
<keywords>Theodore, spiritual discipline, imitation of Christ, asceticism, divine will, theater metaphor, humility</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the initial internal struggle of the subject, Theodorus, as he contemplated a life of devotion versus his current status. The catchword "firman-" suggests the previous page ended with a word like "firmamentum" or "firmandis."</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

I should not promise obedience.
But truly, this philosophy is far too deep: to pass from a wealthy life to that of a pauper. What will you do? You, who were able to give to beggars, will be forced to beg yourself. Do not seek the luxuries which you will no longer possess.

Why do I struggle with myself for so long? Why do I toss about in this <term>Euripus</term> <note>original: "Euripo"; a reference to the Strait of Euripus, famous in antiquity for its violent and unpredictable currents, used here as a metaphor for mental agitation</note> of hope and fear? Do I not see the example of my Lord before me? Is Christ not before my eyes, fixed to the cross, mocked, having suffered unspeakable things? He, having left behind his heavenly treasures, came into this impoverished land. What did he possess? His birth and his death show us: in the former, he had no place to lodge; in the latter, he had nothing to cover himself. He died naked. What was his life like? Spent in flight, in travel, in thirst, and in heat. How tireless he was in acting, how patient in enduring! What he taught, he also did. Who was so devoted to poverty, so ready for labor, so gentle toward insults? I should be ashamed of such a leader, I should blush to be counted among his companions, if I, *Theodorus*, will not be such a man as my Lord *Jesus* wishes me to be. For love of Him, I am prepared to be cold, to hunger, and to thirst; I am prepared to be despised, bound, burned, and cut! These things are brief, nor can they be long-lasting. Only the joys or the torments of *Eternity* are truly enduring. Go away, you fleeting things! I care not a straw for you; *Eternity* alone is my desire.

He grew so heated with this surge of thoughts that he now burned for *Eternity* in heaven alone. And so he decreed to say a long and eternal *Farewell* to money, to parents, and to luxuries.

<summary>Theodorus undergoes a spiritual crisis, ultimately deciding to renounce his wealth and family to follow the impoverished and suffering example of Christ in pursuit of eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>Theodorus, Jesus Christ, poverty, renunciation, eternity, spiritual struggle, asceticism</keywords>

# EIGHTH SECTION

In confirming this decree of his, he acted not so much with haste as with constancy; he did not immediately become the man he desired to be, but at last he succeeded—that is, he became a disciple of Pachomius <note>Saint Pachomius (c. 292–348 AD) was the founder of Christian cenobitic monasticism, which emphasizes organized community life for monks.</note>. More than one tragedy followed this prologue, but contrary to the usual custom of tragedy, a joyful ending followed a sorrowful beginning. Thus Theodore had drunk in Eternity into his very marrow, so that he did not refuse to become a laughingstock to the world, provided he did not believe, along with the world, that Eternity itself was a mere fable.

Do you wish, my fellow Christians, to hear a true and serious word from Theodore—indeed, from the great Paul, or rather from Christ himself? Most of you live as if all Eternity were a fable. *Wide and spacious is the way that leads to destruction, and many are those who enter through it* <note>A reference to Matthew 7:13.</note>: certainly they would not enter through this way if they believed that they would remain forever in the place where they entered. Thus, to you, Eternity is a pious fable. "But we do believe in Eternity," you will say, "we hope for it and desire it." I ask you, how small is this hope or faith, how cold is this desire? Present pleasure, "money in the hand," and the allurements of the flesh so ingeniously deceive many that they gradually extinguish all love of Eternity in a sweet forgetfulness. Indeed, it is shouted a thousand times: *Thus says the Lord, thus God commands* <note>original: "Haec dicit dominus, haec praecipit Deus"</note>; a thousand times this is heard, but a thousand times it is neglected. Let the Lord speak, let God command, yet this way pleases us; <margin>* Jeremiah chapter 18, verse 12.</margin> *for we will follow our own thoughts, and each of us will act according to the wickedness of his own evil heart.* Therefore these...

<summary>The author uses the example of Saint Theodore's devotion to Pachomius to rebuke Christians who live for worldly pleasure while treating the concept of Eternity as a mere story or "pious fable."</summary>

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* Proverbs, chapter 1.</margin>
The Lord says: <note>original: "Interrogate gentes, quis audivit talia horribilia?"</note> *Ask the nations, who has heard of such horrible things?* If those peoples who live without God had known these secrets of Eternity, would they have despised them so? It is well, my fellow mortals; I have cried out, and you did not listen; I have called, and you refused; I stretched out my hands, and you did not look. I also shall laugh at your destruction when sudden calamity rushes upon you, and ruin falls like a storm; when the whole of Eternity overwhelms you. If death strikes you in this state, the sentence is already passed, the door is already closed: depart, eternally miserable, eternally damned.

<margin>‡ Psalm 89.</margin>
Wake up, therefore, Christians, wake up! The judge stands at the door: in a single moment, that which all eternity shall grieve will be done. The great Anthony <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356 AD), a prominent Desert Father and monk.</note> said in a certain sermon to his followers: "Here, my brothers, the prices of things are equal in buying and selling. You give me ten gold pieces, and I give you spices of the same value. I count out fifty florins <note>term: florin (a gold or silver coin used in Europe)</note> to you, and you weigh out the same amount of grain for me. But the calculation is far different in obtaining those things which are eternal. Eternal life stands at an utterly cheap price: a single penny <note>original: "obolus," a small Greek coin</note> can be more justly compared with a whole million in gold than all our labor can be compared with heavenly blessedness." ‡ It is read in the Psalms: *The days of our life are seventy years, or if there is much strength, eighty; whatever remains is labor and sorrow.* Therefore, if we serve God faithfully and fervently for eighty, or at most a hundred years, we shall obtain a kingdom of all ages, and not

# EIGHTH <note>Refers to the eighth section or chapter of the treatise.</note>

<margin>* In the Life of Saint Anthony, chapter 15.</margin>
...we shall reign for only a hundred years: we shall inherit heaven, not the earth. Therefore, little children, let neither weariness exhaust you, nor the ambition of vain glory delight you. For the sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the future glory that shall be revealed in us <note>A reference to Romans 8:18</note>. Let no one, when he has forsaken the world, think that he has left behind great things; for all the spaces of the earth, compared to the infinity of the heavens, are brief and narrow. And even if we should try to retain our riches, we are torn from them against our will by the law of death. Why then do we not make a virtue out of necessity? Why do we not willingly let go of things that must soon be lost as if they were trifles, so that we may receive the greatest rewards? Athanasius <note>Saint Athanasius of Alexandria, the biographer of St. Anthony the Great</note> narrates these things at greater length.

* It was most customary and constant for Saint Pachomius <note>The founder of Christian cenobitic, or communal, monasticism</note> that, as often as an illicit thought tickled his mind, he would repel it with the memory of Eternity. Against a recurring temptation, he would oppose eternal punishments: pains burning without end, unquenchable fire, and the worm that never dies <note>A traditional image of the pangs of conscience and hellfire</note>. Therefore, let Saint Pachomius himself finish this consideration with his own words: "Above all things," he says, "let us have the final day before our eyes, and at every moment let us fear the torments of Eternal sorrows."

<summary>This page cites the teachings of Saint Anthony and Saint Pachomius to emphasize that earthly sacrifices are insignificant compared to the infinite glory of heaven and the necessity of meditating on eternity to resist temptation.</summary>

# CONSIDERATION
## NINE.

*Several conclusions drawn from the sayings concerning Eternity.*

### I. Conclusion.

<margin>* John, chapter 15.</margin>
No man is able to express in words, nor even sufficiently grasp in thought, the infinite length of Eternity. Between a real man and a sculpted one, or between a real fire and a painted one, there is a vast difference, and yet even these are said to be very similar in their own way. But between our fire and the fire of <term>Tartarus</term><note>A classical term for the deepest abyss of Hell, used here to emphasize the severity of divine punishment.</note>, between our pains and the pains of the damned, there is no comparison—none at all. For the measure of our pain is Time, but the measure of theirs is Eternity. Christ expressed this elegantly using the metaphor of the vine branch:
> *If anyone,* He said, *does not remain in me, he is cast out like a branch and withers; and they gather them, and cast them into the fire. And it burns.* <note>original: "Et ardet." The author emphasizes the present tense of the verb to show the unending nature of the fire.</note>

Here, Eternity is described most briefly—indeed, with a single word—and most perfectly. All the other words of Christ carry the distinction of the future tense: "he will wither," "they will gather," "they will cast." He does not say "it will burn," but rather, *It burns*. This will be the state of the damned man: a thousand years will pass, *And it burns*; again, a thousand years will pass, *And it burns*, just as it did in the beginning. Again and again, thousands of years will pass by, *And it burns*, just as it first began. If, after some millions of years, one were to ask: that man who was damned so many thousands of years ago, what is he doing? What is happening to him? No other answer could be given than this very thing: *It burns*, with continuous, unspeakable heat—

<summary>This page presents the ninth consideration on Eternity, arguing that the eternal fires of Hell cannot be compared to earthly fire because they exist outside of time. The author uses a passage from the Gospel of John to emphasize that the punishment of the damned is a perpetual present that never ends.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Christ, Gospel of John, Hellfire, Damnation, Time vs. Eternity, Metaphor of the Vine</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>The catchword "Hic" indicates the beginning of the next section. This page continues a series of spiritual reflections or "Considerations" regarding the state of the soul and the gravity of sin.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* Tractate 81 on the Gospel of John.</margin>
...eternal things: so it shall be from age to age. On this point, Augustine* beautifully writes: One of two things is appropriate for the vine-branch, either the vine or the fire: if it is not in the vine, it will be in the fire; therefore, so that it may not be in the fire, let it be in the vine. <note>original: "Unum de duobus palmiti congruit, aut vitis aut ignis..." Augustine uses this metaphor to explain that a Christian must remain connected to Christ (the vine) or face judgment.</note>

->II. Conclusion.<-

<margin>‡ Matthew 16. Syrus the priest in his life [of the saint], to Rudolph, Abbot of Vallis Umbrosa.</margin>
If those who are in a state of mortal sin <note>A grave sin committed with full knowledge and consent, believed to separate the soul from God's grace.</note> knew how close they were to Eternity and to eternal torments—since they could, by a single nod of God (so to speak), be handed over to death, and by death to the devil—they would not wish for the entire kingdom of the Spains, all the treasures of Asia, or the whole world to be given to them on the condition that they should delay confession and repentance for even a single hour, much less that they should go to bed while burdened by such sin. ‡ For what does it profit a man if he gains the whole world, but suffers the loss of his own soul? <note>A reference to Matthew 16:26.</note> If you lose all things, O man, remember to save your soul. Arialdus, a deacon of Milan, lost almost all his limbs; he was a man of the highest nobility by birth, for he was the brother of a Marquess, but he was also most holy in his life. Because he most constantly resisted heretics and other shameful men, he was finally led to his death: his ears were cut off, his upper lips mutilated, his nose severed, his tongue torn out from his throat, his eyes gouged out, and his right hand amputated—I remain silent regarding the rest. This Saint preserved his soul in a body that was truly and utterly ruined; and so that he might acquire life, he lost his life, in the year 1066 from the Virgin's child-bearing. <note>Arialdus (Saint Arialdo) was a leader of the Pataria movement in Milan who sought to reform the clergy.</note>

<summary>This page reflects on the necessity of remaining connected to Christ to avoid eternal fire and uses the gruesome martyrdom of Saint Arialdus of Milan to illustrate that the soul's salvation is worth more than any worldly treasure or physical safety.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Augustine, Saint Arialdus of Milan, mortal sin, salvation, martyrdom, eternity, repentance, 11th century history</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral reflections on eternity, following the previous discussion on the soul's preparation for the afterlife.</meta>

-># NINTH SECTION<-
<note>original: "NONA"</note>

This noble champion <term>athlete<note>The author uses the Latin 'athleta' to describe a spiritual warrior or one who struggles for virtue.</note></term>, mindful of his own Eternity (as Seneca \* speaks), advances into all that has been and all that is to come through every age. Most excellently did Augustine say: "What then shall we do, Fathers, except that while there is time, we change our lives and correct our deeds, if there be any to correct? So that what will undoubtedly come upon sinners may not find us among those it falls upon—not because we will not exist, but so that it does not find us to be the kind of people for whom such a fate was predicted. Everything we have heard through the scriptures is the voice of God saying, *Observe*. And everything we suffer—the tribulations in this life—is the whip of a God who wishes to correct us, lest He condemn us at the end. These things seem harsh, they are troublesome, they cause a shudder when they are told; yet whatever heavy things anyone suffers in this life, in comparison to the eternal fire, are not merely small, but nothing at all."

### *III. Conclusion.*

A great portion of Christians, even Catholics, do not believe in the Eternity of hell <term>Gehenna<note>A term for the place of fiery punishment.</note></term> and heaven; certainly they would live differently if they truly believed it. Regarding these people, this verse is fitting: ‡ *When the Son of Man comes, do you think he will find faith on earth?* There are those who wish to be seen believing it: they say so with words, but deny it with their deeds. They think of Eternity either never or very rarely; and they only think of it superficially—they do not weigh it, they do not cling to it, they do not examine it, and they do not stir their intellect and will, or their imagi—

---

<margin>\* Seneca, *Consolation to Helvia*, at the end. Augustine, volume 10, sermon 109, *On Time*. ‡ Luke 18.</margin>

<summary>The author cites Seneca and St. Augustine to urge readers to reform their lives through the contemplation of eternity, warning that many profess faith in the afterlife but fail to act upon it.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, St. Augustine, Seneca, repentance, tribulations, hell, faith, spiritual discipline</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the lack of meditation regarding eternity, following the previous page's warning about spiritual blindness.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

They impress nothing upon their prayers: they scarcely begin, and soon wander elsewhere; and if perhaps any good spark is born from this thought, they immediately suffocate it all with cares, business, and pleasures, as if with water. Thus, many enter the path of Eternity toward death with closed eyes and stopped ears; this is what the Holy Fathers <note>The "Holy Fathers" refers to the early influential theologians of the Christian Church.</note> observe in the Gospel's rich man <note>original: "Euangelico epulone"; a reference to the parable of Dives and Lazarus in Luke 16.</note>, that he first raised his eyes only when he was in torments: throughout his whole life he had his eyes closed, especially to the poor, but also to all things of piety; he first opened them in the underworld, but too late. Nor is it anything to wonder at, that many who are so blind hasten with hands and feet into the house of eternal mourning: the way is quite spacious, pleasant, wide, and flat: on this path no one wanders astray, but rather at the end of the path: the way would please many, but the end of the way displeases and terrifies them: therefore, the wise prefer a path that is rough for a time, which nevertheless leads to a perpetually blessed lodging, rather than a path smiling with brief pleasantness, but which leads to a prison destined for perpetual tears and tortures. Truly Job says: *

> *As the cloud is consumed and passes away, so he who goes down to the underworld shall not come up, nor shall he return any more to his house, neither shall his place know him anymore.* <margin>Job 7</margin>

### IV. Conclusion.

Whoever occasionally descends seriously into the thought of Eternity, he is not only unable to live licentiously and petulantly, but he cannot even frequently laugh out loud or guffaw immoderately.

<summary>This page warns that ignoring the reality of eternity leads to spiritual ruin, using the parable of the rich man and the Book of Job to contrast the easy path to hell with the difficult path to salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Dives and Lazarus, spiritual blindness, repentance, afterlife, Book of Job, morality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the gravity of eternity and the fleeting nature of worldly pleasure, following the previous discussion on the finality of death.</meta>

-># NINTH<-

<margin>* Ecclesiastes chapter 2.</margin>
<margin>† Deuteronomy 32.</margin>
will be discovered: as many as have looked upon the nature of the other life from among the dead, and have returned to us restored to life, they were not seen to have laughed easily. This was singularly observed in that friend of Christ from Bethany, Lazarus <note>Lazarus of Bethany, whom Jesus raised from the dead; tradition often holds that he never smiled again after witnessing the afterlife.</note>. All these were able to affirm of themselves with the Preacher <note>original: "Ecclesiaste," referring to the author of the Book of Ecclesiastes.</note>: *I accounted laughter as error, and to joy I said, Why are you vainly deceived?* <note>original: "Risum reputavi errorem, & gaudio dixi, Quid frustra deciperis?"</note> Cyril of Alexandria <note>A 5th-century Patriarch and influential theologian.</note> rightly confesses himself fearful in this matter, and says, "I fear Gehenna <note>term: Gehenna (a place of fiery torment or Hell)</note> because it is unending; I dread the pestilential worm because it is perennial." O that they would be wise and understand, and provide for their last end †; whoever is not impelled toward better things by this thought of Eternity (I shall speak briefly, but I shall speak truly) either has no faith, or, if he has faith, he certainly has no heart or sense. Not absurdly did a learned man once affirm that a wedding is a short and joyful song, but it has a long and mournful concluding refrain <note>term: epiphonema (a finishing stroke or summary exclamation)</note>. So we shall most truly say: Every pleasure of sin is a brief and cheerful little ditty, but it has the longest and saddest concluding refrain: Eternal torment. O Eternity, O Eternity, Eternity!

### V. Conclusion.

When the discourse is concerning Eternity, no one can say too much, or increase the matter too greatly: whatever may be said, there is no exaggeration here, no numbers too extended; that which is said of the infinite and the eternal is always less than the reality. For Eternity possesses these qualities: it remains whole, no matter what you take from it—

<meta>catchword: —take away:</meta>

<summary>This page reflects on the sobering reality of eternity, citing the solemnity of the resurrected Lazarus and the warnings of Church Fathers to argue that the fleeting joys of sin lead to unending sorrow.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Lazarus, Cyril of Alexandria, Gehenna, Ecclesiastes, repentance, afterlife, sin and punishment</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the nature of eternity through various metaphors; this page continues the mathematical and sensory exploration of infinite punishment.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Subtract as many years as there are stars in the heaven, drops in the sea, grains of sand, leaves on the trees, or blades of grass upon the earth; eternity remains whole. Add just as many years, and it shall not be any greater; for as long as God shall exist, so long shall He punish the damned. We have demonstrated this above through several shadowy likenesses; let us now add another from Saint Bonaventure <gloss>*</gloss> <note>Saint Bonaventure (1221–1274) was a major medieval Franciscan theologian and philosopher.</note>. If any one of the damned were to weep so sparingly that he shed only a single tiny tear every hundred years: and if these small drops from so many centuries were preserved until they finally equaled the volume of a sea; alas, how many millions of years must turn before even a small stream—let alone an Ocean—might be formed? And yet, even then it could truly be said: *Eternity* is only just beginning. Even if this were to happen again, and another river or sea were collected from these rare drops, once again, when all that time had passed, it must be said: now *Eternity* begins, and so on into *infinity*. There is nothing here for anyone to doubt, for there is no proportion between the finite and the infinite. But this seems wonderful and incredible to us because our imagination cannot grasp it; it cannot reach such remote things; it does not penetrate here—it is infinite, it is impenetrable. And this is also a reason why our intellect is brought to contemplate *Eternity* with such difficulty: because it feels a certain shame, or is indignant, that it should weary itself with that which it cannot penetrate. But we must not yield to this misplaced modesty; the intellect must be impelled and compelled toward this most wholesome meditation daily.

<insert>* Bonaventure, *On the Infernal Regions*, chapter 49.</insert>

<term>Eternity (Aeternitas)</term>: In this context, the state of being without end, specifically regarding the duration of divine judgment.
<term>The Damned (Damnatos)</term>: Those judged to be separated from God and subject to perpetual punishment.
<term>Intellect (Intellectus)</term>: The faculty of the mind used for reasoning and understanding spiritual truths.

<summary>This page uses a vivid metaphor involving single tears shed every century to illustrate the incomprehensible scale of eternity and the persistence of divine punishment. It encourages the reader to force their mind to contemplate the infinite, despite the human intellect's natural struggle to grasp such a concept.</summary>

# NINTH [SECTION] 155

If one undertakes this, and at least exercises oneself in weighing such similarities, one will never err; one will never imagine so many millions of years that there are not more and more into infinity. This matter is certain and beyond controversy.

<margin>* Daniel 12</margin>
The prophet Daniel, in order to better express the incomprehensible extension of Eternity, pronounced this: *But those who are learned shall shine like the brightness of the firmament, and those who instruct many unto justice, like stars for perpetual Eternities.* <note>original: "in perpetuas Æternitates." This is a Latin rendering of Daniel 12:3, emphasizing the plural nature of the word "eternity."</note> He spoke as if he had said: Words are not sufficient to explain Eternity; I cannot say more, even if more ought to be said. In this, the Prophet added a double <term>auxesis</term> <note>A rhetorical device involving exaggeration or a gradual increase in intensity.</note> to his words when he said "Eternities" and "Perpetual." Indeed, if one eternity is endless, what will two, ten, a hundred, a thousand, or even perpetual ones be? If we compare a year, or "Great Years" multiplied a thousand times, to Eternity, what part of it will we have explained? 

They say that the eighth celestial sphere <note>In the Ptolemaic system, this is the sphere of the fixed stars.</note> moves more than slowly; and although it is turned around daily by the force of the "prime mover," <note>The *primum mobile*, the outermost moving sphere in ancient astronomy.</note> yet by its own motion it is not fully revolved in its circuit except every thirty-six thousand years. This span of time—the <term>Great Year</term>, <note>Also known as the "Platonic Year," the time it takes for all the planets and fixed stars to return to their original positions.</note> the revolving and Platonic year—will be nothing but a moment, an instant, a minute of the clock compared to Eternity. Boethius truly said: An instant or point of time, and ten thousand years—

<summary>The author uses the biblical prophecy of Daniel and the astronomical concept of the "Great Year" to illustrate the staggering, incomprehensible scale of eternity compared to human time. He argues that even the longest cosmic cycles are merely minutes on the clock of the infinite.</summary>

<margin>* 1 John chapter 2. On Psalm 145.</margin>
are more similar to one another than ten thousand years are to Eternity. * Saint John says: *Little children, it is the last hour.* <note>original: "novissima hora est"</note> And yet he said this sixteen hundred years ago. Therefore, Saint Augustine spoke most truly: *Every thing which has an end is brief.* <note>Augustine of Hippo, a foundational Christian theologian (354–430 AD).</note> "Eternity" as a word consists of four syllables, but in itself, it is without end. Love eternity; you shall reign with Christ without end, if Christ is your end <note>The author plays on "end" meaning both "conclusion" and "ultimate goal."</note>.

### VI. *Conclusion.*

<margin>‡ Proverbs chapter 18.</margin>
It is not believable that a person—especially one imbued with the ancient faith—could be found of such a weak and unbridled mind (provided they do not live like a beast and rush headlong into desire ‡ for *When the wicked man comes into the depth of sins, he grows contemptuous*) who, if they granted even a part of an hour once a day to this meditation on Eternity—indeed, if they thought seriously about Eternity even once every week—would not change their life for a far better one. Such a person would become different: from proud to submissive and modest, from angry to mild and gentle, from impure to chaste and continent, from a drunkard to temperate and sober. Indeed, such a man would become truly religious—in spirit certainly, if not in his clothing. But one must not pass through this meditation hastily, or in a passing and perfunctory way. The matter must be weighed and pondered; the thought must be fixed, and the mind must revolve again and again upon the Eternal, the Eternal, the Eternal, which shall never have an end, never, never;

<summary>The author concludes by arguing that even a brief weekly meditation on the infinite nature of Eternity is enough to morally transform any person from vice to virtue. He emphasizes that while the word "Eternity" is short, its reality is an endless state that dwarfs all human time.</summary>

<keywords>Eternity, Saint Augustine, Saint John, repentance, meditation, moral transformation, temperance, finality</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on the afterlife and the necessity of deep spiritual reflection, following the previous discussions on the nature of the soul's journey.</meta>

# NINTH [CONSIDERATION]

than this; because it shall endure for countless, incomprehensible, and infinite ages, and shall never cease to endure. This, I say, must be considered more attentively, and chewed like food. For food, even if it is precious and healthy, is poison and not nourishment if it is not ground by the teeth and digested; it creates all kinds of diseases and remains for a time in the body, but generates no good nutrients. In the same way, thoughts about death, judgment, heaven, and hell are entirely healthy and holy, yet by far the healthiest is the thought of Eternity, which may rightly be called the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>original: "Quinta essentia"; in alchemy and philosophy, the purest and most essential element beyond earth, air, fire, and water</note>. But this, like precious food, must not only be swallowed but also digested. A person alone, free from cares and attentive, should weigh these things within themselves; unless this is done, almost all reading and hearing of divine matters will be without fruit and will be given to the winds. Many hear many sermons, and read some things themselves; they profit hardly at all from them because they weigh neither what is read nor what is heard. Everything passes by, and before it can result in action, it slips away. One must linger a while on what is heard and read, and the will must be instructed by them. This we learn from the Most Blessed Virgin, the Mother of God:
<margin>* Luke, chapter 2</margin>
*But Mary kept all these words, pondering them in her heart.* <note>original Latin: "Maria autem conservabat omnia verba hæc, conferens in corde suo."</note>

->VII. Conclusion.<-

**N**o one believes, or certainly no one understands and weighs, these most clear words of Christ:
<margin>† Matthew, chapter 7</margin>
*† Enter through the narrow gate, for wide is the gate and spacious is the way that leads to perdition, and many are those who enter through*

# CONSIDERATION

<margin>* Luke 13. † Sermon 64 on the words of the Lord.</margin>
it. *How narrow is the gate and constricted is the way which leads to life, and there are few who find it!* <note>original: "quam angusta porta & arcta via est..." quoting Matthew 7:14</note> and He repeats this same thing through the mouth of Saint Luke: *Strive to enter through the narrow gate, for many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able.* † Whosoever, says Augustine, mocks this faith—thinking he should not believe because he does not see—when that which he did not believe finally arrives, he shall blush with shame, and being confounded and separated, he is damned.

<margin>‡ Book 1 on the Good State of Religion, chapter 5. * Matthew chapter 7.</margin>
Hieronymus Platus <note>Girolamo Piatti (1548–1591), a Jesuit writer</note> ‡ reports that not long after the new family <note>The Franciscan Order</note> was established by Saint Francis, Berthold—a distinguished man of that same family—preached from the pulpit with such force and freedom of speech against a certain vice, that a woman conscious of her own guilt in the matter fell down dead. Soon, while the entire assembly prostrated themselves in prayer, she returned to life from the dead; and being asked why she was ordered to return to life and what she had seen in the other world, she recounted it in this manner: "When I stood at the tribunal of God, there stood with me sixty thousand souls, who had been summoned to the Judge from all parts of the world by various kinds of death. Out of this entire number, only three were assigned to the expiatory flames <note>original: "piacularibus flammis," referring to Purgatory</note>; against all the rest, the sentence of eternal punishment was pronounced." Alas! I would not believe this woman's story, had I not first believed Christ affirming so seriously: *Wide is the way that leads to perdition, and many are those who enter through it; narrow*

# NINTH <note>original: "NONA" - likely the ninth section or meditation in a series</note>

Narrow is the way that leads to life, and few find it. Who, indeed, would believe that eternal punishment was decreed by God—not for sixty people, but for so many thousands of thousands—unless the supreme and infinite majesty of God, the inexplicable malice of sin against Him, and the most clear testimonies of Holy Scripture persuaded us of it?

<margin>* Job chapter 10. Chapter 2. Office of the Dead.</margin>
Job trembles: *Where no order, but everlasting horror dwells.* <note>Job 10:22</note> Matthew affirms: *Depart from me, you cursed, into eternal fire.* <note>Matthew 25:41</note> The Church pronounces: *Because I sin daily and do not repent, the fear of death troubles me, for in hell there is no redemption.* <note>A response from the Office of the Dead</note> No redemption; but neither is any consolation, nor even a tiny drop of water hanging from the tip of a finger, permitted to be brought there. 

But if even those who live in the grace of God—men of good mind and will—sufficiently understood from how many torments they were to be delivered by the Judge's sentence on the Day of Judgment, and with how many joys remaining for eternity they were to be filled, they would not delay even for an hour. Instead, they would leave vanities to the vain, the dead to the dead, and the world to its own lovers. They would themselves embrace that religion above all in which they might best please God, and most greatly praise Him throughout their entire lives for that double eternal benefit to be bestowed upon them: the liberation from Gehenna <note>a term for hell or a place of fiery torment</note> and the gift of Paradise.

<margin>‡ Book 10 of the Morals, chapter 10.</margin>

# CONSIDERATION

If one does not wish to consider the rewards that remain, those things which he endures seem heavy. <note>original: "non vult præmia considerare quæ restant, gravia æstimat esse quæ tolerat"</note> But if one once raises himself toward eternal things and fixes the eye of his heart upon those things which remain unchangeably, he perceives that whatever runs toward an end is almost nothing. The same author says: *A song in the night*, he says, *is joy in tribulation.* <note>A reference to Job 35:10; the "song" represents spiritual comfort amidst the "night" of suffering.</note> Because even if we are afflicted by temporal pressures, we nevertheless rejoice in the hope of eternity.

In exactly the same way, Augustine <margin>On Psalm 36</margin> reasoned eloquently; When you consider, he says, what you are to receive, all that you suffer will be worthless to you, nor will you judge those things worthy of that which you receive in exchange; for certainly, brothers, for eternal rest, an eternal labor would have to be undergone: to receive eternal happiness, you ought to sustain eternal passions. But if you sustained eternal labor, when would you come to eternal happiness? Thus, it is necessary that your tribulation be temporal, so that when it is finished, you may come to infinite happiness. Weigh a thousand years against eternity: what are you weighing when you compare any finite amount, however large, with the infinite? Ten thousand years, ten hundred thousand—if it must be said—and thousands of thousands, which have an end, cannot be compared with eternity. To this is added that God willed your labor to be not only temporal, but also brief: the whole life of man is only a matter of a few little days. Therefore, even if someone, through the whole span of life, [is worn down] by labors, hardships, and various pains... <margin>Book 26 of the *Moralia*, chapter 102, on chapter 35 of Job.</margin>

The same Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian</note> says elsewhere: The thoughts of God are made exceedingly deep. Where is the thought of God? For the present, He loosens the reins, but later He will pull them tight. What seems long to you is actually brief: all things pass away quickly. What is the long life of a man compared to the Eternity of God? Do you wish to be long-suffering <term>longanimis: patient or steadfast over a long duration</term>? Look toward the Eternity of God. For you gaze upon your few days, and in those few days you wish for all things to be fulfilled? You wish for all the wicked to be condemned and all the good to be crowned within these days of yours? God fulfills these things in His own time. He is eternal; He delays; He is long-suffering. But you say: "The reason I am not long-suffering is because I am a temporal being." Yet you have it in your power: join your heart to the eternity of God, and with Him, you shall be eternal. If you are already a Christian and well-instructed, you say: "God reserves all things for His judgment." The good labor because they are whipped like sons <note>A reference to the biblical idea that a father disciplines the children he loves</note>; the evil exult because they are condemned like strangers. # CONSIDERATION

<note>The following text uses the metaphor of two sons to explain why the righteous suffer while the wicked seem to prosper.</note>

...one he neglects, the other he dismisses; one does evil <margin>The text is cropped at the right margin, but the sense remains clear.</margin> and is not corrected by the father, while the other, as soon as he has moved <insert>incorrectly</insert>, is struck with blows and whipped. Why is that one dismissed, and this one beaten? It is because the inheritance is being saved for the one who is beaten, while the one who was dismissed has been disinherited. The father sees that he has no hope, and so he lets him go, so that he may do whatever he wishes. The boy who is whipped—if he lacks sense and is unwise and foolish—congratulates his brother who is not beaten, and groans for himself, saying in his heart: "My brother does such great evils; he does whatever he wants against my father’s commands, and no one says a harsh word to him. But as for me, as soon as I move, I am beaten." He is a fool, he is unwise; he pays attention to what he suffers, and does not pay attention to what is being saved for him.

To these words from the same most holy and learned prelate* <note>original: "praesule"; likely referring to Saint Augustine</note>, let us rightly join these others, as if they were a summary of what has been said so far: What is the nature of the mercy of God? He does not say, "Labor for hundreds of thousands of years"; He does not say, "Labor for even a thousand years"; He does not say, "Labor for five hundred years." He says: "While you live, labor." In these few years, there will be rest, and it shall have no end. And though you labor for a few years, even in those labors, consolation is not lacking; daily joys are not lacking. But do not rejoice in the world; rejoice in Christ, rejoice in His word, rejoice in His law. It is true, therefore, what the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in 2 Corinthians 4:17</note> said: *For that which is at present is temporal, and the light weight of our tribulation works for us, in an incredible way and unto an incredible measure, an eternal weight of glory.*

Think on these things, O mortals, and before you belong to eternity—whether in joys or in torments—foresee Eternity. All things pass away; Eternity alone remains, and it shall not pass.

### I. *The punishment of eternal death.*

The Messenians <note>An ancient people of the Peloponnese in Greece.</note> possessed a subterranean prison, ignorant of both air and light, and full of hellish horror. But so that they might veil this most deadly place with an elegant title, it was called the *Treasury* <note>original: "Thesaurus"</note>. This prison had no doors; instead, the accused was lowered down by a rope, and the opening was closed with a massive rock. Into this *Treasury*, even that great General of Greece, Philopoemen <note>A skilled Greek general and statesman, often called "the last of the Greeks."</note>, was hidden away, and there he ended his life by poison. God also has a *Treasury* beneath the earth—but alas, of what kind? It is a treasury of the wicked, the damned, and the most desperate of men.

Ezzelino <note>original: "Actiolinus." Ezzelino III da Romano, a notorious medieval tyrant in Northern Italy known for his extreme cruelty.</note>, formerly the tyrant of the Paduans <margin>Jovius relates this <note>Referring to the historian Paolo Giovio.</note></margin>, maintained many prisons. These were so infamous for every kind of misery and torture that, within them, death was considered a luxury; yet death did not easily arrive, though it was frequently summoned. As many as were thrust in there, weighed down with much iron, were killed in a miserable fashion and by slow degrees by stench, hunger, and filth. They truly felt themselves dying. In that place, no one was considered anything but most wretched, unless he happened to die; the punishment was more grievous than death itself, as they were forced to dwell while living among the dead. For there, bodies were piled up in heaps of rotting—

But the most foul of these prisons, when compared with the dungeons of the underworld, is a paradise and a pleasant palace. Whatever you might endure in the prison of Ezzelino <note>original: "Actiolini." Refers to Ezzelino III da Romano (1194–1259), a notorious Italian tyrant famous for his extreme cruelty and the horrific conditions of his prisons.</note>, all things are tolerable because they are brief, because they look toward their own end, because they finish with life, and because in death all things vanish. But truly that Treasury of the Damned, which is the prison of God, has no comfort from the brevity of tortures; it excludes death, knows no exit, and admits no end—none, alas, none. Most truly did Cassiodorus <note>A 6th-century Roman statesman and writer.</note> say: *Just as no mortal understands what the eternal reward is, so no one grasps what the torment will be that lasts without end.*

<margin>* Franciscus Titelman on Psalm 87.</margin>
Among the Persians there was such a prison that entry into it was indeed easy, but exit was granted with great difficulty, or not at all; for that reason it was called <term>Lethe</term> <note>The Greek word for "Oblivion" or "Forgetfulness," usually referring to the river in Hades that makes souls forget their past lives.</note>. The descent to the underworld is easy, but for those who have once entered, no exit is found; no one has ever retraced their step from there. This prison of God is called by its true name, <term>Lethe</term>, or Oblivion; for God so forgets the damned that He will not remember them for grace throughout all Eternity. Hell is truly the land of oblivion, and that (as a pious and learned writer observes) for a twofold reason. Because, he says, those who are there are no longer mindful of God for any good purpose, nor do they have any [memory] of their former...

# NINTH SECTION

The fire shall not be extinguished. In hell <note>original: "gehenna"</note>, toward which you hasten, there are no feast days on which the kindled furnace might rest: there is eternal pain, eternal death, eternal mourning, never to be mingled with any comforts. Night and day, the sleepless, watchful, unquenchable flame will claim you; you shall live for a constant, perpetual death. * Believe **Augustine** on this point, who warns as eloquently as he does solemnly: The wicked, he says, shall have life in their tortures: but those who live in torments desire (if it were possible) to end such a life, and no one grants them death, just as no one takes away their torture. But look at the Scripture speaking and discerning; it did not deign to call such a life "life." In tortures, in torments, in everlasting fires, it refused to use the name of life, so that the very name of life might be a term of praise, not of sorrow; so that wherever you hear of "life," you do not think of torments. For to be forever in torments is eternal death, not any kind of life. The Scriptures call this the **Second Death**, following this first death which we all owe to the human condition. Even the Second Death is called "death," and yet no one dies there: or rather, I might better say, no one lives there: for to live in pains is not truly living. Therefore, that life in torments is not life; and that life alone is true which is blessed; nor can it be blessed unless it be Eternal. He confirms this very thing elsewhere: If the soul lives in eternal punishments, by which even the unclean spirits themselves shall be tortured; that should rather be called eternal death

Gregory* bears a very similar testimony to this:
> In hell <note>original: "gehenna"</note> there will be for the wretched a death without death, and an end without end, because there death lives, and the end is always beginning.

So it is: the number of years among those below is without number, countable by no one. God Himself, in the counting of it, will not find its final year. After a thousand thousands of millions of years have been counted, just as many will remain to be counted; even when these are reckoned up, not only will the last year not be found, but each individual year will be as if it were the first. For Cain, after the five thousand years which he has lived there in a state of constant dying, the number of sorrowful years remains as complete as if he were being thrust into that flaming prison <term>ergastulum: originally a Roman private prison for slaves, here used for the dungeons of hell</term> for the first time this very day. And after several millions of years have been consumed from this count, the number itself will decrease no more than if he began to burn at this very hour. And although the torments of that wealthy glutton from the Gospel <note>A reference to the Parable of Dives and Lazarus in Luke 16</note> approach two thousand years, he nevertheless burns still, and will burn eternally, and he will never obtain even the tiny drop of water he long ago requested for his parching tongue.

We hear these things, we think on these things, and yet we laugh? Truly, it is a light matter to be in eternal flames

Here one might ask, and not without merit: *Where are your tears, O ridiculous mortals?* For such we certainly are: over a few small coins <note>original: "ob paucos obolos"</note>, over a minor loss, we pour out great drops of tears; yet an immense, irreparable loss we consume with cheerful laughter. Summoned before a human judge, we tremble; yet toward the tribunal of God we walk daily with great strides, even against our will, and we joke freely along the way. About to sail the sea, we dread the dangers; yet toward Eternity itself we run with a laugh.

The prayer of Blessed Bernard\* is most just: *Would that, would that,* he says, *they might savor life.* <note>original: "Vitam... saperent"</note> And to what end, Bernard, do we need this wisdom? So that the image of eternity may be restored in us: namely, that we may govern the present through wisdom, judge the past through understanding, and provide for the final things <note>term: "novissima" - the Four Last Things: Death, Judgment, Heaven, and Hell</note> as a precaution.

Here we have not a prayer from the Divine Paul, but a command; for he does not merely wish for the Ephesians, but orders them: ‡ *See then, brothers, how carefully you walk; not as unwise, but as wise, redeeming the time because the days are evil.* The business of salvation must be cared for circumspectly, diligently, and accurately; they are most foolish who do not spend this brief time granted for obtaining a happy Eternity, but instead squander it on the vainest little amusements: hoping to purchase heaven by joking, playing, and idling. Time is not to be redeemed with fables, nor with leisure or drinking cups, but with frequent pray—

# CONSIDERATION

with serious labor and constant industry, Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity</note> teaches that some time must be stolen even from secular business. He says: "When someone brings a lawsuit against you, lose something so that you may have leisure for God, rather than for lawsuits; for that which you lose is the price of your time." For just as you give coins to buy bread, so give up coins to buy yourself peace and time for God; for this is what it means to redeem the time.

<margin>* In the Sentences. † Ecclesiastes chapter 10. ‡ Galatians chapter 6; Romans chapter 13.</margin>
Thus, the opportunity for doing good must be purchased at the expense of any other thing, because the days are evil. The days of this life are most full of sorrows, dangers, temptations, and hardships, which—as Anselm <note>St. Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), known for his ontological arguments</note> says—either take away or diminish the occasion for doing good. But if we allow this occasion to slip away, and let our life flow out in mere resolutions for a better life, there will not be even a moment more in which to repair what was neglected. Gregory of Nazianzus <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), one of the Cappadocian Fathers</note> says: *Our life is like a marketplace; when its day has passed, there will be no more time to buy what you wish.* Therefore, we must buy now while the market is open; we must live piously now while our strength suffices. Ecclesiastes repeatedly emphasizes this very point: † ***Whatever your hand can do, work at it instantly.*** The Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul</note> urges us often with this same goad of time and opportunity: ‡ ***While we have time, let us do good to all, because it is now the hour for us to rise from sleep.*** You sleep, says Ambrose <note>St. Ambrose (c. 339–397 AD), Bishop of Milan</note>, but your time does not sleep; rather, it walks—
