he walks, or rather, he flies. It is well for him, and well for all those who do not merely think on these things, but also demonstrate them through their work, and live in such a way as they will wish to have lived when the time comes to die; they do those things which, once established in eternity, they will rejoice to have done. *A slight neglect now results in an eternal loss.* <note>original: "Levis nunc neglectus, Æternum sit dispendium."</note> What is once thought, once said, and once done, is Eternal.

### II. *The reward of eternal life.*

Life in heaven is truly vital, and is a perfect life, insofar as it is animal, human, angelic, and divine. <note>The author uses "animal" here in the philosophical sense of "having a soul" or "animate," rather than referring to beasts.</note> Memory lives there, through the recollection of all past things; the intellect lives through the knowledge and vision of God; the Will lives there and enjoys every good, and does so without any fear of losing it. In like manner, the appetite lives there—both the so-called "desiring" and "spirited" parts; <note>term: concupiscible and irascible appetites. These are traditional scholastic terms for the parts of the soul that desire good things and those that resist obstacles or evils.</note> all the senses live and enjoy their own delights. There is no groaning there, no sign of lamentation or mourning, not even the slightest; there is the purest honey of joys, with all the gall of sadness cast out. Be silent here, O eyes, for you have seen nothing like it; be silent, O ears, for you have heard nothing like it; be silent, O heart, for you have imagined nothing like this life. This life includes all delights, riches, honors, and the pleasures of all lives, senses, and powers. Burning with the highest desire for this life, Augustine* exclaims: *How great will be the happiness there, where no evil shall exist, and no good shall lie hidden; where leisure will be spent in the praises of God, who shall be all in all.* <note>original: "Quanta erit illic felicitas..." from City of God, Book 22, Chapter 30.</note> *Blessed are those who dwell*

<meta>catchword: in the house-</meta>

True glory shall be there, where no one shall be praised by the error or flattery of the one giving praise. True honor shall be there, which will be denied to no one worthy, and granted to no one unworthy; nor indeed shall any unworthy person seek it, where none shall be permitted to exist unless they are worthy. He himself, who gave virtue, shall be the reward of virtue; and he has promised himself, than whom nothing better or greater can exist. For what else is it that he said through the prophet? *I will be their God, and they shall be my people.* <note>original: "Ero illorum Deus, & ipsi erunt mihi plebs." Yet this perpetual happiness can be obtained by any man in a very short time, and with labor that is not so very great. * Christ, sighing over the people, said: "I have compassion on the multitude, for behold, they have now stayed with me for three days." <note>A reference to the feeding of the four thousand in Mark 8:2</note> O sweetest Jesus, you now count the three days in which we endure for you; and why, good Christ, do you not count the eternity of days in which you will perpetuate immortal joy for us in heaven? Behold, how easy it is to merit eternal glory through brief labor! God by no means fails to count and value our services: he counts the hairs scattered upon the head, and will he not count the drops of blood shed for his sake?

Allow me, therefore, to proclaim with Blessed Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century scholar and translator of the Vulgate Bible</note>: O how great a blessedness it is to receive great things for small ones, eternal things for brief ones, and to have the Lord as one's debtor! But it is hard, you say, to suffer so many things daily; it is hard to die, even if all other things were easy. What of it, my Christian friend,

->CONSIDERATION<-

...not because it is not worth so much, but so that what is purchased may be possessed. It is indeed worthy to be bought with perpetual labor, but it is necessary that it be bought with temporal labor <note>The author distinguishes between "perpetual labor" (eternal effort) and "temporal labor" (our limited time on earth), suggesting God graciously accepts our brief earthly efforts as payment for an eternal reward.</note>. Therefore, O Christians, let us exhort ourselves to this labor along with Him.

> \* Let us set such a life before our eyes, when Eternity is promised, so that we may remove from it whatever we suffer here that is troublesome: for we shall more easily find what is *not* there, than what *is* there. And yet, it is for sale; buy it, if you wish, and do not be overly distressed by the greatness of the thing because of the magnitude of the price: it is worth as much as you have. Do not ask what you possess, but what sort of person you are. This thing is worth you yourself: it is worth as much as you are. Give yourself, and you shall have it; why are you distressed? Why are you troubled? Will you have to search for yourself, or buy yourself? Behold, you who are, such as you are—give yourself and you shall have it. But I am evil, you will say, and perhaps it will not accept me? By giving yourself to it, you will be good. To give yourself to this faith and promise, this is what it means to be good. Moreover, when you have become good, you will be the price of the thing itself, and you will have not only what I have said—salvation, safety, life, and life without end—you will not only have this, I take away even more. For there will be no growing weary or sleeping, no hungering or thirsting, no growing up or growing old, because there will be no being born where the numbers remain whole. The number that exists is the same; there is no need for it to be increased, because there it does not happen that it is diminished †. Behold how many things I have taken away, and yet I have not said what will be there, because that "neither eye has seen, nor ear has heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man." For how could it enter into my heart, so that I might say what has not entered into the heart of man? <note>original: "quod in cor hominis non ascendit." This is a famous theological concept known as "apophatic" description—defining heaven by what it lacks (pain, hunger, age) because its true glory is beyond human language.</note>

# ON JONAH

And because we have been led here through the books of Augustine, as if through gardens toward the heavenly paradise, let us seal these previous matters with the words of that same most eloquent and holy teacher.

<margin>* From the *Manual*, chapter 15. <note>The *Manual* (Manuale) was a popular medieval devotional text formerly attributed to St. Augustine, though now considered to be by a later author.</note></margin>

> *If it were necessary,* he says, *to endure all manner of torments daily, or to tolerate even hell <note>original: "gehennam"</note> itself for a long time, so that we might see Christ in His glory and be joined to His saints, would it not be worth suffering every sorrowful thing to be considered participants in such great good and such great glory? Therefore, let demons lie in wait; let them prepare their temptations. Let fasts break the body, and rough clothing press upon the flesh. Let labors weigh me down, and vigils dry me up. Let this person shout at me, or that one disturb me. Let the cold bend me, let my conscience murmur, let heat burn me, let my head ache, and my chest glow with fever. Let the stomach be bloated, the face grow pale, and the whole self become weak. Let my life fail in sorrow and my years in groaning; let rottenness enter my bones and swarm beneath me, so that I may find rest in the day of tribulation and ascend to our prepared people. For what shall be the glory of the righteous? How grand the joy of the saints, when every single face shall shine like the sun? When the Lord begins to review His people in the kingdom of His Father, arranged in their distinct ranks, and restores the promised rewards for the merits and works of each—heavenly things for earthly ones, and eternal things for those that are temporal.*

Therefore, think upon the days of old and keep the Eternal Years in your mind. Think upon Eternity, O man; think, think upon the eternal punishments and the eternal joys, and never (I promise this with certainty) will you complain about any adversity. That voice...

that thought will never leave you: "this is too heavy, this is intolerable, this is too harsh"; instead, you will say that all things are bearable and easy, and you will never be more pleased with yourself than when you are most afflicted.

<margin>* Chapter 141.</margin>
John Moschus <note>A 7th-century Byzantine monk and author of the 'Spiritual Meadow'</note> reports that Olympius, a man of singular patience in the monastery of Gerasimus by the Jordan, bore every kind of trouble with the greatest endurance by keeping his gaze on eternal things. When a certain religious guest met him on the road and asked him with no small wonder: "And how, my dear Olympius, can you endure in this cave, in such heat, amidst the stinging gnats and swarms of flies?" Olympius replied: "Son," he said, "these things are light. I tolerate them so that I may escape those intolerable and eternal tortures. I suffer the gnats so that I may flee the immortal worm <note>A reference to Mark 9:48, symbolizing the eternal pangs of conscience or hell</note>; it is easy to bear this heat for one who fears the eternal fire. These troubles of mine, though they are bothersome, are nevertheless brief and will have an end; those others have none." May Truth and Wisdom love you, Olympius! For you have spoken as wisely as you have truly. If only there were many who would think these things with you, there would also be many who would endure these things with you.

When he was asked why he fashioned everything so precisely in every detail, and with such a slow and painstaking brush—

# ACTIONS

...would he fashion it? "I paint for a long time," he replied, "because I paint for eternity." <note>This refers to a famous anecdote about the ancient Greek painter Zeuxis, who worked slowly to ensure perfection.</note> And we too, all of us, paint for eternity; for whatever works we perform, they look toward eternity in such a way that anyone can truly say: I write, I read, I sing, I pray, I labor for eternity. Whatever I do or say, even whatever I think, I think for eternity. If this is the reasoning behind all our labors, let us not paint with a fleeting or yawning hand, but with an attentive one, so that we may send across into eternity works that are perfectly finished. All things must certainly be sent there, whether for punishment or for reward. I repeat what must be repeated and driven home a thousand times: what is once thought, once said, and once done, is Eternal.

<margin>* Book 11 on Job.</margin>
*Saint Gregory* <note>Pope Gregory the Great, a central figure in medieval theology.</note> says: "With vigilant care, through all our works, our intention must be weighed, so that it seeks nothing worldly in the things it does, but fixes itself entirely upon the solidity of Eternity." In all your works, therefore, be perfect; pray, study, endure, strive, and labor for Eternity. Live for God, live for heaven, live for Eternity.

<margin>† Sermon 25 to the Clergy.</margin>
Truly says *Saint Bernard †*: "Our works do not pass away as they seem to; rather, every worldly thing is cast down like the seeds of Eternity. The foolish man will be amazed when he sees a heavy harvest rise up from this modest seed, whether good or bad, according to the quality of the sowing." He who considers this thinks no sin to be small, because he values the future harvest more than the act of sowing.

<meta>This page continues the moral reflection on the nature of eternal life versus earthly vanity, likely following a discussion on the purpose of human creation.</meta>

->CONSIDERATION<-

<margin>* Sermon on the Martyrs, who are called the "White Mass," chapter 2.</margin>
Oh, the madness of Adam! We were created for the possession of infinite and eternal goods; why then do we cast ourselves down with our whole affection toward fleeting and most vain rules? God has enrolled us as heirs of heaven and possessors of Eternity; why do we gape so greedily at the earth, destined to perish miserably in the wrappings of our own vanities? Let us be wise while we may, and *live for Eternity*, daily tending toward it with great strides. The way is short and narrow, but the destination is most spacious.

But oh, how wretched or foolish we are! We wish to obtain eternal life, yet we are unwilling to set foot upon the path to that life; we desire to be there, but we shrink from going there. There is no one who does not wish to be happy. "There is no one," says Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity.</note>, "no matter what kind of life they have chosen, who does not desire a happy life. *A happy life, therefore, is the common possession of all; but how one arrives at it, where one should aim, and by what journey one reaches such a great thing, is a matter of controversy. Consequently, if we seek a happy life on earth, I do not know whether we can find it—not because what we seek is an evil thing, but because we do not seek it in its proper place. One person says: 'Blessed are those who serve as soldiers'; another denies this and says: 'Blessed are those who till the fields'; yet another denies this and says: 'Blessed are those who move in the brightness of the public forum and defend legal cases.' Another denies this and says: 'Blessed are those who judge and have the power of hearing and discerning.' Another denies this and says: 'Blessed are those who sail to many regions, learn many things, and gather profits.' You see, dearest friends, that in all that multitude of lifestyles, not one pleases everyone; and yet, the happy life itself pleases everyone.*"

<summary>The author reflects on the "madness" of preferring earthly vanities over eternal life and quotes St. Augustine to illustrate that while everyone seeks happiness, they disagree on where to find it.</summary>
<keywords>Adam's madness, Eternity, St. Augustine, the happy life, earthly vanity, White Mass martyrs, human desire</keywords>

# ONONIA

Therefore, happiness is not to be expected here, but sought elsewhere, and cannot be found except through a good death. Even the wicked desire a good death, yet they flee from a good life. To die well is a matter of happiness; to live well is a matter of labor: the former cannot be obtained without the latter. Eternity depends upon death, and death depends upon a path that is either good or evil. Choose now: to have perished once is to have perished for Eternity.

<margin>* Numbers, chapter 23.</margin>
It was not many years ago that a man of noble blood and sharp intellect, but a novice in religion, was asked what he thought of the austere life of religious men compared to the more licentious life of others. He replied: "I would rather live with the latter, but I would rather die with the former." This was indeed a witty remark; but he could have answered even more like a Christian in this way: "I wish to live with those with whom I expect to die." Thus, even the vow of Balaam <note>A biblical figure who, despite being a prophet for hire, recognized the blessedness of the righteous.</note> was not bad: *May my soul die the death of the righteous:* but Balaam would have spoken more prudently and more usefully for himself had he said: *May my soul live the life of the righteous, so that it may die the death of the righteous.* For whoever lives the life of the godly will also meet the death of the godly; whoever spends a life among the impious will also perish by their death—and that once, and always, and for Eternity.

<margin>‡ Plutarch, in Laconian Apophthegms.</margin>
‡ Lamachus the Centurion <note>A famous Athenian general known for his strict discipline.</note> was rebuking a soldier for a mistake he had committed. To wash away his guilt, the soldier promised he would never do such a thing again. To him the Centurion said: "In war, my good man, one is not allowed to sin twice." In death, alas, one is not allowed to sin even once: for such a sin is irrevocable; once you are dead, you are dead forever: once

->CONSIDERATION<-

If you have died badly, you are damned forever. You will not be able to correct this death or shake off this damnation through all eternity.

The General Iphicrates <note>An Athenian general known for his strict discipline and military reforms in the 4th century BCE.</note> used to say that this phrase was shameful for a commander: *I had not thought.* Far more shameful and significantly more damaging is this phrase from a Christian: *I had not thought there was such a difference between a chaste life and a life of pleasure; I had not thought that all Eternity depended upon it; I certainly had not thought I would die so soon.* Alas, how sleepily we conduct the business of Eternity! Even though this mortal life of ours does not have a single certain moment of its own; and it is more certain than certainty itself that we must depart from here, and indeed at a most uncertain hour. When that hour comes, it will not seem to you that you have lived so long, but rather that you have flown to death in a single moment. You are a tenant, not an owner:

<margin>* Augustine on Psalm 148.</margin>
* the house is leased to you, not given; even if you are unwilling, you shall move out. We do not have a lasting city here.

<margin>† Chapter 3.</margin>

Those who hoard silver and gold, and there is no end to their acquisitions? Do they still retain their kingdoms and glory?* And the Prophet answers himself: *They have been wiped out, and have gone down to the underworld, and others have risen up in their place.* They have moved on, he says, for they were tenants, not owners; their houses were leased to others once they were cast out and banished to the depths. If one were to ask where the princes of heaven are, who [inhabit] that sublime empyrean <note>The highest part of heaven, believed by ancient and medieval cosmologists to be composed of pure fire or light.</note>

* "Let us crown ourselves with roses," say those men of loose and hopeless lives; truly, those who are about to perish crown themselves with roses, whose beauty and scent vanish in a single day. But the blessed are crowned with gems and most precious stones, whose loveliness does not perish. ‡ On the head of that wondrous woman <note>A reference to the Woman of the Apocalypse described in Revelation 12:1</note> is a crown not of roses from a garden, nor of gems from the sea, but of stars from heaven. Here, a labor that is not long fatigues us; there, an eternal rest receives us. And why do we seek rest before the end of our labor? We are still in the arena, in the dust, in the race; we must sweat, we must break through, we must struggle out.

<margin>‡ Homily 37 on the Gospels.</margin>
Saint Gregory <note>Gregory the Great, Pope and Doctor of the Church</note> says it best ‡: "If we consider," he says, "what and how great are the things promised to us in heaven, everything possessed on earth becomes cheap to the soul. <meta>This page continues the theological reflection on the ascent of the soul and the transition from temporal life to eternity, following the previous discussion on spiritual growth.</meta>

# NINTH

Just as one who climbs a mountain looks down upon all things lying below for a short while, the further he stretches his step toward the heights; so he who strives to fix his intention on the highest things—while he discovers through that very effort that there is no glory in this present life—is raised above the heights of the earth. And that which he previously believed to be above him, when he was placed in the depths of his desires, he recognizes to be beneath him as he advances by ascending.

<margin>
* Treatise 3 on the 12 stages of abuses. Deuteronomy chapter 34.
† 2 Samuel chapter 14. <note>Original: 2. Reg. cap. 14. In the Vulgate, 2 Samuel is titled 2 Kings.</note>
</margin>
To this reasoning of the blessed Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD)</note>, the golden teaching of Saint Augustine * will not be incongruous: That which must eventually be lost through necessity, ought to be distributed freely for the sake of an eternal reward. Moses lived long and lived in health, yet at last he ceased to live; Methuselah lived longer still, but he too finally departed from the living, and he died †. This is the epitaph of us all: *And he died*. We all die, and like water we slip away. The soul is immortal and eternal; it will live forever, either for reward or for punishment. Here we cast the die, the irrevocable die of Eternity—O happy eternity, O eternal happiness! How does it happen that we think of you so rarely, so modestly, so in passing? How does it happen that we do not labor more, or be more solicitous for your sake? O my God and my all, open our eyes, so that we may recognize what it is, how immense it is, how happy or miserable *Eternity* is. You created us for yourself, you created us for *Eternity*, because you are *Eternity*; you wished, commanded, and decreed to make us sharers of your *Eternity*; grant that this moment...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a mountain climber to describe spiritual ascent and reflects on the inevitability of death, citing the examples of Moses and Methuselah to emphasize the importance of preparing for eternity.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual ascent, mortality, eternity, Saint Gregory, Saint Augustine, Moses, Methuselah, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological reflection on eternity, moving from the general concept of time to the specific biblical promises found in the blessings of Moses.</meta>

# CONSIDERATION

Let us weigh this time piously and holily, so that in it we may labor for Eternity, suffer for Eternity, and strive for Eternity: and so that we may cry out the same to all people, and save as many souls as we can that are otherwise destined to perish forever. Hear, O Christians; hear, O Pagans; hear, O Kings and Princes; hear, O Germany; let the whole world hear:

->*No security is great enough where Eternity is at stake.*<-
<note>original: "Nulla satis magna securitas, ubi periclitatur Æternitas." This was a famous maxim by St. Bernard of Clairvaux, emphasizing that no earthly safety matters if one's soul is in eternal danger.</note>

O long, O deep, O bottomless, O eternal Eternity! Blessed are those who dwell in your house, O Lord; upon the eternal mountains, through infinite myriads of ages, they shall praise you.

<margin>Deuteronomy, chapter 33.</margin>
Moses, now very near to his own death <note>literally "his funeral"</note>, commending Israel to God with auspicious prayers, bid farewell to the tribe of Asher in the final place, saying: *Blessed be Asher among sons; let him be pleasing to his brothers, and let him dip his foot in oil. Iron and brass shall be his shoe. As the days of your youth, so also shall be your old age. There is no other God like the God of the most righteous. The rider of the heavens is your helper. By his magnificence the clouds disperse; his dwelling is above, and underneath are the everlasting arms.* <note>This passage from Deuteronomy 33:24-27 uses the "everlasting arms" as a metaphor for God's eternal support.</note> Thus, through the heavens, God extends the arms of his power into the immense distance, and beneath these arms of his, this entire world, all of time, and all the things of this world are contained, pass away, and are directed. Thus, from the beginning, by the single eternity of his Predestination <term>Predestination: the theological concept of God's foreknowledge and purpose for humanity</term>, God has been the dwelling place of all the upright, whom he both embraces and protects as if by certain eternal and continuous

<meta>catchword: arms-</meta>

<summary>The author exhorts all people to live with a focus on eternity and cites the blessing of Moses from Deuteronomy to illustrate God's eternal protection. The text emphasizes that the entire world and all of time are held within God's "everlasting arms."</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, salvation, Moses, Tribe of Asher, Divine Providence, Predestination, Deuteronomy, everlasting arms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual meditation on the soul's ascent, following the previous discussion of earthly struggles.</meta>

# NINTH SECTION

arms. Ascend, therefore, and enter into this dwelling of yours, O soul, you who here struggle with clods of earth and mud; stretch yourself upward and rise to the one who ascends the heavens, to your God who dwells upon the highest mountains of Eternity. Once placed there in safety, look down upon the earth, and see how small are those things which here either entice you with desire or strike you with terror: see how meager are all things contained within this tiny point of earth: see how, in the presence of God and in the face of Eternity, all things are vain, weak, and brief—indeed, how all things are a vile and vast emptiness, a great nothingness. Therefore, strive for the one supreme and immeasurable good, and hold the rest in low esteem: lean upon God, expand yourself in God: trample underfoot whatever beneath the sun or beneath the moon either alluringly entices or terribly threatens; think on eternal things, and constantly revolve that saying of the blessed Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father known for his Latin translation of the Bible.</note> within your mind: *no labor ought to seem hard, and no time ought to seem long, by which the glory of Eternity is acquired.*

<margin>* *Jerome, in the Life of Hilarion, chapter 18.*</margin>
An evil spirit <note>original: "Cacodæmon"</note> once possessing a camel was brought to the divine Hilarion <note>St. Hilarion (291–371 AD), an anchorite who spent most of his life in the desert.</note>; it began to rage in a terrible manner, as if it were about to devour the holy man. To which Hilarion said: "You do not frighten me, devil, with such a massive body; in a little fox or in a camel, you are one and the same to me." Presently, this ferocious camel collapsed before him, and all its former fierceness vanished into a ridiculous tameness. Such are all the allurements, all the temptations of the World, and all its terrors—what do you hope for?

<summary>This page exhorts the soul to rise above earthly concerns and vanity toward the eternal presence of God, using the hagiographic example of St. Hilarion taming a possessed camel to illustrate the powerlessness of worldly terrors.</summary>
<keywords>soul, heaven, Eternity, St. Jerome, St. Hilarion, demon, the World, temptations</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections on the nature of divine protection and the insignificance of earthly suffering compared to eternity.</meta>

-># CONSIDERATION<-

What do you fear? What do you love? The one who ascends the heavens <note>original: "Ascensor cæli"</note> is your helper: he embraces you with his everlasting arms. With those same arms, he so tightly binds your enemies—who either entice you or terrify you—that he can crush them all with a single nod, as if they were little flies. Therefore, does the pleasure of lust or gluttony solicit you? It is a small thing; despise it, and think instead of eternal pleasure. Do threats terrify you, do hardships press upon you, does contempt afflict you, does disease infest you, or does hidden poverty weary you? All these things are small, and the more sharply they rage against you, the shorter they shall last. Despise these things, look up to heaven, and think of eternity. \* *Nothing that happens to the just man shall make him sad.* <note>Proverbs 12:21</note> Even if the heavens should fall, the ruins would strike him unafraid. Is the just man then never sad? Does no tribulation ever assail him? Not at all; † *many are the tribulations of the just,* <note>Psalm 34:19 (Vulgate 33)</note> but they regard all such evils as mere trifles. They consider only that to be truly evil which is eternal, which separates one from God—as sin separates us, along with the wages of sin, which is eternal death. ‡ *While we look not at the things which are seen, but at the things which are not seen* (says the <term>Teacher of the Gentiles</term> <note>A traditional title for the Apostle Paul, referring to his mission to non-Jewish nations.</note>), for those things alone are truly great, whether they be good or evil.

But we, who so rarely contemplate the things we do not see, how much like children are we, whether as adults or as the elderly? We weep over ice that has slipped from our hands, which we could not have held onto anyway. We stand astonished by shadows, and we think ourselves wise in the midst of dreams. We waste away over things that will not only pass away shortly, but are already passing away even now. It will not pass—

---

\* *Proverbs, chapter 12.* † *Psalm 33.* ‡ *2 Corinthians, chapter 4.*

<meta>catchword: figu-</meta>

<summary>A meditative text encouraging the reader to disregard earthly pleasures and pains in favor of eternal spiritual truths, citing the Apostle Paul and the Psalms. It characterizes worldly attachments as fleeting "ice" or "shadows" compared to the permanence of God.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, divine protection, Stoicism, Apostle Paul, sin, suffering, spiritual perspective, vanity</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the fleeting nature of worldly life and the endurance of eternity, following the previous page's focus on spiritual vigilance.</meta>

# THE NINTH <note>original: "NONA" - likely referring to the ninth hour or ninth section of a devotional work</note>

The form of this world is passing away; it is already in transition. Just as all these good things which affect us here are unstable, so too all the evils by which we are oppressed are not long-lasting. Only those things which are not seen possess a perennial state; they know no end, admit no limit, and are strangers to change; they are stable, immovable, and eternal. I repeat a thousand times, and it must be repeated a thousand times more, the warning of Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father known for his Latin translation of the Bible</note>: *No labor should seem hard, and no time should seem long, by which the glory of Eternity is acquired.*

*Symphorianus* <note>A 2nd-century martyr of Autun</note>, a Christian youth, did not merely perish by the simple torture of whips. When he was already being led to his final punishment at *Autun* <note>original: "Augustoduni" - a city in Roman Gaul, modern-day France</note>, his mother met him on the road. But she did not tear her hair, nor bare her breast, nor wail like a common woman; rather, she acted as befitted a Christian heroine. "Son," she cried out, "my son, remember eternal life! Look up to heaven, and behold the One reigning there: life is not being snatched away from you, but is being changed for a better one." Animated by this motherly voice, the youth steadfastly offered his throat to the blade.

And we, O my fellow Christians, are already being led. We are all now going toward death, that supreme punishment, though with a slower step. All the inhabitants of heaven cry out to us: *Remember eternal life, look up to heaven: and behold the One reigning here.* Act as a Symphorianus here, whoever you are that calls yourself a Christian: do not withdraw yourself from labor and struggle, and even receive the sword bravely for your Christ, if necessary. Here, dare to be bold; here, show your spirit; and when you are tempted, when you grieve, are saddened, or are distressed; when you—

---

<margin>*Surius, August 22nd* <note>Refers to Laurentius Surius, a hagiographer who compiled lives of the saints</note></margin>

<summary>This page contrasts the fleeting nature of earthly suffering with the permanence of eternity, using the martyrdom of Saint Symphorianus and his mother's encouragement as a model for Christian endurance.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, St. Jerome, St. Symphorianus, Autun, martyrdom, Christian courage, Laurentius Surius</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page concludes the main body of the text and begins the index. It follows the final exhortations on enduring earthly suffering in light of eternity.</meta>

<note>original: "CONSIDERATIO"</note>
### CONSIDERATION.

You are despised, mocked, and slandered; when you are despoiled and tortured in various ways even beyond the trials of Job <note>The biblical figure Job is the archetype of the "patient sufferer" who lost his wealth, family, and health but remained faithful.</note>, imitate Symphorian <note>A 2nd-century French martyr who encouraged his mother as he was led to execution.</note> and a thousand other Christians, and with a steadfast heart, burst forth even a thousand times into this bold cry: "Whatever this is, it is a small thing, it is brief; farewell to all things, and greetings to you alone now, my Eternity."

->T H E  E N D<-
->*Of Eternity, there is none.*<-
